Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)

1. Executive Summary
Summary of Regulatory Action
Novo Nordisk originally submitted their Biologic License Application (BLA) for marketing
approval of concizumab for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of
bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII
deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX
inhibitors on August 24, 2022. Concizumab is an anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor
administered by injection. During the initial review cycle of the application, multiple deficiencies
were identified which precluded approval. This included concerns about inadequate monitoring
for missed doses, need for clear labeling instructions regarding missed doses, need for a
Medication Guide, issues regarding the interim concizumab assay proposed for product launch
and product quality review issues including facilities inspectional deficiencies. The Applicant
received a Complete Response Letter (CRL) on April 24, 2023. The reasons for the CRL are
summarized below in Section 2, Regulatory History, of this review.
On June 20, 2024, the Applicant submitted a response to the CRL, addressing the deficiencies
listed in the CRL. This resubmission review focuses only on the review of the deficiencies
identified in the CRL, updated safety information, labeling and postmarketing commitments.
This review is a joint review by Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology.
The Applicant has adequately addressed all deficiencies in the CRL. Each discipline recommends
approval, and the signatory authority concurs that the application should be approved. The
overall benefit/risk profile is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below.

Benefit-Risk Assessment

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
Benefit-Risk Dimensions
Dimension

Analysis of
Condition

Evidence and Uncertainties
• Hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by
mutations in the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or
absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in approximately 1
in 5000 live male births. Patients are typically diagnosed in infancy.
Patients are at increased risk for minor and serious bleeding including
life threatening spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease
manifestations include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and
muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis and excessive bleeding with procedures or
trauma. Bleeding can occur in vital organs (including the brain). Adults
commonly experience hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint
bleeds, which is associated with significant disease morbidity.
• Hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by
mutations in the FIX gene which results in a severe deficiency or
absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in approximately 1
in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor IX levels result in an
increased risk for minor and serious bleeding, including life threatening
spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are
the same as HA described in the text above.
• One of the most serious treatment complications of HA and HB is
inhibitor development. Approximately 30% of patients with HA and 515% of patients HB develop inhibitors to factor replacement. Because
bleeding is more difficult to prevent and treat, patients with inhibitors
often experience increased disease morbidity and mortality.

CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

Conclusions and Reasons
Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency)
with FVIII inhibitors (HAwI) and hemophilia B
(congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX
inhibitors (HBwI) are serious chronic diseases
with significant morbidity and mortality due to
bleeding. Patients with inhibitors have a worse
disease course.

10

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
Dimension

Current
Treatment
Options

Evidence and Uncertainties
• FDA approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or control
bleeding in patients with inhibitors. Activated prothrombin complex
concentrate (aPCC) is indicated to control and prevent bleeding
episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI. Recombinant activated
coagulation factor VII (rFVIIa), is indicated for the treatment of
bleeding episodes in patient with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these
products can be variable, and they carry a boxed warning for
thrombosis. Antibodies to FVIIa can develop in patients with HAwI
and HBwI and interfere with the effectiveness of these factor-based
therapies.
• Alloantibodies or inhibitors develop with exposure to exogenous
factor, and block the activity of factor VIII or IX. Inhibitors can make
it difficult to stop excessive bleeding as it leads to reduced efficacy
of factor replacement.
• Patients with HAwI and HBwI may undergo immune ITI to eradicate
the inhibitor, but this is only successful in 60-80% of cases in
patients with HAwI and 15-30% of patients with HBwI.
• Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor IXa and factor X-directed
antibody that was FDA approved in 2017 for the indication of
routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding
episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older
with HA or HAwI. Emicizumab is administered by weekly up to every
4 weeks by SC injection. Emicizumab has a boxed warning for
thrombotic microangiopathy and thromboembolism associated with
use with aPCC.

CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs

Conclusions and Reasons
There is a need for available, safe and effective
therapies. Patient with HA and HB can develop
inhibitors after exposure to exogenous factor.
For patients with HAwI and HBwI,
prophylactic treatment options are very
limited, in particular for patients with HBwI.

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
Dimension

Benefit

Evidence and Uncertainties
Pivotal, Phase 3 Study 4311
• Study 4311 was a multi-center, open-label, randomized-controlled
study in which 52 adult and adolescent patients (27 HAwI, and 25
HBwI), previously treated with on-demand therapy, were
randomized 1:2 to arm 1, no prophylaxis (on demand therapy)
(N=19) or arm 2, concizumab prophylaxis (N=33) for at least 24
weeks. Arms 3 and 4 included 81 additional patients (53 HAwI and
28 HBwI) treated with concizumab prophylaxis. Any bleeds
occurring in the clinical study were treated with the patient’s usual
bypassing product or factor. Superiority of concizumab PPX (arm 2)
over no PPX (arm 1) was confirmed for the primary endpoint of
number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes.
The annualized bleeding rate (ABR) was calculated as the number of
treated spontaneous or traumatic bleeds divided by the number of
days in the analysis set, multiplied by 365. The ABR was 1.7 with
95% CI (1.01, 2.87) for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and 11.8
with 95% CI (7.03, 19.86) for patients on no PPX (arm 1). The
estimated ABR ratio was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29)
corresponding to an 86% reduction in ABR for patients on
concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to no PPX (arm 1). Results were
statistically significant and the magnitude of reduction in bleeding
rate was clinically meaningful for a patient population at risk for
frequent bleeding that is difficult to treat. While there is a
theoretical concern that patients may alter their behavior due to
knowledge of their treatment assignment, the highly statistically
significant efficacy result provides reassurance of treatment effect.
• Key secondary endpoints of change in SF-36v2 bodily pain and change
in SF-36v2 physical functioning were not statistically significant.

CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

Conclusions and Reasons
Prophylactic treatment with daily concizumab
Is expected to reduce spontaneous and
traumatic bleeding episodes in patients with
HAwI and HBwI. This was evident by the
reduction in the number of bleeding events in
patients treated with concizumab prophylaxis
compared to on demand therapy.
Concizumab will fulfill an unmet need in that
there are no non-factor therapies currently
approved to reduce bleeding in patients with
HBwI.

Evidence and Uncertainties

Conclusions and Reasons

• In a descriptive analysis, 94.7% of patients in arm 1 (no PPX) had a
treated bleed or discontinued before week 24 compared to 48.5%
in arm 2 (concizumab PPX).
Supportive, Phase 2 Study 4310
• Study 4310 was a supportive, multicenter, open-label, randomizedcontrolled study in which 26 patients (16 HAwI patients, 10 HBwI)
patients were randomized 2:1 to concizumab PPX (n=17) and
eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) on demand (N=9) for at least 24 weeks. The
primary endpoint the number of bleeding episodes during at least
24 weeks from treatment onset. The ABR was significantly lower in
the concizumab PPX arm compared to the eptacog arm. The
estimated ABR during the entire treatment period was also lower in
the concizumab arm compared to the eptacog alfa arm: 5.4 (95% CI:
3.8, 7.6) and 20.6 (95% CI: 13.7, 30.8), respectively.
Overall Safety Findings
• The review of safety focused on Study 4311, which included 127 patients
with HAwI and HBwI exposed to concizumab, including 33 patients
randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to 19 patients randomized
to no PPX (arm 1) in the main treatment period. The safety analysis was
further supported by Study 4310.

and occurred in a higher proportion of patients in the concizumab arm
compared to no PPX was local administration reactions, which occurred in
18% of patients in the concizumab arm (arm 2) compared to 0% in the onCDER Clinical Review Template

Evidence and Uncertainties

Thrombosis
• In total, four patients across clinical trials experienced serious events of
thrombosis, including arterial and venous thrombosis, after receiving
concizumab, compared to no patients in the control group. Notable
characteristics were additional risk factors for thrombosis (i.e., smoking,
obesity and HTN), receiving factor product or a bypassing agent within 24
hours of the thrombotic event and elevated concizumab levels (>4000ng/mL).
• The Applicant had modified the concizumab dosing regimen during the
clinical development to target a concizumab level between 200-4000ng/mL,
to prevent bleeding and thrombosis. In addition, the Applicant included a
number of mitigation strategies, including using the lowest labeled dose of
factor or bypassing agent.
Risk of Bleeding with Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels.
• Across clinical trials, the majority of patients with subtherapeutic
concizumab levels (<200 ng/mL) had no bleeding or minor bleeding although,
6 (1.9%) patients had serious bleeding events and 2 (0.6%) patients died with
subtherapeutic levels.
• Large PK fluctuations were noted in clinical studies, this was in part due to
issues with non-compliance. PK analysis demonstrates missed doses of
concizumab can result in subtherapeutic levels within a few days, further,
once the patient restarts administration it may take several days to become
therapeutic.

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
HAwI and HBwI are rare and serious diseases, associated with significant morbidity and
mortality due to a risk of frequent and severe bleeding. Prophylactic factor-based
treatment is considered standard of care to prevent bleeding related complications.
However, antibodies to FVIIa can develop in patients and interfere with the effectiveness
of these factor-based therapies. There is an unmet medical need for prophylactic nonfactor therapy options for patients with HAwI and HBwI.
Concizumab is an anti-tissue factor pathway inhibitor which enhances FXa production during
the initiation phase of coagulation which leads to improved thrombin generation and clot
formation to achieve hemostasis.
The efficacy of concizumab was based on a single, adequate and well controlled trial, study
4311. Study 4311 compared ABR “on demand” treatment with a bypassing agent, eptacog alfa
or activated prothrombin complex concentrate, administered at the time of bleeding with
prophylaxis concizumab treatment. The efficacy results demonstrated a statistically significant
reduction in median ABR for the prophylaxis concizumab treatment group when compared with
the “on demand” treatment group. Confirmatory evidence included study results from study
4310, which also demonstrated a large reduction in ABR in the group on concizumab
prophylaxis and mechanistic support.
The safety of concizumab was assessed in the randomized controlled portion of study 4311,
along with the entire safety database in the concizumab hemophilia development plan
including 320 patients with HAwI, HBwI, HA and HB. Common adverse reactions included
injection site reactions and urticaria. Similar to other treatments for hemophilia, concizumab
carries an increased risk of thromboembolic events. The risk of thrombosis can be decreased
with adjusting the concizumab maintenance dose to the concizumab concentration target
range as described in the USPI and following blood management guidelines. We will continue to
assess the risk of thrombosis with postmarketing enhanced pharmacovigilance. Concizumab
also carries an increased risk of serious hypersensitivity reactions.
The benefit/risk profile of concizumab is now favorable, as the Applicant has adequately
addressed deficiencies in the CRL, including guidance on missed doses, emphasized the
importance of drug compliance in labeling, and resolved issues related to product quality and
facility inspections. At this time, there is no FDA-cleared assay to measure concizumab levels,
which are used to guide safe and effective dosing. While the Applicant completes development
of a companion diagnostic assay (see post-marketing commitment in Section 8) an interim
assay will be used to assess concizumab levels. The unapproved interim assay was reviewed by
the Agency and determined to be adequate for use for this purpose.
.

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)

2. Regulatory History
Novo Nordisk originally submitted their BLA for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital
factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency)
with FIX inhibitors on August 24, 2022. The Applicant received a CRL on April 24, 2023. The
reasons for the CRL are briefly summarized below, see finalized CRL in DARRTS for further
details.
Deficiencies identified in the CRL:
Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology: At the target exposure range of concizumab, targetmediated drug disposition (TMDD) plays a dominant role in concizumab clearance, leading to
rapid drug elimination and a relatively shorter half-life at the proposed doses of 0.15 - 0.25
mg/kg. As a result, concizumab concentrations can decline steeply to subtherapeutic levels with
missed doses and it may take several days of consecutive dosing to gradually return to target
concentrations of >200 ng/mL. Therefore, a plan for dosing after one or more missed doses is
required, including an additional concizumab pharmacokinetics (PK) monitoring and/or a
repeat loading dose. In addition, the importance of adherence to daily dosing and maintenance
of therapeutic concizumab levels should be stressed in a Medication Guide, labeling and
educational materials.
Device: Four weeks after initiating concizumab prophylaxis, a concizumab-mtci plasma
concentration will be measured by Concizumab Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay (ELISA)
for dose optimization purposes. Based on the ELISA assay results, the dose of concizumab may
be modified in order to maintain the target range. The Applicant’s interim assay to monitor
concizumab concentration was inadequate for a multitude of reasons outlined in the CRL.
Product Quality:
are present in the concizumab drug product. The
Applicant provided insufficient information and data provided to address the risk of
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
on safety and efficacy and the control strategy for
was inadequate to ensure product quality, safety, and efficacy. Other deficiencies
were identified including inadequate cell bank stability protocols, inadequate reference
material stability protocols, inadequate drug substance (DS) and drug product (DP) release and
stability specifications, and inadequate DS and DP post-approval annual stability protocols.
(b) (4)

Facility Inspection: Following inspection of
manufacturing facility listed in this application, FDA conveyed deficiencies to the
representative of the facility.

(b) (4)

Type A Meeting to Discuss Deficiencies in CRL:
On June 23, 2023, a Type A in-person meeting was held to discuss the deficiencies in the CRL

Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs

17

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
and potential path forward. Key discussion points from the meeting are listed below, see
finalized meeting minutes in DARRTS for further details.
- The Applicant proposed guidance for resumed dosing after missed concizumab doses.
The rationale of this plan was to ensure that patients who have missed concizumab daily
injection at the maintenance dose stage could reach steady-state concizumab exposure
levels as quickly as possible upon resumed dosing without exceeding steady-state levels.
Overall, the Agency commented that the proposal was reasonable, however emphasized
that the USPI and Medication Guide should further stress the importance of adherence
to daily dosing and the consequence of missed doses.
- The Applicant proposed to include an additional concizumab plasma concentration level
to be performed 8 weeks after initiation of maintenance dosing. The Agency

3. Deficiencies Identified in the CRL
Clinical and Clinical Pharmacology
To address the potential for significant fluctuations in concizumab concentration levels, which
can lead to subtherapeutic levels in the setting of missed doses and thus increasing the risk of
serious bleeding events, the Applicant proposed several mitigation strategies, including (1)
guidance on missed doses, (2) guidance on additional concizumab measurements, and (3)
additional education and guidance to emphasize the importance of adherence to the
prescribed dosing regimen.
Guidance on missed doses when a patient has forgotten or neglected to take their once-daily
maintenance dose
The Applicant has proposed the following plan to be included in the USPI for concizumab:
- 1 missed dose: Resume once-daily treatment at the maintenance dose level
- 2 to 6 missed doses: Resume treatment with a double dose followed by once-daily
treatment at the maintenance dose level
- 7 or more missed doses: Physician should be contacted, and a new loading dose should
be considered
The above proposal was based on population PK modelling. The final population PK model,
reviewed earlier by the Agency with original BLA submission, was used to simulate individual
concentration-time profiles for 10,000 subjects with different numbers of missed doses
followed by treatment being resumed with either a single dose, a double dose or a new loading
dose. The maintenance dose setting was implemented as a dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg or a
decrease to 0.15 mg/kg 6 weeks after first dose, based on Ctrough values 4 weeks after first
dose. In the simulations, exposure was at steady state at the time of the first missed dose and
the body-weight distribution was similar to trial 4311.
In the setting of 1 missed dose, resumption of treatment at the same maintenance dose level
on the following day with continued once-daily maintenance dose is shown to result in patients
quickly reaching and maintaining the steady state levels (Figure 1).

CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

19

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)

Figure 1: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a
single maintenance dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 1 missed dose.
In the setting of 2 to 6 missed doses, simulations show that doubling the maintenance dose
following missed dose(s) is able to bring concizumab exposures above 200 ng/mL reasonably
quickly and achieve steady state exposures with continued once-daily maintenance doses
(Figure 2). Use of a loading dose to resume treatment in the scenario of 2 to 6 missed doses is
not appropriate because the resulting exposures is expected to exceed the prior steady state
levels.

Figure 2: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a
double maintenance dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 2 (left) or 6 (right)
missed doses.
In the setting of 7 or more missed doses, a loading dose of 1 mg/kg followed by once-daily
maintenance doses is more appropriate as it results in concizumab exposures achieving steady
state levels as quickly as possible without exceeding the prior steady state levels (Figure 3).

Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)

Figure 3: Simulation of concizumab plasma concentration when treatment is resumed with a
loading dose followed by once-daily maintenance dose after 7 missed doses.
Guidance on Concizumab Measurements
Four weeks after initiation of concizumab, prescribers are advised to check concizumab-mtci
plasma concentration by Concizumab ELISA prior to administration of next scheduled dose for
dose optimization. Based on the results the concizumab dose may be adjusted. In order to
further monitor patient compliance, Novo Nordisk proposed to add the following guidance for
additional monitoring of concizumab plasma concentration to the USPI:
Based on the patient’s condition, additional measurements of concizumab-mtci plasma
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
concentration
be taken
so long as the patient has been on the same
maintenance dose for 8 weeks of treatment to ensure steady-state plasma concentration.
The timing of the additional measurement i.e., 8 weeks beyond the dose adjustment period is
reasonable, as it ensures achievement of new steady state levels in 90% of patients who
underwent dose-adjustment. The Agency clarified further in the USPI that additional
measurements of concizumab-mtci plasma concentration can be used to assess compliance.
Guidance and Education on Compliance
In order to stress the importance of adherence to daily dosing during the initial dosing period
for appropriate maintenance dose setting, Novo Nordisk further proposed including the
following guidance in the USPI and the corresponding instructions to be included in the
Medication Guide:
Adherence to daily dosing of Alhemo is important to maintain protection against bleeding. This
is especially important during the initial 4 weeks of treatment to ensure a correct maintenance
dose is established. Patients who miss a dose during the initial 4-week period should inform
their healthcare professional and resume once-daily dosing at the initial 0.2 mg/kg dose level.
Advise patients on the importance of adherence to daily dosing and to speak with their
healthcare provider before discontinuing treatment with Alhemo as patients who are not
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

21

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
adhering to daily dosing or stop treatment with Alhemo may no longer be protected against
bleeding.
The Medication Guide will replace the Patient Product Insert (PPI) and text will be added on the
carton with directions regarding dispensing the Medication Guide to each patient. The
Medication Guide will include the following statement:
It is important to follow the daily dosing schedule to stay protected against bleeding. Take
ALHEMO exactly as prescribed by your healthcare provider.
In addition, Novo Nordisk commits to developing educational material conveying similar
messages based on the final approved labeling text.
Summary
The Applicant has adequately addressed the clinical and clinical pharmacology concerns
regarding missed doses and strategies to monitor and promote patient compliance. The Agency
agrees with the additional guidance on missed doses to be included in labeling, the
recommendation for an additional concizumab level anytime beyond 8 weeks post-dose
adjustment and guidance along with education materials to stress the importance of adherence
to the prescribed dosing regimen.

Adequacy of the safety database:
In total, 320 patients with HAwI, HBwI, HA and HB were exposed to concizumab in clinical trials.
The safety update is adequate for review.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
In the original BLA submission, a clinical inspection summary was completed by Dr. Anthony
Orencia in the Office of Scientific Investigations, final signature date March 2, 2023, and no
major issues were identified. For the safety update, no issues identified regarding data
integrity and submission quality.
Categorization of Adverse Events
Adverse events were coded as Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred
terms (version 17.0 to 26.0). The Applicant used version 26.0 of MedDRA for the outputs based
on the pooled data. The majority of adverse events were analyzed using standardized MedDRA
queries. Hypersensitivity was analyzed using FDA Medical Query.

Safety Results
Deaths
In the original safety pool, there were 4 patient deaths including events of road traffic accident,
COVID-19, alcoholic coma and intracranial hemorrhage. The event of intracranial hemorrhage
occurred in an adolescent with subtherapeutic concizumab levels (see finalized Integrated
Review dated April 24, 2023, for further details).
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

30

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
One new death in a patient with HA (treated on study 4307) was reported in the Safety Update
(b) (6)
Report. Patient
, was a 54-year-old male, with HA treated with concizumab PPX who was
reported to have died due to intra-abdominal hemorrhage. In Trial 4307, on study day 276, the
patient was found dead at home. The exact event onset date remains unknown. The cause of
death based on autopsy was reported bleeding to death by diffuse intra-abdominal bleed of 1.7
L of blood without a clearly localizable bleeding source. The event was judged by the
investigator as possibly related to concizumab, with alternative causality reported as
“underlying disease severe hemophilia A”.
This patient was treated with concizumab PPX at a dose of 0.15 mg/kg in trial 4255 for
approximately 2 years and 8 months. The patient was then enrolled in Trial 4307 and was
treated with concizumab PPX (0.25 mg/kg) for approximately 8 months prior to the onset of the
fatal event. There were no bleeding events reported for the patient in either trial up until the
fatal event. Of note, the patient had an ongoing concomitant illness of hypertension treated
with bisoprolol. Blood pressure measurements performed at the last 5 visits before event
onset were reported to have from around 120-140/80 mmHg to around 155-170 /100110mmHg.
An Information Request was sent to the Applicant to provide additional information regarding
concizumab level(s) and compliance. The Applicant reported that all concizumab plasma
concentration measurements performed for the patient after the maintenance dose setting
were below 200 ng/mL. The concizumab plasma concentration gradually increased from
approximately 70 ng/mL to fluctuating between approximately 90 and 175 ng/mL from week 8
up until the last measurement at week 32. There was seemingly only a minor effect of the
maintenance dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg.
Despite the low plasma concizumab levels, free TFPI in plasma was well-suppressed at all visits
where measured. The patient did not test positive for anti-concizumab antibodies (ADAs) at
any visit. No protocol deviations related to missed doses or compliance issues were registered
for the patient.
Reviewer comment: The cause for this patient’s intra-abdominal bleeding event is not clear.
Although concizumab levels were consistently below the recommended levels of 200ng/mL the
patient did not have other bleeding events. Notably, there was no evidence of antidrug
antibodies. As described in the original BLA review, with low concentration of concizumab
(<200ng/mL) patients are at risk for serious and life-threatening bleeding events. In fact, there
was one other case of a fatal bleed described in the original review; however, this
patient/caregiver may have had compliance issues. Per the Applicant the other patient who
died due to intra-abdominal hemorrhage was compliant, but uncertainty remains.
To minimize risk of serious and life-threatening bleeding with subtherapeutic concizumab levels,
the label should include instructions to advise prescribers/patients to consider alternative
CDER Clinical Review Template
Version date: March 8, 2019 for all NDAs and BLAs
Reference ID: 5500478

31

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
therapies if the therapeutic range for concizumab cannot be achieved. Additional concizumab
level monitoring to assess for subtherapeutic levels needs to be clearly stated in the label.

In total, 14 (4.4%) patients had serious bleeding events. This included events of pharyngeal
hemorrhage, gastrointestinal hemorrhage, intra-abdominal hemorrhage, renal hematoma, post
procedural hematoma, traumatic hematoma, retroperitoneal hemorrhage/hematoma, muscle
hematoma/hemorrhage, hemorrhagic shock, puncture site hemorrhage, subdural hemorrhage,
hemorrhage intracranial, melaena, hematemesis, and hemarthrosis. The majority of serious
bleeding events were related to GI bleeding. Gastrointestinal bleeding disorders includes the
pooled terms intra-abdominal hemorrhage, gastrointestinal hemorrhage, hematemesis,
melena, retroperitoneal hematoma, and retroperitoneal hemorrhage, were reported in 7/320
(2.2%) of patients.
Reviewer comment: In the Information Request Response dated December 2, 2024, the
Applicant responded to the query if there were subjects who may have had a subtherapeutic
concizumab level and also had a serious bleeding adverse event reported after the 56-week cut
off. The Applicant responded that a total of 14 patients reported a total of 19 bleed-related SAEs
in trials 4311 (17 events in 12 patients) and 4307 (2 events in 2 patients) after the 56-week cutoffs i.e., based on on-going trial data. Among the 14 patients who reported bleeding SAEs after
the 56-week cut-offs in trials 4311 and 4307 there are 6 patients whose plasma concizumab
concentration was reported to be low (i.e., below 200 ng/mL) around the time of onset of the
event. These patients had maintenance concizumab doses ranging from 0.20mg/kg to
0.25mg/kg. It is not clear why patients on 0.20mg/kg did not have a dose increase. Three of the

Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
In the updated total safety pool, 60 (18.8%) patients who temporarily interrupted treatment or
permanently discontinued therapy compared to 21 (16.9% patients originally reported in study
4311 and 4310). There were 10 (3.1%) patients who permanently discontinued study drug. AEs
leading to study drug discontinuation were all reported in 1 patient each, including, intraabdominal hemorrhage, procedural complication, acute myocardial infarction, cardiac failure,
deep vein thrombosis, injection site pain, cardiomyopathy, renal infarct, hypersensitivity and
COVID-19 infection. Patients with permanent study drug discontinuation ranged in age from 1367 years. The duration of therapy prior to discontinuation ranged from 10-205 days. Of note,
protocols required temporary treatment discontinuation for COVID-19 infection.

Source: Study Update Report

Clinical reviewer comment: Patient
had a serious event of intra-abdominal bleeding. The
patient was a 43-year-old male with HB who was treated with concizumab on study 4307. On
day 2 of therapy the patient was admitted to hospital due to right upper quadrant abdominal
pain. The patient underwent a CT scan of the abdomen and chest which showed blood in the
area of the spleen and liver, and a lung mass, respectively. The lung mass was biopsied which
was subsequently shown to have lymphocytic changes but no malignancy. The patient’s
concizumab therapy was discontinued at the time of the event. Although abdominal bleeding is
not a common site for bleeding in patients with hemophilia it has been reported. This serious
bleeding event is likely due to the underlying disease as the patient had just initiated
concizumab therapy.
(b) (6)

The majority of events leading to treatment discontinuation are included in labeling with
mitigation strategies (i.e., bleeding due to subtherapeutic levels, thrombosis, and
hypersensitivity reaction).

Clinical Review
BLA 761315
Alhemo (concizumab)
Adverse Events of Special Interest
1. Thromboembolic events
In the Safety Update Report, 4 (1.3%) patients experienced SAEs of thromboembolic
events, 3 out the 4 patients were reported in the original review. The one new case of
serious thromboembolic event (cerebral infarction) occurred in a 63 year-old male with
HA. The patient had a medical history of hypertension since 2013 and was treated with
2 anti-hypertensive medications. The patient was previously enrolled in the phase 2 trial
4255 and had more than 5 years of concizumab exposure up until onset of the SAE,
including exposure before and after the treatment pause in trial 4307. On the day of
event onset, the patient experienced visual disturbance, facial weakness, ataxia and
slurred speech. The patient was diagnosed with an ischemic stroke. Different scans, MRI
brain scans, were performed, and a small recent infarction was detected in 2 different
areas (left hemipons and corona radiata). According to the investigator, the MRI images
of the patient’s brain as well as the CT scans taken after event onset were shared with a
specialist (neuro-radiologist) to get a second opinion. According to the specialist, the
specific lesions (perforating arteries) found on the scans are most seen in patients with a
medical history of hypertension. The patient discontinued concizumab treatment
permanently.
Clinical reviewer comment: Thrombosis is a serious adverse reaction associated with
concizumab, likely due to exaggerated pharmacologic effect and will be included in the
Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. While providing specific guidance for use
of the lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab
PPX and a concizumab dosing regimen which targets concizumab levels <4000ng/mL,
this risk remains, as evident by the event of cerebral infarction. Risk factors for
thromboembolism further increase the risk of thrombosis and may include the use of
high and/or frequent doses of breakthrough bleed treatments (factor products or
bypassing agents) or conditions in which tissue factor is overexpressed (e.g.,
atherosclerotic disease, crush injury, cancer, disseminated intravascular coagulation,
thrombotic microangiopathy, or septicemia). Thromboembolic events occurred in
patients with multiple risk factors for thromboembolism, including the use of high doses
or prolonged treatment with factor product or bypassing agent (2 of 4 events). Enhanced
pharmacovigilance is recommended to further monitor the risk of thrombosis.
2. Hypersensitivity reactions
In the Safety Update Report, the search for FDA Medical Query (FMQ) for
hypersensitivity reactions was reported in in 30 (9.4%) patients totaling 55 events
(0.1 events per PYE). The most common terms reported in ≥ 4 (approximately ≥ 1%)
patients were ‘hypersensitivity’, ‘injection site reaction’, and ‘pruritis’. The reviewer’s
table below shows the proportion of patients with hypersensitivity reactions reported in

Clinical reviewer comment: The plasma concentration of concizumab is positively
correlated with fibrin D-dimer and prothrombin fragments 1.2 indicating a hemostatic
effect of concizumab. For patients taking concizumab, these coagulation biomarkers
may not be reliable predictive markers for clinical decision-making with suspicion of

5. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
An advisory committee (AC) meeting was not convened to discuss this application. No issues
were identified that would have benefitted from a public discussion with external experts.

6. Labeling Recommendations
Prescription Drug Labeling
Alhemo will be approved with Prescribing Information, Medication Guide, and 3 Instructions for
Use (one per pen strength).
CDER chose to approve concizumab without its companion diagnostic given the unmet need for
patients with hemophilia with inhibitors. A postmarketing commitment is recommended for the
development of the companion diagnostic. The label will state there are no FDA authorized
tests. This is the same approach we have consistently used when CDER chooses to approve a
drug without its companion diagnostic. The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH)
agreed with CDER’s approach and advised on labeling related to the use of an unauthorized
concizumab-ELISA.

7. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
Based on the available data, a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy is not necessary to ensure
the benefits of concizumab in patients with HAwI and HBwI outweigh the risks.

8. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
PMC 4761-1: Conduct adequate analytical and clinical validation testing to establish and
support labeling of an FDA cleared or approved in vitro diagnostic device to accurately and
reliably detect concizumab concentration in human 3.2% citrated plasma that is safe and
effective for concizumab dose adjustment decisions for Hemophilia A and B patients (with and
without inhibitors) after 4 weeks from the initiation of treatment with concizumab. The results
of the validation studies are intended to inform product labeling.

NNC0172-2021
Novo Nordisk
(b) (4)
injection
The recommended dosing regimen comprise a loading dose
of 1 mg/kg followed by an initial once daily dosing of
0.20 mg/kg. Based on the concizumab exposure level
measured after 4 weeks of treatment, the maintenance dose
will be set to 0.15, 0.20 or 0.25 mg/kg once daily.
For routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of
bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with:
• hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII
inhibitors
• hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with FIX
inhibitors

BLA 761315

Concizumab

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On August 24, 2022, Novo Nordisk submitted BLA 761315 for concizumab for the following
indications: ALHEMO® is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI)-directed antibody indicated
for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and
pediatric patients with hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII inhibitors
(HAwI) and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX deficiency) with coagulation factor IX (FIX)
inhibitors (HBwI). This application was reviewed by a multidisciplinary team. This application
was given priority review because the proposed indication would have addressed an unmet
medical need of a prophylaxis treatment for patients with HBwI.
In 2018 and 2019, concizumab received orphan designation for hemophilia A (HA) and
hemophilia B (HB), and in 2019 received Breakthrough Therapy designation for HBwI.
In clinical studies, concizumab prophylaxis use demonstrated an improvement in the annualized
bleeding rate (ABR) compared to on demand treatment using a blood product approved for the
on-demand treatment of bleeding events for patients with HA and HB. ABR endpoint has been
used for product approval for decades for products to treat bleeding events due to hemophilia.
The application has multiple deficiencies including concern about inadequate monitoring for
missed doses, need for clear labeling instructions regarding missed doses, need for a Medication
Guide, the interim assay proposed for product launch and product quality review issues including
facilities inspectional deficiencies which preclude approval.

2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework

Analysis of
condition

Conclusions and Reasons

HA is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in
the FVIII gene which results in a severe deficiency or
absence of the clotting protein factor VIII. HA occurs in
approximately 1 in 5000 live male births. Patients are
typically diagnosed in infancy. Patients are at increased risk
for minor and serious bleeding including life threatening
spontaneous or traumatic bleeding. Disease manifistations
include mucosal bleeding, easy bruising, soft tissue and
muscle bleeds, hemarthrosis and excessive bleeding with
procedures or trauma. Bleeding can occur in vital organs
(including the brain). Adults commonly expierence
hemophilic arthropathy due to recurrent joint bleeds, which
is associated with signficant disease morbidity.
HB is an X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in
the FIX gene which results in a severe deficiency or
absence of the clotting protein factor IX. HB occurs in
approximately 1 in 30,000 live male births. Deficient factor
IX levels results in an increased risk for minor and serious
bleeding, including life threatening spontaneous or traumatic
bleeding. Disease manifestations of HB are the same as HA
describedin the text above.
One of the most serious complications of HA and HB is
inhibitor development. Approximately 30% of patients with
HA and 5-15% of patients HB develop inhibitors. Because
bleeding is more diffiucult to prevent and treat, patients with
inhibitors often experience increased disease morbidity and
mortality.

Hemophilia A (congenital factor VIII deficiency) with FVIII
inhibitors (HAwI) and hemophilia B (congenital factor IX
deficiency) with FIX inhibitors (HBwI) are serious chronic
diseases with significant morbidity and mortality due to
bleeding. Patients with inhibitors have a worse disease course.

Evidence and Uncertainties

Current
treatment
options

•

FDA approved bypassing agents are used to prevent or
control bleeding in patients with inhibitors. Activated
prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) is indicated to
control and prevent bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI
and HBwI. Recombinant activated coagulation factor VII
(rFVIIa), is indicated for the treatment of bleeding episodes
in patient with HAwI and HBwI. Efficacy of these products
can be variable, and they carry a boxed warning for
thrombosis.
Alloantibodies or inhibitors develop with exposure to
exogenous factor, and block the activity of factor VIII or IX.
Inhibitors can make it difficult to stop excessive bleeding as
it leads to reduced efficacy of factor replacement.
Patients with HAwI and HBwI may undergo immune ITI to
erradicate the inhibitor, but this is only sucessful in 60-80%
of cases in patinets with HAwI and 15-30% of patinets with
HBwI.
Emicizumab (HEMLIBRA) is a bispecific factor IXa and
factor X-directed antibody that was FDA approved in 2017
for the indication of routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce
the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric
patients ages newborn and older with HA or HAwI.
Emicizumab is administred by weekly up to every 4 weeks
by SC injection. Emicizumab has a boxed warning for
thrombotic microangiopathy and thromoemoblism
associated with use with aPCC.

There is a need for available, safe and effective therapies.
Patient with HA and HB can develop inhibitors after exposure
to exogenous factor. For patients with HAwI and HBwI,
prophylactic treatment options are very limited, in particular for
patients with HBwI.

Pivotal, Phase 3 Study 4311
• Study 4311 was a multi-center, open-label, randomizedcontrolled study in which 52 adult and adolescent patients
(27 HAwI, and 25 HBwI), previously treated with on-demand
therapy, were randomized 1:2 to arm 1, no prophylaxis (on
demand therapy) (N=19) or arm 2, concizumab prophylaxis
(N=33) for at least 24 weeks. Arms 3 and 4 included 81
additional patients (53 HAwI and 28 HBwI) treated with
concizumab prophylaxis. Any bleeds occuring in the clinical
study were treated with the patient’s usual bypassing

The benefit of concizumab was clearly demonstrated in Study
4311, in which prophylatic treatment with daily concizumab
reduced spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes in
patients with HAwI and HBwI. This was evident by the
reduction in the number of bleeding events in patients treated
with concizumab prophylaxis compared to on demand therapy.
While there is a concern that patients may alter their behavior
due to knowledge of their treatment assignment, the highly
statistically significant efficacy result provides reassurance of
treatment effect.

Evidence and Uncertainties
product or factor. Superiority of concizumab PPX (arm 2)
over no PPX (arm 1) was confirmed for the primary endpoint
of number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding
episodes. The annualized bleeding rate (ABR) was
calculated as the number of treated spontaneous or
traumatic bleeds divided by the number of days in the
analysis set, multiplied by 365. The ABR was 1.7 with 95%
CI (1.01, 2.87) for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and
11.8 with 95% CI (7.03, 19.86) for patients on no PPX (arm
1). The estimated ABR ratio was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07,
0.29), corresponding to an 86% reduction in ABR for
patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to no PPX
(arm 1). Results were statistically significant and the
magnitude of reduction in bleeding rate was clinically
meaningful for a patient population at risk for frequent
bleeding that is difficult to treat.
• Key secondary endpoints of change in SF-36v2 bodily pain
and change in SF-36v2 physical functioning were not
statistically significant.
• In a descriptive analysis, 94.7% of patients in arm 1 (no
PPX) had a treated bleed or discontinued before week 24
compared to 48.5% in arm 2 (concizumab PPX). )
Supportive, Phase 2 Study 4310
• Study 4310 was a supportive, multicenter, open-label,
randomized-controlled study in which 26 patients (16 HAwI
patients,10 HBwI) patients were randomized 2:1 to
concizumab PPX (n=17) and eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) on
demand (N=9) for at least 24 weeks. The primary endpoint
was the number of bleeding episodes during at least 24
weeks from treatment onset. The ABR was significantly
lower in the concizumab PPX arm compared to the eptacog
arm. The estimated ABR during the entire treatment period
was also lower in the concizumab arm compared to the
eptacog alfa arm: 5.4 (95% CI: 3.8, 7.6) and 20.6 (95% CI
13.7, 30.8), respectively.

Conclusions and Reasons
Substantial evidence of effectiveness of concizumab for
prophylaxis in patients with HAwI and HBwI was based on one
adequate and well controlled study, Study 4311, and
confirmatory evidence. Confirmatory evidence was provided
from Study 4310 based on a large reduction in ABR in the
concizumab arm compared to the eptacog alfa arm in patients
with HAwI and HBwI, along with mechanistic support of
reduction in TFPI with corresponding increased thrombin
generation.

Concizumab

Risk and risk
management

Overall Safety Findings
• The review of safety focused on Study 4311, which
included 127 patients with HAwI and HBwI exposed to
concizumab, including 33 patients randomized to
concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to 19 patients
randomized to no PPX (arm 1) in the main treatment
period. The safety analysis was further supported by
Study 4310.
• TEAEs were reported in 60% of patients in concizumab
PPX arm 2 compared to 42% patients in the on-demand
arm 1 in the randomized main treatment portion of study
4311. The most common TEAE (>10%) in the main
treatment period and occurred in a higher proportion of
patients in the concizumab arm compared to no PPX
was local administration reactions, which occurred in
18% of patients in the concizumab arm (arm 2)
compared to 0% in the on-demand therapy arm (arm 1).
The most common ARs (>10%) at any timepoint (main
and extension parts) after exposure to concizumab were
local administration reaction (23.7%), nasopharyngitis
(14.9%), and arthralgia (11.4%). Additional notable
safety findings included one patient (<1%) with an AR of
serious hypersensitivity reaction, prothrombin fragment
1.2 increased (6.1%), and fibrin d-dimer increased
(5.3%).
Thrombosis
• In total, three patients experienced non-fatal events of
thrombosis, including arterial and venous thrombosis,
after receiving concizumab, compared to no patients in
the control group. Notable characteristics were
additional risk factors for thrombosis (i.e., smoking,
obesity and HTN), receiving factor product or a
bypassing agent within 24 hours of the thrombotic event
and elevated concizumab levels (>4000ng/mL).
• The Applicant had modified the concizumab dosing
regimen during the clinical development to target a
concizumab level between 200-4000ng/mL, to prevent
bleeding and thrombosis. In addition, the Applicant

Several risks have been identified that should be included in
the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI if
concizumab is approved, this includes risk of thrombosis,
hypersensitivity reactions, and increased laboratory values of
fibrin D-dimer and prothrombin fragment 1+2. Local injection
site reaction was the most common AR.
Patients with prolonged subtherapeutic levels (<200ng/mL) of
concizumab are at increased risk of bleeding, which can be
serious and life-threatening. Compliance to the daily SC
dosing regimen of concizumab is critical to prevent prolonged
subtherapeutic levels. Therefore, the importance of adherence
to daily dosing should be stressed in labeling and a Medication
Guide. In addition, a plan for missed concizumab doses should
be described in labeling.
Concizumab levels >200ng/mL is key to the therapeutic effect
of concizumab and levels <4000ng/mL is critical to decrease
the risk of thrombosis, therefore it is necessary to to have an
accurate and reliable concizumab ELISA in order to dose
adjust concizumab to a safe and efficacious concentration.
CDRH has identified multiple issues with the concizumab
ELISA and has determined it is inadequate for postmarket use.

Risk of Bleeding with Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels.

Concizumab

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
BLA 761315, ALHEMO® (concizumab), provided substantial evidence of effectiveness based on
data from clinical studies. However, the application will receive a Complete Response letter
because of the inspection, multiple product quality deficiencies, an inadequate interim assay to
monitor concizumab concentration after dose adjustment and an inadequate plan for monitoring
for missed doses that preclude approval.
The review teams acknowledged that the hemophilias are serious and life-threatening diseases
with few to no treatment options depending upon the subtype. If left untreated, hemophilia will
result in significant morbidity and early mortality from uncontrolled bleeding arising
spontaneously, following injury, or related to surgery. Thus, affected patients start on a life-long
treatment program of blood products or recombinant factor concentrates designed to “bypass”
the absent clotting factor, or emicizumab to prevent severe bleeding for patients with hemophilia
A with and without inhibitors. Some patients treated with blood products and recombinant factor
concentrates will develop antibody inhibitors which render the treatment ineffective, leaving
these patients with few to no options depending upon the subtype.
Concizumab focuses on providing a prophylaxis treatment for those with congenital hemophilia
A or B with inhibitors. For the subgroup of patients with congenital hemophilia B with inhibitors
this product has the potential to address an unmet medical need because of a lack of available
therapies available for prophylaxis. The nonclinical, clinical, statistical and clinical
pharmacology teams support the approval based on the nonclinical and clinical data packages
and did not identify any deficiencies. No important differences of opinion were voiced during the
review process.
The clinical data package consisted of several clinical studies that demonstrated an effect on
ABR, an endpoint that has been used for the approval of other monoclonal antibodies or blood
products for the treatment of hemophilia. The main study was 4311, an international,
multicenter, open-label, randomized controlled study. Study 4311 was designed to compare “on
demand” treatment with a bypassing agent, eptacog alfa or activated prothrombin complex
concentrate, administered at the time of bleeding with prophylaxis concizumab treatment. The
efficacy results demonstrated a markedly lower reduction in median annualized bleeding rates
for the prophylaxis concizumab treatment when compared with the “on demand” eptacog alfa or
activated prothrombin complex concentrate treatment. Median ABR for the concizumab
combined arms was 1.7 [1.01, 2.87] bleeds compared with 11.8 [7.03, 19.86] bleeds for the
eptacog alfa treatment “on demand” arm. During the initial part of the study, an increase in
thrombotic events occurred in the concizumab treatment arm which resulted in a study pause and
clinical hold because of concerns of excessive TFPI inhibition. Thus, Novo Nordisk developed
an algorithm based on concizumab pharmacokinetics (PK) levels to adjust concizumab dose if
needed. The study resumed and no further thrombotic events were observed. The major Study
4311 concizumab safety findings were bleeding, thrombosis, hypersensitivity reactions, and
injection site reactions.
The clinical pharmacology review concurred with the Applicant’s assessment of 200 ng/mL as
the lower end of the target concentration for concizumab. Levels lower than 200 ng/mL are
associated with increased risk for bleeding. During the review, the team noted that for some
9
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

patients there was significant intrasubject PK fluctuation, which the Applicant attributed to target
mediated drug disposition (TMDD). The clinical pharmacology review team considered other
scenarios potentially accounting for the observed results including antidrug antibodies, problems
with the PK assay, and membrane bound TFPI and treatment compliance issues. The issue of
missed dosing (compliance) is a significant concern because there would be a potential for
bleeding risk for the few patients whose exposure falls below 200 ng/mL. Because spontaneous
bleeding events and sites cannot be predicted, understanding how patients and their healthcare
providers should handle missed doses is an important key to the safe and effective use of
concizumab. The importance of adherence to daily dosing and maintenance of adequate levels
should be stressed in any labeling, Medication Guide and educational material.
The nonclinical review did not identify any unexpected risks with concizumab. The nonclinical
toxicology studies’ results were consistent with the expected mechanism of action of TFPI
inhibition. Toxicities observed reflected the expected consequence of higher-than-expected
exposures than those anticipated at the proposed therapeutic dose.
(b) (4)

ovo Nordisk proposed an interim assay and process which would be available for a
concizumab launch. CDRH’s review identified deficiencies for the interim assay and plan which
include assay accuracy, precision, sample handling, and methodology.
Besides the issues with the interim assay, the Agency’s product reviewers identified chemistry,
manufacturing, and controls issues: the failed facilities inspection and multiple deficiencies
including inadequate cell bank stability protocols, inadequate reference material stability
protocols, inadequate DS and DP release and stability specifications, and inadequate DS and DP
post-approval annual stability protocols.

BLA 761315

Concizumab

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Product Introduction
Concizumab (proposed proprietary name, ALHEMO®), a new molecular entity (NME), is a
monoclonal antibody antagonist of endogenous tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI). It is
directed against TFPI which allows increased downstream Fxa production by the coagulation
factor VIIa (FVIIa)/tissue factor (TF) complex and further downstream generation of thrombin in
the coagulation cascade. Concizumab acts independently from FVIII and coagulation factor IX
(FIX).
The Applicant proposes the following indication: for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients with hemophilia A with inhibitors
(HAwI) and hemophilia B with inhibitors (HBwI).
The proposed dosing regimen:
•

Day 1: Loading dose of 1 mg/kg

•

Day 2: Once-daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg until individualization of maintenance dose (see
below)

•

4 weeks after initiation of treatment: For dose optimization measure concizumab plasma
concentration once by concizumab enzyme-linked immunoassay (ELISA) prior to
administration of next scheduled dose

•

After concizumab plasma concentration result is available but recommended no later than 8
weeks after initiation of treatment: Individualize maintenance dose of concizumab once
based on the following concizumab plasma concentrations:
– <200 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.25 mg/kg
– 200 to 4000 ng/mL: continue once-daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg
– >4000 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.15 mg/kg

Disease Background
Patients with congenital hemophilia A or B lack specific clotting factors (factor VIII or IX,
respectively) and thus have spontaneous bleeding events which can affect critical organs and be
fatal. Patients with hemophilia A and B are typically treated with blood/recombinant factor
concentrates which supply the necessary missing clotting factors. Some patients with hemophilia
will develop antibodies (also known as inhibitors) to the factor concentrates rendering the factor
concentrates ineffective to prevent bleeding events. These patients who have hemophilia and
inhibitors are then treated with a bypassing strategy designed to overcome the missing factor and
its inhibitor.

11
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

HAwI
Congenital hemophilia A (HA) is an X-linked bleeding disorder characterized by the deficiency
or absence of factor VIII (FVIII). Hemophilia A occurs in 1 in 5000 live male births, without
ethnic predominance, which results in an incidence in the US of about 20,000 people and
400,000 people worldwide (Soucie et al. 1998). The absence of functional FVIII interrupts the
coagulation cascade and the ability to form a stable clot in response to trauma. The resulting
bleeding complications can be severe and life threatening and can lead to long-term morbidity
from joint damage due to recurrent bleeds. The standard of care in patients with HA is treatment
with recombinant or plasma-derived FVIII concentrates. Treatment can either be in response to a
bleeding event (episodic or “on demand” treatment) or scheduled treatment to prevent bleeds
(prophylactic treatment). In resource-rich countries, prophylactic treatment initiated prior to any
joint bleeds is preferred. In 30-35% of patients with hemophilia A, a neutralizing antibody,
known as a FVIII inhibitor, forms which renders exogenous and endogenous FVIII
nonfunctional. Patients with FVIII inhibitors are more difficult to treat in response to bleeding
events, leading to a higher rate of progressive joint disease from frequent joint bleeds and an
increased risk of death from uncontrolled bleeding.
HBwI
Congenital hemophilia B (HB) is an X-linked bleeding disorder caused by the deficiency of FIX,
a vitamin K-dependent plasma protease that participates in the intrinsic blood coagulation
pathway and whose function is to cleave and activate FX within the coagulation cascade. The
prevalence of the disease is estimated to be 1:30,000 males (Santoro et al. 2018). Bleeding
tendency in HB is in accordance with the severity of factor deficiency. Patients with the severe
form (FIX <1%), who account approximately for 30 to 45% of persons with HB, usually suffer
from recurrent joint, soft-tissue, and muscle bleedings. FIX concentrates are administered in
order to reduce bleeding complications (Santoro et al. 2018). The reported rate of FIX inhibitors
is 1 to 15% of all patients with HB (Male et al. 2021).
Current Therapy Options
Current therapeutic options for patients with HAwI and HBwI are summarized below (Table 3).
HAwI
Patients with inhibitors cannot be treated with FVIII replacement as the inhibitor would clear the
exogenous FVIII and likely increase the inhibitor titer. To clear the FVIII inhibitor, patients may
undergo immune tolerance induction (ITI). ITI involves frequent administrations of high-dose
FVIII to eliminate the antibody over time and allows patients to resume their prophylactic
treatment with FVIII. While this is successful in some patients, there is a failure rate of 20-40%,
and those patients who have a resurgence of their antibody after ITI tend to be even more
refractory to treatment. ITI also presents a heavy burden to patients both in terms of effort and
cost from daily high dose factor VIII infusions. The available treatments in patients with FVIII
inhibitors are episodic treatment with a bypassing agent which bypasses FVIII in the coagulation
cascade, often during ITI. Unfortunately, the efficacies of these agents are unstable compared to
FVIII infusions for patients without inhibitors. Table 3 below summarizes the available therapies
12
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

for the treatment of HAwI. NovoSeven® and FEIBA® are available agents have productspecific characteristics that limit their usability. NovoSeven is a recombinant activated factor VII
(rFVIIa) product which has a short half-life and requires intravenous (IV) treatment every two to
three hours. FEIBA, or Factor Eight Inhibitor Bypassing Activity, is a plasma-derived activated
prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC) which is predominately composed of prothrombin and
Factor X, but also contains lesser amounts of Factors VII and IX. This product requires a long
infusion time which can limit its use. It also contains small amounts of FVIII, resulting in the
risk of augmenting inhibitor formation in patients not currently undergoing ITI. Patients with
FVIII inhibitors can also receive prophylactic treatment with a bypassing agent. FEIBA was
approved in 2013 for this indication. The primary efficacy outcome for the registration study was
annualized bleeding rate (ABR), which is the rate of bleeds normalized to one year. Patients on
FEIBA prophylaxis had a median ABR of 8 compared to 29 for patients receiving episodic
treatment with FEIBA. Prophylactic treatment is costly and burdensome given the need for
every-other-day IV infusions of FEIBA. NovoSeven is not approved for prophylaxis with the
exception of prevention of bleeding during surgical or invasive procedures, and therefore the
dosing regimens used are not standardized and with variable rates of efficacy.
Emicizumab is a humanized monoclonal modified immunoglobulin G4 (IgG4) bispecific factor
Ixa-and factor X-directed antibody, which was approved for marketing on November 16, 2017,
under BLA 761083. Emicizumab is indicated for routine prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the
frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and pediatric patients ages newborn and older with HA
with or without factor VIII inhibitors (i.e., HA and HAwI). The recommended loading dose of
emicizumab is 3 mg/kg administered by SC injection once weekly for the first 4 weeks, followed
by a maintenance dose of 1.5 mg/kg once every week, or 3 mg/kg once every two weeks, or
6 mg/kg once every four weeks. The selection of a maintenance emicizumab dose is based on
healthcare provider preference with consideration of regimens that may increase patient
adherence. The emicizumab product label contains a boxed warning that states cases of
thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events were reported when on average a cumulative
amount of >100 U/kg/24 hours of aPCC was administered for 24 hours or more to patients
receiving emicizumab prophylaxis. The boxed warning contains the recommendation to monitor
for the development of thrombotic microangiopathy and thrombotic events if aPCC is
administered, and to discontinue aPCC and suspend dosing of emicizumab if symptoms occur.
The most common adverse reactions (incidence ≥10% of patients) reported in clinical studies are
injection site reactions, headache, and arthralgia.
HBwI
Treatment of hemorrhagic episodes in patients with HBwI must be individualized. The choice of
therapy depends on the type of bleeding episode, the inhibitor titer, and the history and severity
of previous infusion reactions. Bypassing agents (activated prothrombin complex concentrate
[aPCC]; activated recombinant FVII [rFVIIa]) are therapeutic options for treatment or prevention
of bleeding episodes in high-responding inhibitor patients. Bypassing agents have been shown to
be effective in acute bleeding control in ∼80% of patients with HBwI (Santoro et al. 2018). The
presence of FIX in aPCC, in patients with previous inhibitor recognition, generally requires
administration of rFVIIa. In cases of life-threatening bleeding episodes and failure of treatment
with bypassing agents, plasmapheresis or immunoadsorption with staphylococcal protein A may

be considered to remove anti-FIX antibodies, and to transiently restore replacement therapy with
FIX (Santoro et al. 2018). Because of the lack of therapeutic options for a large subpopulation of
patients with HBwI, concizumab would address an unmet medical need.

4. Patient Experience Data
In the Phase 3 study, Study 4311, patient-reported outcomes (PROs) were assessed. Two patientreported outcome (PRO) endpoints were included as key secondary endpoints:
•

Change in SF-36v2 bodily pain from start of treatment (Week 0) until Week 24

•

Change in SF-36v2 physical functioning from start of treatment (Week 0) until Week 24

Other exploratory PRO endpoints were included by the Applicant but not assessed by the review
team because they were not included in the statistical testing hierarchy. Of note, all PRO
endpoints are challenging to interpret given the open-label design of the study.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
5.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
Concizumab is a humanized recombinant monoclonal antibody (mAb) that inhibits the function
of TFPIα and TFPIβ, that represent the two major TFPI-1 isoforms (splice variants) involved in
the down regulation of the coagulation cascade in humans. Concizumab binds specifically to the
Kunitz-domain-2 (K2) of the TFPI and prevents TFPI from binding to and blocking the active
site of the coagulation factor Xa (Fxa). When the TFPI inhibitory activity is reduced, the Fxa
produced by the coagulation factor VIIa (FVIIa)/tissue factor (TF) complex results in sufficient
generation of thrombin to achieve hemostasis. The mechanism of action of concizumab is
independent of the presence or absence of FVIII or FIX, and would exert pharmacodynamic
activity in both hemophilia A and B patients, regardless of their inhibitor status. Concizumab
does not bind to the human TFPI-2 protein.
Figure 1. Mechanism of Action (Concizumab)

Source: Applicant’s Figure Nonclinical Overview
Abbreviations: a, activated; TF, tissue factor; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor

The pharmacological activity of concizumab was confirmed by thromboelastography (TEG), that
measures the shear elasticity and the dynamics of clot formation and the strength and stability of
clot formation, using blood derived from humans and cynomolgus monkeys. Concizumab
reduced clotting times and improved clot development in a concentration-dependent manner in
both human and cynomolgus monkey whole blood. Thromboelastography (TEG) conducted
using human whole blood under antibody-induced hemophilia A-like conditions resulted in half
maximal effective concentration (EC50) values of 0.43nM (clot time) and 2.2nM (clot
development), and under antibody-induced hemophilia B-like conditions resulted in EC50 values
of 0.44nM (clot time) and 2.6nM (clot development). Comparable results were observed in
whole blood derived from normal cynomolgus monkeys under antibody-induced hemophilia A21
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

like conditions where the EC50 values were 0.38nM (clot time) and 0.54nM (clot development).
The in vitro, ex vivo and in vivo pharmacology studies are discussed in further detail under
Section 13.1.1.1.
Concizumab neutralized TFPI-mediated inhibition of Fxa and the FVIIa/TF/Fxa complex (in
vitro) with EC50 values of 0.8nM and 1.1nM, respectively. Neutralization of plasma TFPI was
further quantified by measuring TFPI activity in human plasma and assessing FX activation in
vitro (EC50 value, 6nM). These effects occurred at clinically relevant exposures based on the
steady state concizumab concentrations (maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), mean) observed
in hemophilia patients during the Explorer7 study (1167 ng/mL = 7.83nM). Concizumab was
effective at neutralizing TFPI present on the surface of endothelial cells (the predominate form of
TFPI on the cell surface of endothelial cells is TFPIβ). Concizumab associated with the cell
surface (KD values, 0.1nM to 0.4nM) of endothelial cells (HUVEC) and endothelial-like cells
(ECV304), and promoted activation of FX (EC50 values were below 0.5nM for both cell lines).
Based on the pharmacology data and the lack of cross reactivity to rodent species, the rabbit was
selected as the nonclinical efficacy species (hemophilia animal models). The cynomolgus
monkey was selected as the non-rodent nonclinical safety species for repeat-dose toxicology and
both species were used in pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PD) assessments.

5.1.2. Animal Model Data Showing Proof of Concept
In vivo proof-of-concept studies were conducted in a cuticle bleeding model in New Zealand
White (NZW) rabbits where hemophilia A-like conditions were simulated by administration of a
neutralizing FVIII antibody. When administered intravenously (IV) prior to induction of
bleeding, concizumab reduced blood loss in a dose-dependent manner in rabbits with a half
maximal effective dose (ED50) of 0.23 mg/kg (0.074 mg/kg, human equivalent dose (HED)) over
the 1-hour observation period. The antihemorrhagic effect was maintained for up to 7 days after
a single IV dose of 20 mg/kg (6.5 mg/kg, HED) followed by treatment (on Day 4, 7 or 10) with a
neutralizing FVIII antibody to induce pseudo hemophilia. The absence of a notable PD effect on
Day 10 corresponded to a significant decline in plasma concizumab exposure at the same
timepoint. Procoagulant effects were not observed when concizumab was administered (IV) after
an extended bleed induction period (15-30 min), indicating that concizumab needs to be
administered prior to or relatively soon after (≤5 minutes) injury to be efficacious in this rabbit
model of HA. Concizumab was also effective after subcutaneous (SC) administration (24 hours
prior to induction of bleeding using the clinical ROA) in the rabbit cuticle bleeding model at a
dose of 10 mg/kg (3.2 mg/kg, HED).

The effects of concizumab on local clot formation and general coagulation activation were
assessed in a venous stasis model conducted in non-hemophilic NZW rabbits. There were no
notable differences in the number of clots or clot size in ligated facial vein segments, and no
significant differences in platelet counts, prothrombin time (PT), thrombin-anti-thrombin (TAT),
and fibrinogen levels found in the absence or presence of concizumab (2 mg/kg, IV). Lung
thrombi were noted microscopically in 50% of the rabbits administered concizumab and in the
heart of a single control rabbit (25%). The relationship of distal clot formation in the lung and
heart to the clot formation in the ligated facial vein segments could not be definitively
determined, and while the Applicant argues that the formation of lung thrombi may be related to
the experimental procedures, a potential relationship to the administration of concizumab cannot
be excluded.
In a second study, the effect of a low molecular weight heparin (LMWH) in combination with
concizumab was evaluated in a modified venous injury model in non-hemophilic NZW rabbits to
assess the ability of LMWH to interfere with concizumab-induced clot formation. Intravenous
injection (2 mg/kg) of concizumab was followed by a standardized crush injury and eventual
ligation of the facial veins. Local clot formation following crush injury was significantly
increased by the presence of concizumab; however, when followed 5 minutes later by the
administration of LMWH prior to the initiation of the crush injury, local clot formation was
prevented. Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time (APTT) declined minimally in concizumabonly dosed rabbits and APTT clotting time could not be established in the presence of LMWH.
No other notable changes in coagulation parameters (platelet counts, PT, and fibrinogen) were
observed with concizumab dosing, and these results suggest that LMWH can prevent the

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
Table 8. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Characteristic
Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic Concizumab is a tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) antagonist.
class (EPC)
Mechanism of action
Concizumab is a humanized recombinant monoclonal antibody (IgG4) antagonist of human tissue factor pathway inhibitor 1
(TFPI-1), including TFPIα and TFPIβ, that represent the two major TFPI-1 isoforms found in humans. Concizumab specifically
targets the Kunitz 2 (K2) domain of TFPI and prevents TFPI from interacting with the active site of the Stuart–Prower factor
[i.e., activated blood coagulation factor X (Fxa)]. In the presence of reduced TFPI inhibitory activity, the Fxa produced by the
activated factor VII (FVIIa)/tissue factor (TF) complex (extrinsic tenase) results in generation of thrombin. As the mechanism
of action of concizumab does not require the presence of FVIII or FIX, concizumab has the potential to elicit hemostatic
activity in patients with either hemophilia A (congenital FVIII deficiency) or hemophilia B (congenital FIX deficiency),
regardless of their inhibitor (anti-clotting factor antibody) status.
Active moieties
The active moiety is the i (b) (4) humanized Immunoglobulin G (IgG) 4 monoclonal antibody (mAb).
QT prolongation
A thorough QT study was not conducted in humans. QT prolongation was not observed during ECG assessments in
cynomolgus monkeys administered repeat doses of concizumab up to 200 mg/kg/once weekly (IV), 70000x MRHD (AUC)
and 10000X MRHD (Cmax), or 50 mg/kg/day (SC), 14000x MRHD (AUC) and 12600X MRHD (Cmax), for up to 13 weeks. In
addition, QT prolongation was not observed during ECG assessments in cynomolgus monkeys administered repeat doses of
concizumab up to 9 mg/kg/day (SC), 4300x MRHD (AUC) and 4400X MRHD (Cmax), for up to 52 weeks.
Bioanalysis
Total plasma concizumab (bound and unbound) concentration in the Phase 3 study was quantified using a validated ELISA.
Healthy subjects versus

Dose proportionality
Concizumab concentrations increased with increasing dose in a greater-than-dose-proportional manner following SC
administration. Relative to the 0.25 mg/kg dose, geometric mean AUCτ values were 3-fold higher in the 0.50 mg/kg dose and
nearly 11-fold higher in the 0.80 mg/kg dose.

Elimination
N/A
Both linear and non-linear pathways contribute to the elimination of concizumab. This non-linear pharmacokinetic behavior is
caused by target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) which occurs when concizumab binds to endothelial cell-anchored TFPI
with subsequent elimination of the drug-target complex. Non-linear PK via TMDD plays an important role in concizumab
elimination at the clinically relevant concentrations.
Half-life
Concizumab terminal half-life increased with increasing dosage and ranged from 1.6 days to 8.1 days in healthy and
hemophilia subjects receiving a single SC dosing of 0.25 to 3 mg/kg. In the target exposure range of 200 to 4000 ng/mL, the
effective half-life is about 1-2 days.
Metabolic pathway(s)
Concizumab is expected to be metabolized into small peptides by catabolic pathways.

Drug Information

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
The apparent clearance and volume of distribution of concizumab increased with increasing body weight (27 kg to 130.7 kg).
Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment, patients with low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg,
respectively. No further adjustment to dose based on body weight is warranted beyond the recommended adjustment by
Week 8.
No clinically significant differences in the PK of concizumab were observed based on age (12 year to 67 years).
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal impairment. As concizumab is a monoclonal
antibody, there is no expectation for concizumab exposures to be different in patients with renal impairment.
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of hepatic impairment. As concizumab is a monoclonal
antibody, there is no expectation for concizumab exposures to be different in patients with hepatic impairment.

N/A as concizumab is a monoclonal antibody

Immunogenicity
Anti-concizumab antibody assay and in vitro neutralizing antibody assay have been reviewed by Dr. Scott Walsh and were
considered as not fully validated (see comments in the CR letter for details)
In concizumab clinical program, 47 out of 185 of treated patients (25%) developed anti-concizumab antibodies. Twelve (12)
patients (6.5%) tested positive for anti-concizumab antibodies with in vitro-neutralizing effects.
Binding anti-concizumab antibody titers were generally low. In 1 of 47 patients who developed ADAs with a high titer level,
plasma concizumab concentrations cannot be measured, likely due to ADA interference in the PK. In addition, free TFPI
levels returned to the baseline, indicating a loss of treatment effect. For the rest of 46 ADA positive patients, there was no
identified clinically significant effect of the antibodies on PK, PD, safety, or effectiveness of concizumab.
