Concizumab

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
The proposed dosing regimen of concizumab is a loading dose of 1 mg/kg via SC administration
on Day 1 followed by once daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg starting from Day 2 until individuation of
maintenance dose is determined:
•

Four weeks after initiation of treatment: For dose optimization measure concizumab plasma
concentration once by concizumab enzyme-linked immunoassay (ELISA) prior to
administration of next scheduled dose.

•

After concizumab plasma concentration result is available, but recommended no later than 8
weeks after initiation of treatment: Individualize maintenance dose of concizumab once
based on the following concizumab plasma concentrations:
– <200 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.25 mg/kg
– 200 to 4000 ng/mL: continue once-daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg
– >4000 ng/mL: adjust to a once-daily dose of 0.15 mg/kg

The proposed PK-guided dosing with one time PK collection and dose adjustment between
weeks 4 to 8 was implemented in Study 4311 (after restart) and is considered acceptable based
on favorable efficacy and safety results, aided by pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD)
and exposure-response relationships in patients with hemophilia. Large fluctuations in
concizumab plasma concentrations were noted in certain patients in Study 4311 (after restart)
which may be due to an interplay of concizumab’s target mediated drug disposition (TMDD) and
patient noncompliance. It is critical that patients are compliant with administering concizumab
daily (See Section 7.2 for more details).
Selection of Concizumab Target Exposure of 200 ng/mL to 4000 ng/mL
The Applicant proposes that concizumab dose be adjusted once to achieve target trough
concentration between 200 and 4000 ng/mL. The selection of lower exposure limit of 200 ng/mL
is based on exposure-response analysis using data from main part of Studies 4255 and 4310. As
shown in Table 9, at concentrations above 200 ng/mL, there was a trend towards a lower
bleeding rate.
The upper exposure limit of 4000 ng/mL has been set as a measure to avoid patients persistently
reaching very high concentrations of concizumab. Before the pause of Study 4311, 2 of the 3
patients with thrombotic events in phase 3 studies showed exposure levels above 5000 ng/mL
(Figure 2). Despite the lack of data showing that high exposure per se is causative of thrombotic
events, the observation suggests that the concizumab exposure in combination with other risk
factors could contribute to thrombosis. Therefore, to avoid patients reaching very high
concizumab exposure levels, the Applicant proposed a cut-off of 4000 ng/mL at Week 4. The
selected target exposure of 200 to 4000 ng/mL was agreed by the Agency and implemented in
Study 4311 after the trial was restarted.

Necessity of PK-Guided Dosing for Concizumab
Both linear and non-linear clearance pathways contribute to the elimination of concizumab. At
concentrations of approximately 10000 ng/mL or above, the linear clearance pathway is
dominant, and the estimated half-life is approximately 22 days for a 75 kg patient based on
population PK modeling analysis. Over concizumab’s target exposure range of 200 to
4000 ng/mL, the non-linear clearance pathway via TMDD is dominant and the effective half-life
of concizumab is much shorter, about 1 to 2 days. As a result, large between-subject variations in
concizumab exposure are expected due to variability in target pool (i.e., membrane bound TFPI
in the endothelium) among individual patients.
Population PK simulations suggest that a dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg or decrease to 0.15 mg/kg
can reduce the population-level variability and ensure that majority of patients have concizumab
trough concentrations within the target range of 200 to 4000 ng/mL (Figure 3). In Study 4311,

Rationale for Loading Dose
Following a single loading dose of 1 mg/kg, the geometric mean concizumab plasma
concentrations reached steady-state levels by end of Day 1 and stayed within the target exposure
range with daily maintenance doses. This observation was further supported by population PK
predicted concizumab plasma concentration-time profile with a loading dose of 1 mg/kg on Day
1 (Figure 3 and Figure 4).

Source: Figure 20 of EOP2 meeting package dated 01 February 2019

Rationale for Once-Daily Dosing
Study 4159 assessed the PK and PD of concizumab following SC administration of 0.25 mg/kg,
0.5 mg/kg and 0.8 mg/kg doses given every 3 days. This is equivalent to 0.83, 0.17 and
0.27 mg/kg/day. Because clearance is much faster in the target range (as stated earlier), a oncein-3-day dosing is expected to result in larger peak-to-trough fluctuations than desired.
Combined with high inter-subject variability (as noted in the study), this regimen may put some
patients at risk of going below or above the target range. Therefore, in order to reduce peak-totrough fluctuations and maintain exposures within the target range in majority of the patients, a
once-daily dosing was implemented.
Exposure-Response and PK/PD Analysis Supporting the Proposed Dosing Regimen
An exposure-response analysis of bleeding episodes shows that the concizumab dosing regimen
maintains plasma concentrations at or near the plateau of effect (Figure 5). The maximum drug
effect (Emax) was predicted to be a 93% reduction in ABR with an EC50 around 45.2 ng/mL. In
addition, the correlation between pre-dose free TFPI and concizumab plasma concentration
confirms that the most prominent effect is observed at concizumab plasma levels >200 ng/mL
with free TFPI approaching the lower limit of quantification (9.6 ng/mL, Figure 6). Similarly,
PK/PD analysis of thrombin peak shows that the concizumab dosing regimen maintains plasma
concentrations at the plateau of effect.

Concizumab

6.2. Clinical Studies Intended to Demonstrate
Efficacy
6.2.1. Study 4311
6.2.1.1. Study Design, Study 4311
Study Overview
Study 4311 was a Phase 3 prospective, multicenter, open-label, randomized-controlled study in
patients with HAwI and HBwI. The primary objective of the study was to compare concizumab
prophylaxis to no prophylaxis on top of standard of care (on demand therapy) in preventing
bleeding episodes in patients with HAwI and HBwI.
The schema for Study 4311 is shown in Figure 7 below and included 4 study arms. Patients were
randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2), no PPX (arm 1), or assigned to non-randomized
concizumab PPX treatment (arms 3 or 4), based on their treatment regimen for hemophilia
before entering the study. Patients included in the no PPX arm (arm 1) of the study continued the
same on-demand treatment regimens with bypassing agents that they received before entering the
clinical study, reflecting local standards of care. Patients randomized to the concizumab arm
were permitted to use on demand treatment(s) (i.e., bypassing agents and factor) as needed for
breakthrough bleeding. Arm 3 included patients who were previously enrolled in the concizumab
Phase 2 study (NN7415-4310 (explorer4)). Arm 4 included patients previously on prophylaxis
with bypassing agents as well as any patients receiving on-demand therapy who were screened at
a timepoint where the required number of patients in arms 1 and 2 had been randomized.
The study consisted of a 3-week screening period, a main part (study period between Weeks 2432), an extension part (study period between Weeks 128-136), and a 7-week follow-up period.
The main part of the study was considered completed for a patient when at least 24 weeks of
participation (screening period not included) had been completed for patients on no PPX (arm 1),
or 32 weeks of participation (screening period not included) for patients on concizumab PPX
(arms 2, 3 and 4). The longer duration of the study’s main part for arms 2, 3 and 4 was due to the
dose adjustment period in these. After the main part of the study, all patients were offered
opportunities to continue in the extension part of the study and receive treatment with
concizumab for up to an additional 128 weeks (arms 2-4) or 136 weeks (arm 1). A primary
analysis cut-off (PACO) was defined as when all patients on no PPX (arm 1) had completed visit
9/9a (or withdrawn), and all patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) had completed visit 10a (or
withdrawn). Furthermore, a 56-week cut-off was defined as when all patients on concizumab
PPX (arms 2, 3 and 4) had completed visit 13a (or permanently discontinued treatment). At this
56-week cut-off, an additional evaluation was planned to assess bleed-related endpoints and
safety.
The randomization between treatment arms 1 and 2 was stratified according to hemophilia
subtype (HAwI, HBwI) and bleeding frequency during the 24 weeks prior to screening
(<9 bleeding episodes, ≥9 bleeding episodes).
33
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Treatment Pause
Study 4311 was initiated in October 2019. Concizumab treatment was paused in March 2020,
and clinical studies were subsequently put on clinical hold due to the occurrence of 5 non-fatal
thromboembolic events in 3 patients (see Section 7.7.1 for further details). Patients who were
randomized to arm 1 before the treatment pause were instructed to continue their on-demand
treatment during the pause. Patients who were randomized to arm 2 were instructed to
discontinue concizumab treatment, and these patients received available standard of care
treatment during the pause.
In order to mitigate the risk for thromboembolic events the Applicant initiated a new guidance
for treatment of mild and moderate breakthrough bleeding episodes with specific guidance for
use of the lowest dose of rescue factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab PPX.
The Applicant also revised the concizumab dosing regimen, consisting of a loading dose of
1.0 mg/kg (unchanged from the initial dosing regimen), and an initial daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg
concizumab (instead of 0.25 mg/kg). The Applicant also revised the criteria for an increase or
decrease in the daily maintenance dose to 0.25 mg/kg or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively, based on
concizumab exposure level at the Week 4 visit after initiating treatment. After the restart of the
study, patients continued in the treatment arm to which they were initially randomized or
assigned. Newly eligible patients were randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2), no PPX (arm 1),
or assigned to arm 4.
The study visit schedule is shown in Figure 62 and the study schedule of events are shown in
Table 119. Drug stopping criteria included clinical or laboratory evidence of thrombosis,
microangiopathy, disseminated intravascular coagulopathy, or hypersensitivity reactions to
concizumab.

Clinical Reviewer Comment: The open-label study design of comparing concizumab PPX to on
demand treatment with bypassing agents is acceptable, as it would be unethical to include a
placebo control arm. This approach is consistent with other drugs approved for hemophilia (e.g.,
emicizumab). See Section 7 for further discussion regarding the potential impact of the study
pause on the study results.

6.2.1.3. Endpoints, Study 4311

The number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes:

least 24 weeks)

•

Concizumab PPX (arm 2): From start of the new concizumab dosing regimen (Week 0) up
until the primary analysis cut-off (at least 32 weeks)

Key Secondary Endpoints
•

Change in SF-36v2 bodily pain, from start of treatment (Week 0) until Week 24

•

Number of treated spontaneous bleeding episodes

the number of patients with bleeding episodes. This endpoint is clinically meaningful, as having
zero patients with bleeds is the ultimate goal to hemophilia prophylaxis.
6.2.1.4. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 4311
Key information regarding statistical analysis from the clinical study report, protocol, and
statistical analysis plan (SAP) are summarized in this section.
Analysis Population
The following analysis sets were defined for the statistical analysis:
•

Full analysis set (FAS): All patients randomized to concizumab PPX or no PPX or allocated
to arm 3 or 4. Patients from arms 1 and 2 will contribute to the evaluation ‘as randomized’.

•

Safety analysis set (SAS): All patients exposed to concizumab PPX or randomized to no
PPX. All patients will contribute to the evaluation ‘as treated’.

The defined analysis data sets for the main part of the study are included in Figure 9.

Abbreviations: ABR, annualized bleeding rate; HAwI, hemophilia A with inhibitors; HBwI, hemophilia B with inhibitors; PPX,

The comparison of the number of treated bleeds between the randomized arms 1 and 2 was based
on the FAS and the on-treatment without ancillary therapy exclude data on initial regimen for
patients exposed to both regimens (OtwoATexIR) analysis data set using negative binomial
regression with the patient’s number of bleeds analyzed as a function of the randomized
treatment regimen, type of hemophilia (HAwI or HBwI) and bleeding frequency
(<9 or ≥9 bleeding episodes during the past 24 weeks prior to screening) and the logarithm of the
length of the observation period included as an offset in the model.
From the statistical model, an estimate of the rate ratio of the annualized bleeding rate (ABR)
between the treatment regimens (concizumab prophylaxis and no prophylaxis) with
corresponding 95% confidence interval and a p-value for the test for superiority were provided.
Estimates of the actual ABRs with corresponding 95% confidence intervals were also provided
for arms 1 and 2.
Analysis of Key Secondary Endpoints
The two key secondary endpoints included in the statistical testing hierarchy were:
•

Change in SF-36v2 bodily pain from start of treatment (Week 0) until Week 24

•

Change in SF-36v2 physical functioning from start of treatment (Week 0) until Week 24

The Week 0 is defined as time of randomization to on-demand administration or time of start of
the new concizumab dosing regimen.
The two key secondary endpoints based on SF-36v2 were analyzed including all patients in the
FAS using the on-treatment without data on initial regimen (OtexIR) analysis dataset.
Due to the low number of patients in arms 1 and 2 contributing data both at baseline and postbaseline, the pre-planned multiple imputation (MI) analyses for key secondary endpoint based on
SF-36 could not be performed as planned due to non-convergence. Instead of using MI, a post
hoc mixed model for repeated measurements (MMRM) was used in the following way:

39
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

The changes in SF-36v2 scores from Week 0 to Weeks 4, 8, 16 and 24 were analyzed using a
MMRM, with treatment, type of hemophilia (HAwI or HBwI) and bleeding frequency
(<9 or ≥9 bleeding episodes during the 24 weeks prior to screening) as factors, and baseline SF36v2 as a covariate, all nested within week (Week 4, Week 8, Week 16 and Week 24). An
unstructured covariance matrix was used to describe the variability for the repeated
measurements for a patient. From the MMRM model, the treatment difference at Week 24 was
estimated and the corresponding 95% confidence interval and p-value were presented. Change
from baseline estimate and 95% CI for each arm was also obtained, in addition to the estimated
mean score at Week 24 and corresponding 95% CI for each arm.
Statistical Reviewer’s Comments: Because of the low number of patients in arms 1 and 2 and the
following two reasons, the program for the MI analysis could not converge:
•

Large amount of the imputes values lied outside the range with unrealistic imputed
values, (e.g., lower than -200 or higher than 300) which exceed the range of allowed
values for the given PROs

•

Not enough observations to fit regression models with a monotone regression method.

The Applicant discussed the issue with the Agency during the pre-BLA meeting and proposed to
use MMRM as an alternative model. The proposed alternative model was determined to be
reasonable. However, the PRO endpoints were conducted in open-label studies, thus the
Applicant’s results should be interpreted with caution. As discussed in the FDA guidance for
industry Patient-Reported Outcome Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support
Labeling Claims 1, “Open-label clinical studies, where patients and investigators are aware of
assigned therapy, are rarely adequate to support labeling claims based on PRO instruments.
Patients who know they are in an active treatment group may overestimate benefit whereas
patients who know they are not receiving active treatment may under-report the improvement
they experienced.”
Multiple Testing Strategy
The type I error was controlled at 5% significance level (two-sided) using a hierarchical
(fixed sequence) testing procedure. This was based on a priority ordering of the null hypotheses
for major efficacy endpoints. If a hypothesis was rejected (superiority confirmed), the
significance level was to be reallocated to the next hypothesis in the hierarchy and then that
hypothesis was to be tested at the 5% significance level. This process was repeated sequentially
until no further hypotheses can be confirmed.
The steps in the hierarchical testing procedure were as follows:
•

Step 1: Superiority in number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes

•

Step 2: Superiority in change from baseline in SF-36v2 bodily pain

•

Step 3: Superiority in change from baseline in SF-36v2 physical functioning

Per the protocol for Study 4311, randomization between the treatment arms 1 and 2 was stratified
according to hemophilia subtype (HAwI versus HBwI) and bleeding frequency (<9 bleeding
episodes, ≥9 bleeding episodes) during the 24 weeks prior to screening of patients. This
stratification did not apply to treatment arms 3 and 4. In treatment arm 1 there were 7/19 (36.8%)
patients with <9 bleeding episodes and 12/19 (63.2%) patients with ≥9 bleeding episodes during
the 24 weeks prior to screening, respectively. In treatment arm 2, there were 10/33 (30.3%)
patients with <9 bleeding episodes and 23/33 (69.7%) with ≥9 bleeding episodes during the 24
weeks prior to screening, respectively. The table below summarizes the key baseline disease
characteristics for patients in Study 4311.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Concizumab

While more patients in arm 1 (no PPX) had high titer inhibitors levels at screening compared to
arm 2 (concizumab PPX), the patient population remains consistent with patients with HAwI and
HBwI as all patients had a documented history of an inhibitor level >0.6BU. Given the
mechanism of action of concizumab, which is not impacted by FVIII or FIX, it is not expected
that the titer of the inhibitor would impact the efficacy results.
The historical bleeding rate of each patient enrolled was reviewed by investigators and only
documented as above or below 9 bleeds in the previous 24 weeks. The actual number of bleeds
was not recorded in datasets so the baseline bleeding rate could not be reported for each patient.
While not ideal, the study results are still interpretable as the Applicant did stratify
randomization for above or below 9 bleeds in the previous 24 weeks therefore, the severity of
hemophilia based on the previous 24 weeks is expected to be similar between study arms.
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint, Study 4311
Superiority of concizumab PPX (arm 2) over no PPX (arm 1) was confirmed for the primary
endpoint number of treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes. Using a negative
binomial regression model, the estimated mean ABR was 1.7 with 95% CI (1.01, 2.87) for
patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and 11.8 with 95% CI (7.03, 19.86) for patients on no PPX
(arm 1). The estimated ABR ratio between patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) and no PPX
(arm 1) was 0.14 with 95% CI (0.07, 0.29) and 2-sided p-value<0.001, corresponding to an 86%
reduction in ABR for patients on concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to no PPX (arm 1).
The Applicant’s primary efficacy analysis results were confirmed by the FDA statistical
reviewer. Results of the primary efficacy endpoint are summarized in Table 16.
Descriptive results (e.g., median, IQR, mean, standard deviation (SD), min, max, etc.) for
bleeding episodes related endpoints were also provided by the Applicant for arm 1 to 4 and
summarized in Supplementary Section 16.1.

Statistical Reviewer’s Comments: Study 4311 had a concizumab treatment pause for 6 months
during the study due to thromboembolic events reported in 3 patients. After the concizumab
treatment pause and subsequent restart of Study 4311, a new concizumab dosing regimen was
introduced to concizumab PPX arms. The original randomization was potentially compromised
because more than half of the planned sample size had already been enrolled and treated on the
original regimen. There were 4 patients randomized before pause in concizumab PPX arm 2 who
did not restart on the new concizumab dosing regimen. Please refer to Section 8.5.1 for further
clinical and statistical evaluation and discussion regarding the determination if the pause in
Study 4311 due to thrombotic events impacted the benefit (ABR).
Analysis of Key Secondary Endpoints, Study 4311
Because the primary efficacy endpoint analysis was statistically significant (2-sided pvalue<0.001) in favor of concizumab PPX, the two key secondary efficacy endpoints were
further tested in the prespecified hierarchical order.
For both key secondary endpoints, the low number of patients in arms 1 and 2 contributing data
at both baseline and post-baseline caused a convergence issue in the pre-planned multiple
imputation (MI) analyses. Instead of using MI, the Applicant conducted post hoc mixed model
for repeated measurements (MMRM) for both key secondary endpoints.
SF-36v2 Bodily Pain
The first key secondary endpoint was change in SF-36 v2 bodily pain from Week 0 to Week 24.
Applying a mixed model for repeated measurements (MMRM), the estimated mean change in
the bodily pain score from baseline to Week 24 was 9.2 with 95% CI (5.06, 13.25) for patients
on concizumab PPX (arm 2) compared to 2.2 with 95% CI (-5.14, 9.52) for patients on no PPX
(arm 1), yielding a difference estimate at Week 24 of 6.96 with 95% CI (-1.64, 15.57) and 2sided p-value=0.109. This endpoint failed to achieve statistically significant superiority in SF36v2 bodily pain score change from baseline to Week 24 for concizumab PPX compared to no
PPX. The statistical testing hierarchy for key secondary endpoints was stopped at the endpoint
SF-36v2 bodily pain and the results of the endpoints after it can only be considered descriptive.
The Applicant’s primary efficacy analysis results were confirmed by the FDA statistical reviewer
and summarized in Table 17.

Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoints, Study 4311
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess potential differences in the treatment effect for
various demographic and clinical characteristics groups. Because of the small size of the study,
only descriptive statistics for the randomized arm 1 (no PPX) and arm 2 (concizumab PPX) by
age, race, ethnicity, region, and hemophilia subtype were summarized by the FDA statistical
reviewer in Table 19. The sample size was imbalanced for different categories of subgroups and
many subgroup analysis results were limited due to very small numbers of patients. Therefore,
the subgroup analysis Results should be interpreted with caution. For those categories with
relatively larger sample size, results were generally consistent with the full analysis data set
results.
Exploratory Endpoint, Study 4311
The Applicant described the number of patients with treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding
episodes in the first 24 weeks of treatment. For patients on no PPX (arm 1), 17 patients (89.5%)
reported at least one bleeding event during the first 24 weeks, compared to 12 patients (36.4%)
on concizumab PPX (arm 2). In total, 94.7% of patients in arm 1 (no PPX) had a treated bleed or
discontinued before Week 24 compared to 48.5% in arm 2 (concizumab PPX).
Clinical Reviewer Comment: A numerically lower proportion of patients in the concizumab PPX
arm experienced at least one bleeding episode in the main treatment period compared to no
concizumab PPX. While clinically meaningful, these results should be interpreted with caution
as this endpoint was not prespecified.

6.2.2. Study 4310
6.2.2.1. Design, Study 4310
Study 4310, EXPLORER 4, is titled, “A Multi-Centre, Randomized, Open-Label, Controlled
Trial Evaluating the Efficacy and Safety of Prophylactic Administration of Concizumab in
Hemophilia A and B Patients with Inhibitors.” This study is considered supportive of the safety
and efficacy profile of concizumab for the treatment of patients with HAwI and HBwI.
Study 4310 is a Phase 2 interventional, multi-national, multicenter, randomized (2:1), open-label,
controlled, dose escalation study of concizumab 0.15 mg/kg, 0.20 mg/kg and 0.25 mg/kg
administered daily SC compared to treatment with eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) in patients with HAwI
and HBwI. Only patients using on-demand therapies were eligible for the study. The study
consisted of a 2-week screening period, a subsequent 76 to 118-week treatment period and an 8week follow-up period. The 76 to 118-week treatment period consisted of a main part which
lasted 24 weeks for patients randomized to eptacog alfa and at least 24 weeks for patients
randomized to concizumab. The extension part lasted up to 94 weeks. The study schema is
shown in the figure below.
In all patients treated with concizumab, a single 90μg/kg dose of eptacog alfa was administered
in a non-bleeding state one week after dosing with concizumab had been initiated. The
investigator evaluated if there were any safety concerns 24 hours post eptacog alfa
administration. Furthermore, the scheduled administration of eptacog alfa was staggered for the
first four patients entering the study with concizumab. In the comparator arm, patients received
eptacog alfa on-demand treatment during the main part of the study. After completion of the
main part, the patients continued the study in the extension part being treated with prophylactic
daily SC doses of 0.15 mg/kg concizumab (with potential dose escalation).
Figure 10. Trial Schema, Study 4310

Source: Applicant CSR for Study 4310
Abbreviation: CTR, clinical trial report; rFVIIa, recombinant activated factor VII; V, visit

6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study 4310
Key Inclusion Criteria
•

Informed consent obtained before any study related activities

•

Male HA or HB patients with inhibitors aged ≥18 years at the time of signing informed
consent

•

Patients currently in need of treatment with bypassing agents

Key Exclusion Criteria
•

Known or suspected hypersensitivity to study product(s) or related products

•

Known inherited or acquired bleeding disorder other than hemophilia

•

Ongoing or planned immune tolerance induction therapy or prophylaxis with FVIII or FIX
6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study 4310

Key information regarding statistical analysis from the clinical study report, protocol, and
statistical analysis plan (SAP) are summarized in this section.
Analysis Population
All dosed patients were included in the Full Analysis Set (FAS) as well as in Safety Analysis Set
(SAS).
Statistical Reviewer’s Comments: According to the Study 4310 Clinical Study Report (CSR), all
the 26 subjects randomized to this study were dosed. Thus, the FAS is the same as the Intent-toTreat, which is defined as all randomized patients.
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
The primary endpoint for this study was the number of bleeding episodes occurring at least 24
weeks from treatment onset. All treated bleeding episodes were considered for this endpoint,
including bleeding episodes recorded as post-surgical or caused by surgery or other medical or
dental procedures. The reporting of the primary endpoint was produced via two Clinical Proof of
Concept (CpoC) criteria that were evaluated in a hierarchical fashion:
•

The primary CpoC (CpoC1) criterion aimed at evaluating the effect of concizumab at the last
dose level reached for a patient. Hence, for this evaluation, only observations from the period
where patients were on their last dose level at the time of analysis contributed to the analysis.

•

The secondary CpoC (CpoC2) criterion aimed at evaluating the effect of concizumab when
given as an escalation regimen. This analysis included all bleeding episodes (i.e., from the
entire treatment period).

54
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

The primary endpoint was analyzed based on the FAS using a negative binomial regression with
log of exposure time in the included observational period of the main part as offset and regimen
as factor.
•

The offset for first CpoC criterion of patients in concizumab arm is the log of the individual
exposure time at the last dose level reached at the time of analysis excluding the 2 weeks runin period for patients on 0.15 mg/kg

•

The offset for the second criterion of patients in concizumab arm is the log of the individual
exposure time in the main part

•

For patients in the on-demand arm regardless of criterion, the offset is the log of the exposure
time in the main part

The analysis provides an estimate of the ABR ratio between regimens (concizumab prophylactic
and on-demand eptacog alfa (rFVIIa)) with corresponding 95% confidence interval in addition to
the actual estimate of the ABR with corresponding 95% confidence interval for each regimen.
Multiple Testing Strategy
Two CpoC criteria were evaluated in a hierarchical fashion following the aforementioned order.
No secondary endpoints were included in the statistical testing hierarchy.
Handling of Intercurrent Event or Missing Data
The primary endpoint: The primary analysis method had the underlying assumption that the
missing data mechanism is “missing at random”, (i.e., MAR). Only data collected prior to
discontinuation of study product or initiation of alternative treatment options were used to draw
inference.
6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study 4310
Patient Disposition
Overall, in Study 4310 there were 26 patients randomized, 17 patients (HAwI = 9, HBwI = 8)
randomized to concizumab and 9 patients (HAwI = 7, HBwI = 2) randomized to eptacog alfa.
The table below shows the patient disposition in Study 4310. A total of 25 patients completed the
main part and were exposed to concizumab in the extension part. Of the 25 patients, 22 (88%)
patients completed the treatment, and 3 patients discontinued the treatment, 2 were discontinued
due to ‘other’ reasons (1 was due to withdrawal of consent,1 was due to suspect of no therapeutic
effect due to normal TFPI level with binding antibody) and 1 was due to lack of efficacy (more
than 3 treated spontaneous bleeding episodes within 12 weeks on 0.25 mg/kg concizumab).

At baseline, 54% of patients had family history of hemophilia. The mean time from diagnosis

came from an on-demand treatment regimen. However, four of the patients also received
prophylaxis within a year prior to screening (two patients in each study arm). At baseline, 20 out
of 26 patients were reported to have at least one target joint. The target joint location for these 20
patients included ankle, elbow, hip, knee, shoulder, and wrist. The mean number of bleeds in
specified joint within 1-year prior to screening was 8.1 (range 3−30 bleeds) in the concizumab
Arm and 6.3 (range 3-12) in the eptacog alfa arm.
Clinical Reviewer Comment: Overall, the baseline demographic and disease characteristics
were well balanced between study arms.
Analysis of the Primary Endpoint, Study 4310
The Primary CpoC (CpoC1) Criterion
The primary CpoC (CpoC1) criterion aimed at evaluating the effect of concizumab at the last
dose level reached for a patient. Hence, for this evaluation, only observations from the period
when patients were on their last dose level at the time of analysis contributed to the analysis.
In the concizumab arm, the estimated ABR on the last dose level for each patient was 4.5 with a
95% CI of [3.2, 6.4]. In the eptacog alfa (rFVIIa) on-demand arm, the estimated ABR was 20.4
with a 95% CI of [14.4, 29.1]. The ABR ratio was 0.22 with a 95% CI of [0.13, 0.36]. The
analysis results are summarized in Table 23.

Concizumab

Dedicated juvenile animal studies have not been conducted with concizumab as studies in
neonatal and infant monkeys are technically challenging and are unlikely to identify potential
reactions that were not identified from studies in fully developed (adult) animals. Notable
findings in the hemostatic system and immune-related vascular toxicity were observed in
adolescent (2 to 3 years of age) and sexually mature (3.5 to 6 years of age) cynomolgus
monkeys. The human hemostatic system matures during the early weeks and months of life, and
the concentrations of most hemostatic proteins, both In full-term and preterm infants, are
comparable to adult values by 6 months of age (Albisetti 2003; Chalmers 2004).
Table 25. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Pregnancy and Lactation
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
Pregnancy
Dedicated reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were not
conducted as concizumab is primarily indicated for use in male patients with
hemophilia and the clinical development program was conducted exclusively in
males.
Hemophilia A is inherited in an X-linked recessive manner and the disease
which occurs predominantly in males (99%) is extremely rare in females.
Concizumab is also being indicated for hemophilia B (Factor IX deficiency or
Christmas disease, caused by mutations in the Factor IX (FIX) gene on the X
chromosome) and hence, is predominantly observed in males (some females
who carry the gene may display mild or, rarely, severe symptoms of bleeding).
8.3 Females and
Histopathological evaluations conducted during repeat-dose toxicology studies
Males of
in adolescent (up to 13 weeks in duration) and sexually mature (up to 52
Reproductive
weeks in duration) male and female cynomolgus monkeys did not elicit any
Potential
notable effects in reproductive organs.
In mice, inactivation of the TFPI-gene has been shown to result in embryonic
lethality (Huang et al. 1997; Ellery et al. 2015) and total TFPI deficiency has
not been described in humans (Wood et al. 2014). As placental transfer of IgG,
particularly during organogenesis, may differ between mice and humans, the
clinical relevance of these findings is unknown (DeSesso et al. 2012).
Recurrent pregnancy loss has been observed in women who exhibited TFPI
deficiency in combination with an Activated Protein C (APC) resistance
phenotype or in women with anti-TFPI antibodies in combination with
antiphospholipid antibodies and suggests that TFPI may be critical to the
maintenance of normal pregnancy in humans (Martinuzzo et al. 2005;
Gardiner et al. 2006).
The potential risk of blocking TFPI with a monoclonal IgG antibody (e.g.,
concizumab) in pregnant women is not clear.

Concizumab

13.1 Carcinogenesis,
Mutagenesis,
Impairment of Fertility

Concizumab is not functional in rodents and standard carcinogenicity studies
have not been conducted. Instead, the Applicant conducted a carcinogenicity
risk assessment of concizumab using a weight-of-evidence (WOE) approach
that considered the mode of action (MOA), a review of relevant literature and
an evaluation of the nonclinical and clinical data. It was concluded that
treatment with concizumab has the potential to enhance the prothrombotic
conditions observed in some cancer patients (as would be expected for any
procoagulant compound). However, the assessment did not identify evidence
that treatment with concizumab could definitively lead to an increased risk of
inducing or enhancing tumor development in humans. The Division determined
that no additional nonclinical studies assessing the carcinogenic potential of
concizumab were warranted for the indications of hemophilia A and hemophilia
B and the CDER Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC)
concurred with the Division’s recommendation.
Genotoxicity studies were not conducted, as concizumab consists of naturally
occurring amino acids (endogenous) and is not anticipated to be genotoxic
and there were no mechanistic data that suggested a mutagenic potential for
concizumab.
Fertility evaluations were included in the 26-week chronic toxicity study
conducted in sexually mature male and female cynomolgus monkeys and
there were no notable effects on the menstrual cycle duration, the length,
width and volume of the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility or sperm
morphology in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at doses up to
9 mg/kg/day (SC).
Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of reproductive toxicology studies submitted to the BLA.
Abbreviations: APC, Activated Protein C; ECAC, Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee; FIX, Factor IX; IgG,
immunoglobulin G; MOA, mode of action; SC, subcutaneous; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor; WOE, weight-of-evidence.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Determination If the Pause in Study 4311 Due
to Thrombotic Events Impacted the Benefit
(ABR)
Issue
Determination if the pause in Study 4311 due to thrombotic events impacted the benefit (ABR).
Background
Study 4311 had a concizumab treatment pause for 6 months due to thromboembolic events
reported in 3 patients. After the concizumab treatment pause and subsequent restart of Study
4311, a new concizumab dosing regimen was introduced to concizumab PPX arms. The original
randomization could have been compromised because more than half of the planned sample size
(refer to Table 27) were already enrolled and treated on the original regimen. Four patients
randomized before the study pause in the concizumab PPX arm 2 did not restart on the new
concizumab dosing regimen.

Assessment
As recommended in previous IND meetings, the impact of potential bias introduced by the
treatment pause and the robustness of the primary analysis was investigated by the Applicant
with four sensitivity analyses. The results from all four sensitivity analyses in Table 28 were
confirmed by the FDA statistical reviewer. It was determined that the results of superiority from
the primary analysis are robust.

Conclusion
Study 4311 was paused due to thromboembolic events however, multiple sensitivity analysis
confirmed the conclusion of superiority of concizumab PPX over on-demand therapy in
reduction in ABR. Despite the study pause, Study 4311 is determined to be an adequate and well
controlled study and provides substantial evidence of effectiveness of concizumab for routine
prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and adolescent
patients with HAwI and HBwI.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Toxicological characterization of concizumab was assessed in cynomolgus monkeys (4 weeks,
13 weeks, 26 weeks, and 52 weeks) with normal intact coagulation systems (non-hemophilic)
during repeat-dose toxicology studies. Cynomolgus monkeys were considered the most relevant
62
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

nonclinical species for toxicological evaluations based on amino acid sequence homology
between humans and cynomolgus monkeys in the target epitope (K2 domain), and the observed
binding and functional cross-reactivity of concizumab to human and cynomolgus monkey
endogenous TFPI (Section 13.1).
No definitive risks were identified during in vitro safety pharmacology assessments (human Fc
receptor binding, antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC), complement fixation
and binding to peripheral blood cells) or during in vivo safety pharmacology assessments
conducted as part of the 13-week and/or 52-week repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus
monkeys (cytokine release, blood pressure measurement, ECG, respiration rate, urinalysis, CNS
endpoints). Furthermore, activated protein C (APC) generation and antithrombin activity
(negative regulators of the coagulation cascade) were not affected by the presence of concizumab
(in vitro), and these results were consistent with the absence of changes in APC and antithrombin
levels in the repeat-dose toxicology studies conducted in cynomolgus monkeys. The in vitro and
in vivo safety pharmacology studies are discussed in further detail under Section 13.1.1.2.
The results of the nonclinical toxicology studies were consistent with the expected mechanism of
action of TFPI inhibition, which results in a downstream increase in thrombin generation and
hemostasis. These events were reflected by an increase in markers of coagulation activation,
Thrombin-Antithrombin (TAT) and D-dimers, and a decrease in fibrinogen levels in repeat-dose
cynomolgus monkey studies. Minor changes in clotting parameters (prolonged APTT and PT
and/or a decrease in platelet counts) were induced at supra-pharmacological exposures that
exceeded 400-fold the anticipated exposure at the proposed therapeutic dose during the 26-/52week toxicity studies. This response is likely indicative of homeostatic regulation of the
coagulation system in cynomolgus monkeys in a normo-coagulative state.
Notable findings in nonclinical toxicology studies conducted in cynomolgus monkeys included
thrombi and focal vascular changes. Thrombi were predominantly observed in the lungs and
heart and were associated with a single early mortality (HD female presented with lung thrombi,
lung parenchymal necrosis, and anemia in the 26-week study) across all of the repeat-dose
toxicology studies conducted in cynomolgus monkeys. The onset of thrombi was regarded as an
exaggerated pharmacological effect of administering a procoagulant to cynomolgus monkeys
with normal intact coagulation systems. Focal vascular changes were primarily observed in the
choroid plexus (in the cerebral ventricles of the brain), lungs, and at the injection sites and
included endothelial hypertrophy, subendothelial thickening and/or inflammatory cell
infiltration. Mechanistic analyses (discussed in detail under Section 13.1.2.5.3) suggested that the
observed vascular changes were due to an immunogenic reaction driven by the development of
anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) and subsequent formation/deposition of immune-complexes (Ics).
The extent of ADA-related-immune complex formation exceeded the clearance capacity for Ics
in cynomolgus monkeys and the associated vascular changes were found to be reversible,
consistent with the eventual clearance of the Ics. There were no notable changes observed in
immunotoxicity, local tolerance, or fertility endpoints incorporated into the repeat-dose toxicity
studies.
The toxicities observed in nonclinical studies occurred at exposures which were significantly
higher than those anticipated at the proposed therapeutic dose and interpretation of these safety
margins should also take into consideration that the toxicology studies were conducted in
normal, healthy cynomolgus monkeys with intact coagulation systems. This clearly shifted the
63
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

toxicological exposure/response curve leftward, and the exaggerated pharmacological effects and
immunogenic responses observed in cynomolgus monkeys were not recapitulated in clinical
studies (albeit at significantly lower exposures). Thus, the results from the toxicology studies
more accurately represent the consequences of excessive procoagulant effects in normal (nondisease state) animals and, presumably, what may occur in human subjects who do not harbor a
clotting disorder, such as hemophilia. Additionally, patients with hemophilia would be expected
to have a reduced immunogenic response towards a humanized mAb like concizumab, and the
risk of IC formation exceeding the clearance capacity in humans is considered low. The repeatdose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys are discussed in further detail under Section 13.1.2.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Concizumab elicited a minimal release of TFPI from the surface of endothelial cells (compared
to heparin) and is consistent with the mechanism required for TFPI-release that involves
association with the C-terminal region (not the K2 domain) of the TFPIα form. This result
suggests that the potential risk of concizumab-mediated release of TFPI from the surface
endothelial cells (an anticoagulant effect) is low.
In certain pathological situations (e.g., severe generalized infections and/or sepsis) increased
expression of tissue factor (TF) can occur. Because TFPI is involved in regulation of the TFrelated response, it is a theoretical concern that inhibition of TFPI in situations with increased TF
expression may lead to increased thrombogenicity, and thereby complicate and/or aggravate
these clinical conditions. Situations with increased expression of TF have not been specifically
investigated in nonclinical studies conducted with concizumab, and based on the literature, a
theoretical concern of thrombotic events cannot be excluded if concizumab is used in patients
with increased TF expression.
Issue
PK fluctuations leading to subtherapeutic concentrations during concizumab treatment
64
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Background
As stated earlier, concizumab’s clearance through TMDD is predominant in the target exposure
of 200 to 4000 ng/mL. The effective half-life in the target exposure range is estimated to be only
one to two days which necessitates once-daily dosing of concizumab to reduce peak-to-trough
fluctuations. Additionally, due to variability in target pool (membrane bound TFPI) between
patients, PK guided dosing is necessary to maintain concizumab exposures within the target
range. Despite these measures, significant fluctuations in plasma PK profiles were observed in
individual patients.
In Study 4311, PK samples for measuring concizumab concentrations were collected at each
study visit throughout the study, which allows for review of the observed PK profile over the
course of concizumab treatment in each individual patient (Figure 11). For example, patient ID
(b) (6)
had an 87-fold higher concizumab concentration at Week 12 compared to Week 8 at the
dose of 0.2 mg/kg. Following the dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg between Week 12 and Week 16,
the concizumab concentrations at Week 20 was 10500 ng/mL but decreased to 970 ng/mL
(approximately 90% decrease) at Week 24, while the dose was not changed. In another example,
(b) (6)
patient ID
exhibited an increase in concizumab concentration to 1200 ng/mL after the
dose increase to 0.25 mg/kg, but during subsequent visits the levels decreased to 5 ng/mL (99%
decrease in exposure) while on the same maintenance dose. These patients PK profiles prompted
the review team to look systematically into patients whose concentrations during concizumab
treatment either went below or exceeded the target exposure range. The review team noted that
42 (43%) out of 97 patients had concizumab exposure levels below 200 ng/mL at some point
during the maintenance dose setting and up until primary analysis cut-off (PACO). Upon further
(b) (6)
review, the team identified a small proportion of patients (e.g., patient ID
) who had concizumab concentrations far below the therapeutic range for a
prolonged period.
It should be noted that the lower end of the target exposure range for efficacy i.e., 200 ng/mL, is
adequately supported by exposure-response (E-R) and PK/PD data from Study 4311 (See Section
6.1). An increased risk for bleeding is expected when concentrations fall below 200 ng/mL. That
said, concizumab exposures occasionally dropping below 200 ng/mL probably does not pose a
significant risk, as the previously identified 42 patients have ABR that was significantly lower
than in patients without concizumab treatment. However, it is the few patients whose exposure
falls below 200 ng/mL for prolonged periods that is of concern. This is reflected in patient ID
(b) (6)
, who had concizumab concentrations near or below the subtherapeutic levels for
prolonged periods before the fatal bleeding event (intracranial hemorrhage). A 100-fold change
(b) (6)
in concizumab levels were observed in Patient ID
, where the trough concentration was
about 3000 ng/mL at Week 24 when concizumab was dosed at the study site and 28 ng/mL at
Week 48, 8 days before the fatal bleeding event occurred (See Table 30). Therefore, the review
team is mainly concerned that consistently lower concentrations of concizumab far below
200 ng/mL for a prolonged period of time may put the patient at risk for bleeding, sometimes
even a serious/fatal bleeding event.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Assessment
The issue of significant PK fluctuation in few individual patients was communicated to the
Applicant. The Applicant associates the high degree of inter- and intra-subject variability to
concizumab’s TMDD. While a high inter-subject PK variability of concizumab can be expected
based on varying target pool between patients, the magnitude of within-subject PK changes, as
noted in few individuals, is unlikely to be completely explained by TMDD alone. Therefore, the
review team considered other factors that may play a role in these observations. These are
discussed in detail below:
•

ADAs: A total of 16 out of the 42 patients with concizumab exposure levels below
200 ng/mL tested positive for binding ADAs at some point during maintenance dose setting
and up until PACO. Most occurrences were transient, and titers were very low. Three
(b) (6)
patients had in vitro neutralizing antibodies at one or a few visits (Subjects
); however, binding titers were very low, and no increased bleeding were
observed in these patients. In general, there was no association between occurrence of ADAs
and bleeding episodes (if any) for patients testing positive for ADAs. The immunogenicity
analysis based on comparison between ADA positive and negative patients also supports a
lack of impact on concizumab PK and PD due to ADAs (See Section 14.4).

•

PK assay: ELISA that was used in the clinical study was adequately validated and accepted
by the Agency as fit-for-purpose to determine concizumab plasma concentrations. During the
study, PK and PD samples were collected in a time-matched manner. Considering that the
concentration of free TFPI (PD marker) was determined using a commercial assay and which
mostly shows an inverse relationship with PK, it suggests that the large fluctuation in
concizumab concentration is unlikely to be due to any imprecision with the PK assay.

•

Membrane-bound TFPI: Concizumab clearance is mainly mediated by TMDD, where
concizumab binds to endothelial cell-anchored TFPI (the target) with subsequent endocytosis
and degradation (target-mediated clearance). It is not clear if the expression of membrane
bound TFPI could increase during concizumab treatment and lead to increased drug
clearance. However, the mean Cave of concizumab in all patients receiving concizumab was
relatively stable over the treatment period, which suggests that increased target synthesis may
be unlikely and cannot explain the large PK variability observed in a small portion of
patients.

Lastly, one plausible explanation for significant changes in drug levels during treatment could be
due to lack of patient adherence to daily SC injection of concizumab. As noted earlier, based on
concizumab’s PK characteristics, administration once-daily is critical to avoid large peak-totrough fluctuations. The assessment of the review team related to patient compliance is discussed
below:
•

The impact of missed doses on concizumab concentrations was demonstrated in Study 4159.
In this study, concizumab was dosed at 0.25, 0.5 and 0.8 mg/kg on Day 1, Day 2, and every 3
days thereafter. As all concizumab SC injections were administered at the study site, the
dosing records were reliable and complete, which allows for the analysis on the impact of
missing doses on concizumab PK. Table 31shows the pre-dose concizumab concentrations at
each visit in the 6 subjects who were assigned to the 0.8 mg/kg group (equivalent to
68

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

0.27 mg/kg/day). Although there is some degree of concentration fluctuation at each visit, a
more than 10-fold change in PK concentration within individual patients is primarily
(b) (6)
associated with missing doses. For example, subject ID
(represented by the blue line)
had a big drop in concentration from 3140 ng/mL on Day 5 to 346 ng/mL on Day 11 due to
one missed dose on Day 8. Similar trends for larger drops in concentration with a missed
dose were also observed in other patients (highlighted in red).
•

For patient ID
, at the two study visits where drug dosing was done on site and when
rich PK samples were collected, concizumab concentrations were 1280 ng/mL at the end of
Day 1 and 2890 ng/mL at Week 24. However, at other visits where only pre-dose PK
samples were collected, concizumab levels were mostly below the target level of 200 ng/mL
(Table 30). The reason for such a pattern is hard to explain for anything other than patient
noncompliance. To corroborate this inference, the patient’s e-Diary shows multiple
incidences of missing dose for more than four consecutive days (See Section 7.7.3). As per
the Applicant, the trend for lower concentrations coincided with large number of missing
doses in this patient. In addition, protocol violation for missing doses were also reported in
other patients.

•

The significant impact of missed dosing on concizumab concentration is also consistent with
the PK characteristics of this drug. As a monoclonal antibody, the PK of concizumab is
characterized by a combination of saturable non-linear TMDD and nonspecific linear
elimination processes. At the target concentration range, TMDD plays a dominant role in the
clearance of concizumab leading to a rapid clearance and a short half-life. As illustrated in
Figure 12, following administration of a single SC dose of 1 mg/kg, concizumab
concentrations drop steeply from a Cmax of around 2000 ng/mL to <200 ng/mL within a
couple of days without subsequent dosing. When concentrations drop to low subtherapeutic
levels due to missed doses, in the absence of a loading dose, it may take several days of
consecutive dosing to gradually return to target concentrations of >200 ng/mL (light blue line
in Figure 13).

(b) (6)

Taken together, these assessments strongly suggests that patient compliance to daily
administration of concizumab is critical to maintain therapeutic concentrations.

Conclusion
Overall, based on the PK characteristics of concizumab and observation in Study 4159, patient
compliance to daily administration is critical to maintain therapeutic concentrations. Therefore,

the Applicant should propose labeling language that clearly informs of the risks of missed doses,
the importance of adherence prior to the measurement of concizumab concentrations after four
weeks of treatment, and a proposal for how to initiate the next dose after missed dose(s) (e.g.,
repeat loading dose?). The Applicant should also enhance patient education on adherence to
therapy, likely by including a Medication Guide in FDA labeling.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Concizumab is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or elsewhere.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety review focuses on the Phase 3 Study 4311. Supportive safety information was
obtained from the Phase 2 dose escalation Study 4310. Studies were analyzed separately due to
differences in study design and concizumab dosing regimen.
The safety database of Study 4311 included a total of 133 adolescent or adult patients with HAwI
or HBwI, of which 127 patients were exposed to concizumab PPX in either the main treatment
period or the extension period. In total, there were 19 patients in the no PPX arm 1 in the main
treatment portion (randomized portion consisting of 24 weeks of study drug therapy) of the study
and 13 patients continued the study to receive concizumab prophylaxis (PPX) in the extension
portion of the study. In arm 2 (concizumab PPX arm) of the study, 33 patients were evaluated in
the main treatment portion of the study. In total, there were 81 patients in arms 3 to 4 who
received concizumab PPX, which includes patients who were enrolled in the main treatment
portion of the study, the extension portion, and follow-up parts of the study.
Support for the safety database included 26 adult patients with HAwI or HBwI from Study 4310,
of which 25 patients were exposed to concizumab in either main treatment period or the
extension period.
The data cutoff date for the safety assessments submitted in the BLA was March 10, 2022. The
120-Day safety update provided additional safety data collected from ongoing clinical studies,
including Study 4311 through July 11, 2022.
Review of safety was based upon:
•

Clinical Study Report (CSR) for studies

•

Protocol for studies

•

Data sets for the populations described above

•

Summary of clinical safety

•

120-day safety update

•

Patient narratives

•

Case report forms

Concizumab

Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for adverse events (AEs) of
interest, serious adverse events (SAEs), and deaths. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs)
of interest were thromboembolic events.
No major issues were identified with the respect to recoding, coding, and categorizing AEs. The
analysis dataset for adverse events data file was reviewed for accuracy of translation of the
verbatim to preferred term by manual review.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The Applicant reported that a total of 185 unique patients with HAwI and HBwI and HA have
been exposed to concizumab, corresponding to a total of 228.6 patient-years exposed (PYE) in
the overall concizumab clinical development program. Across the concizumab clinical
development program, 148 (80%) patients of the pooled population were exposed to concizumab
for at least 6 months, corresponding to the duration of the main part in Study 4311 and Study
4310.
In Study 4311, the median duration of the main treatment period was 31.1 weeks (range 3.9, 72.9
weeks) in arm 1 (No PPX) compared to a longer duration of therapy of 45.0 weeks (range 10.0,
58.3 weeks) in arm 2 (concizumab PPX). The proportion of patients who completed between 32
and 52 weeks of therapy was 6/19 (32%) patients in arm 1 and 22/33 (66.7%) patients in arm 2.
Median duration based on the main treatment and extension period for arms 2 to 4 was 40.7
weeks (range 32.1, 68.2 weeks).
Overall, the duration of therapy was adequate to evaluate the safety of concizumab compared to
no PPX. The reviewer table below summarizes the duration of exposure in the safety population
in Study 4311.
In Study 4310 only patients on previous on-demand therapy were enrolled. The median duration
of treatment in study 4310 was 39.3 weeks (range 23.3, 48.1 weeks) in the concizumab arm and
23.9 weeks (range 10, 24.7 weeks) in the eptacog alfa arm before the data cut-off for the main
part of the study. The median duration of therapy in the 24 week main treatment period and
extension portions of the study was 30.3 weeks (21.3, 46.1 weeks) in the concizumab arm and
2.4 weeks (range 1.0, 5.0 weeks) in the eptacog alfa arm in the main portion of the study. After
24 weeks patients in the eptacog alfa arm crossed over to concizumab treatment. There were 16
out of 17 patients in the concizumab arm in Study 4310 who were treated with concizumab for at
least 24 weeks. No patients randomized to the eptacog alfa arm received eptacog alfa beyond 24
weeks.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Results, Study 4311
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study 4311
In Study 4311, more patients in the concizumab PPX arm 2 experienced TEAEs compared to the
no PPX arm 1, 60.6% versus 42.1%, respectively. AEs in either treatment arm were primarily
reported as mild severity. A similar proportion of patients in arms 1 and 2 reported SAEs in the
main part of the study, although more patients reported adverse events (AEs) leading to study
drug dose modification in arm 2 compared to arm 1, 15.2% versus 0%, respectively. AEs leading
to treatment dose modification in arm 2 were headache, dizziness, melena, each reported in one
patient and COVID-19 infection reported two patients. The reviewer’s table below shows an
overview of adverse events in Study 4311.

to bleeding related complications. As a recent concizumab level was not reported, it is not clear
if the patient had a subtherapeutic level at the time of the event, increasing the risk of fatal
bleeding. .

. This patient was a male age 13 years with HBwI. In the 24 weeks prior to
study enrollment, 3 bleeding episodes and 5 spontaneous bleeding episodes (<9 bleeding

were administered at the site at visit 2a and 9a. The patient was treated in arm 2 with
concizumab. He had his individual maintenance dose set at visit 6a, in which the dose of
concizumab was increased to 0.25 mg/kg due to a subtherapeutic level. All other injections
were to be administered at home by the patient or caregiver. On study day 345, the patient
developed an intense headache and repeated vomiting and was instructed by the investigator
to go to the nearest emergency room (ER). The following day, the mother took the patient to
the ER. A CT scan was performed and showed an intracranial hemorrhage that required
surgical intervention with hematoma drainage. Surgery was performed the same day. Six
days following the surgery, the patient died after presenting tachycardia with hypotension
and subsequently cardio-respiratory arrest (death diagnosis: brain death). According to the
Applicant, the patient’s mother confirmed that the bleeding event was spontaneous and there
was no recent history of trauma. There was no family or personal history of intracerebral
hemorrhage or aneurysms.

Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as AEs that occurred in the On-treatment observation period.
The On-treatment observation period was used for assessment of AEs and is defined as the time period where subjects are considered to be affected by on demand treatment or concizumab
treatment. Thus, for concizumab treated patients, On-treatment includes data before the treatment pause (including 7 weeks follow-up from the last dose of concizumab) as well as after concizumab
treatment restart.
Serious adverse events defined as any untoward medical occurrence that, at any dose that results in death, is life-threatening, requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization,
results in persistent incapacity or substantial disruption of the ability to conduct normal life functions, or is a congenital anomaly or birth defect.
Duration is at least 24 weeks (arm 1) or 32 weeks (arms 2-4) for the main part, 128-136 weeks for the extension part, and 7 weeks for the follow-up part of the study.
Median analysis duration based on the data are 31.1 weeks (No PPX Arm 1 Main), 45 weeks (Concizumab PPX arm 2 Main), 49.4 weeks (arm 1 Extension), 55.3 weeks (arm 2 Overall), 70.4 weeks
(arm 3 Overall), and 33.1 weeks (arm 4 Overall).

Clinical Reviewer Comment: SAEs occurred infrequently in Study 4311, the most common
serious adverse reaction in the entire study was viral infection, which occurred in 2 patients.
Hemorrhage was not considered related to concizumab, as it is likely due to the underlying
disease. Thrombotic events are discussed in Section 7.7.1. Of note, one patient in arm 2 of the
clinical study experienced a serious hypersensitivity reaction 12 hours after exposure to
concizumab. Symptoms included generalized erythema and pruritus with skin rash, persistent dry
cough, and abdominal pain, leading to hospitalization. Concizumab was temporarily
discontinued and symptoms improved with antihistamines and steroids. As this event was likely
related to study drug, if approved, the product label should include a warning for
hypersensitivity reactions.
7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Study 4311
Adverse events (AEs) leading to treatment discontinuation were uncommon in Study 4311. In
arm 2 there was 1 patient who had an adverse event (AE) leading to treatment discontinuation
(i.e., renal infarct (reported as arterial thrombosis by narrow FDA medical query) during the
randomized treatment portion of the study as shown in the table below.
In total, 4 patients (3.5%) discontinued concizumab due to a TEAE during the entire study (main
and extension portion).

Concizumab

Clinical Reviewer Comment: Drug discontinuation rates were low, supporting the tolerability of
concizumab. Thrombotic events, including the case of renal infarct are discussed in Section
7.7.1.
One patient discontinued due to a TEAE of congestive cardiomyopathy on study Day 4. Of note,
the patient had pre-existing congestive cardiomyopathy, which worsened during the study. Due
to the AE, the patient prematurely discontinued treatment on study Day 24 and later recovered.
This case is confounded by pre-existing heart failure; therefore, attribution is difficult.
One patient discontinued due to a hypersensitivity reaction. On study Day 14 (1 day after SC
administration of concizumab in the abdomen), the patient had a non-serious event of injection
site rash with burning and itching. Concizumab was temporarily discontinued for 7 days. After
restarting concizumab the patient experienced a headache and fatigue which was treated with
antihistamines. Following this, concizumab was permanently discontinued. Given the event was
due to concizumab, along with another patient experiencing a serious hypersensitivity reaction
following concizumab treatment (see Section 7.6.1.3 for further details), if approved,
hypersensitivity reactions should be included as a warning in the product label.
7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 4311
TEAEs were reported in 60% of patients in arm 2 compared to 42% patients in arm 1 in the
randomized treatment portion of Study 4311. With the exception of 2 patients in arm 2 reporting
arthralgia, no AE was reported in ≥2 patients in either treatment arm in the randomized treatment
portion of Study 4311 as shown in the table below.
Overall, in arms 2-4, 75 patients (65.8%) experienced a TEAE after exposure to concizumab.
The most common TEAEs which occurred at any timepoint in the study (main and extension
parts) were arthralgia (11.4%), injection site erythema (7.9%), upper respiratory tract infection
(7%), prothrombin fragment 1.2 increased (6.1%), fibrin d-dimer increased (5.3%), headache
(5.3%), COVID-19 (5.3%), nasopharyngitis (5.3%), and pyrexia (5.3%).

One patient was reported to have a seizure in arm 2 of the study. The patient is a male age 16 with a history of epilepsy who had a seizure event. The event is not considered related to concizumab
therapy. Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as AEs that occurred in the On-treatment observation period.
The On-treatment observation period was used for assessment of AEs and is defined as the time period where subjects are considered to be affected by on demand treatment or concizumab
treatment. Thus, for concizumab treated patients, On-treatment includes data before the treatment pause (including 7 weeks follow-up from the last dose of concizumab) as well as after concizumab
treatment restart.
Duration is at least 24 weeks (arm 1) or 32 weeks (arms 2-4) for the main part, 128-136 weeks for the extension part, and 7 weeks for the follow-up part of the study.
Median analysis duration based on the data are 31.1 weeks (No PPX arm 1 Main), 45 weeks (Concizumab PPX arm 2 Main), 49.4 weeks (Arm 1 Extension), 55.3 weeks (Arm 2 Overall), 70.4 weeks
(arm 3 Overall), and 33.1 weeks (arm 4 Overall).
Overall includes the main, extension, and follow-up parts of the study. Arm 1 Extension includes the extension and follow-up parts of the study.
Coded as Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms.
Risk difference (with 95% confidence interval) is shown between treatment and comparator. An asterisk (*) indicates the 95% confidence interval excludes zero.
Arm 1 and arm 2 consist of patients previously treated on demand. Arm 3 consists of patients transferred from Phase 2 Study 4310. Arm 4 consists of patients previously treated on PPX or on
demand.
Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; CI, confidence interval; N, number of patients in treatment arm; n, number of patients with adverse event; PPX, prophylaxis

Concizumab

The table below shows the AEs by System Organ Class (SOC) and narrow FMQ. AEs by narrow
FMQ reported in at least three patients in the randomized portion of Study 4311 are local
administration reaction (arm 2 n = 6 (18%) patients compared to arm 1 n = 0 (0%) patients),
nasopharyngitis (arm 2 n = 3 (9%) patients compared to arm 1 n = 3 (16%) patients) and
hemorrhage (arm 2 n = 4 (12%) patients compared to arm 1 n = 2 (11%) patients).
The most common TEAEs (by narrow FMQ) at any timepoint after exposure to concizumab in
arms 2-4, were local administration reaction (23.7%), nasopharyngitis (14.9%), hemorrhage
(13.2%), viral infection (7%), erythema (7.9%), pyrexia (5.3%), arthritis (5.3%), headache
(5.3%), and rash (5.3%).

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Clinical Reviewer Comment: The most common TEAEs (>10%) in the main treatment period
and occurred in a higher proportion of patients in the concizumab arm compared to no PPX was
local administration reactions. The preferred terms encompassed under this FMQ were injection
site bruising, injection site erythema, injection site hemorrhage, injection site hematoma,
injection site reaction, injection site urticaria, injection site induration, injection site pain,
injection site pruritis, injection site rash, injection site swelling, and injection site hypoesthesia.
The most common TEAEs (>10%) at any timepoint (main and extension parts) after exposure to
concizumab were local administration reaction (23.7%), nasopharyngitis (14.9%), hemorrhage
(13.2%), and arthralgia (11.4%). Hemorrhage is not considered an adverse reaction as it is most
likely related to the underlying disease.
In total, 6% of patients had elevated prothrombin fragment and 5.3% had an elevated d-dimer in
arms 2-4. No patients in the arm 1 (no concizumab PPX) had elevated prothrombin fragment or
d-dimer. Elevated prothrombin fragment and d-dimer are associated with an increased risk of
thrombosis therefore, if approved, the product label should include a warning for elevated
prothrombin fragment and elevated d-dimer.
7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 4311
There were no significant clinical laboratory changes in serum albumin (Alb), creatinine (Cr),
alkaline phosphatase (AlkP), total bilirubin (Tbili), estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR,
Cockroft-Gault), reported in Study 4311. There were three patients reported with elevation in
serum AST, ALT (>3x, >5x and >10x ULN, respectively). However, no patients reported
elevations in serum Tbili in the randomized portion of the study. In the extension portion of the
study there were 3 patients who had reported >3x ULN serum Tbili, 1 patient with >5x ULN and
1 patient with >10x ULN. However, there were no patients who reported corresponding
significant changes in serum AST or ALT.

Concizumab

Reviewer Comment: There were three patients reported with elevation in serum AST and ALT
(>3x, >5x and >10x ULN, respectively) in the extension portion of the study. Of note, none of the
patients had concurrent elevation in bilirubin at the time of transaminitis. One of the three
patients reported with elevated hepatic enzyme elevations had ALT>5 UNL (519 U/L) and AST
>5 UNL (612 U/L) at Week 32 of concizumab therapy. The event was reported 1 day after the
patient had an AE of COVID-19 (the AE of COVID-19 was fatal), therefore it is unlikely the
elevated liver enzymes were related to concizumab. The remaining 2 cases appear to be
potentially related to concizumab therapy as no other etiology for liver enzyme elevation was
identified (no patient narratives provided).
In addition, in the extension portion of the study there were 3 patients who had reported >3x
ULN serum Tbili, 1 patient with >5x ULN and 1 patient with >10x ULN. An etiology for
hyperbilirubinemia was not identified (no patient narratives were provided). There were no
patients who reported corresponding significant changes in serum AST or ALT.
The elevated liver enzymes and hyperbilirubinemia is concerning, although with the exception of
the patient with the fatal event due to COVID-19, none of the events of liver enzyme increase of
bilirubin increase led to concizumab interruption or discontinuation. Further investigation is
required to assess the hepatotoxicity risk, including a request for patient narratives which
describes any concurrent illness and medications.
There were two patients in Study 4311 in arm 1 (ID#
) who did not have
clinical laboratory postbaseline laboratory data during the randomized portion of the study.
These patients were discontinued from the study for the following reasons.

discussing temporary closure of study due to the reported SAE of thromboembolism.

7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study
4311
The figure below shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver
injury (DILI). No potential cases met Hy’s Law criteria. One patient (as shown in Figure 14) had
evidence of cholestasis (maximum Tbili (3.95xULN), without elevated liver enzymes. This
patient had an elevated bilirubin at baseline.

7.6.1.8. Vital Signs’ Analyses, Study 4311
No clinically significant risk differences in vital signs were reported among patients in arm 1
compared to arm 2 in Study 4311. In the randomized portion of the study after 24 weeks the
median systolic/diastolic blood pressure was 119/77 mm Hg (range 102-191/62-118 mm Hg) in
arm 1 and 122/80 mm Hg (range 101-150/53-103 mm Hg) in Arm 2 in the randomized portion of
the study. The median systolic/diastolic change from baseline was 3/1 mm Hg (range -16-23/-922 mm Hg) in arm 1 and 2/1 mm Hg (range -26-33/-17-32 mm Hg) in arm 2. Overall, the median
heart rate was similar in both treatment arms after 24 weeks of therapy during the randomized
portion of the study, i.e., 73-76 beats/min (range 55-129 beats/min). Overall, the median change
in heart rate from baseline to Week 24 was -1 beats/min (range -29-50 beats/min) in both
treatment arms.
7.6.1.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study 4311
Overall, no significant differences in serious adverse events were reported between patients in
treatment arms 1 and 2 based on race. However, most patients (67 of 118 (57%) enrolled in
Study 4311 were white. Only 8 of 118 (7%) of patients enrolled in Study 4311 were black. There
were 3 of 118 (3%) patients enrolled in Study 4311 were American Indian or Alaska Native.
There were 5 of 118 (4%) of patients who did not have a race reported in Study 4311.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Study 4310
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Study 4310
In Study 4310, there were 17 patients who were treated with concizumab PPX and 9 patients
who were randomized to the no PPX arm. In total, there was 1 of 17 (6%) patients and 3 of 9
(33%) patients in the concizumab PPX and no PXX arms, respectively, who had a SAE reported.
Overall, 13 of 17 (76%) patients in the concizumab PPX arm and 8 of 9 (89%) patients in the No
PPX arm who had any AE reported. Most AEs were mild.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Study 4310
No deaths were reported in Study 4310.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study
4310
One SAE was reported among patients in the concizumab PPX arm, i.e., requirement for removal
of a central venous catheter. SAEs reported in the no PPX arm included anemia, congestive
cardiomyopathy and hordeolum in the randomized portion of the study, as shown in the table
below.
In patients exposed to concizumab at any time (randomized and extension periods), 20% of
patients experienced an SAE. Of note, the same patient experienced SAEs of hemorrhagic shock
(after femoral artery puncture), muscle hemorrhage (after fall), puncture site hemorrhage, and
subdural hemorrhage (after fall), the patient narrative is below.
