Central venous catheter removal was due to line infection. Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as Treatment-emergent
adverse events defined as adverse events with onset from randomization and until the last planned visit in the study (i.e., end of the
post-treatment follow-up period 8 weeks after the last dose of concizumab).
Serious adverse events defined as any untoward medical occurrence that, at any dose that results in death, is life-threatening,
requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization, results in persistent incapacity or substantial disruption of the
ability to conduct normal life functions, or is a congenital anomaly or birth defect.
Duration is 24 weeks (Main part), 52 weeks (Extension part), and 8 weeks (Follow-up part).
Overall includes the main, extension, and follow-up parts of the study.
The Concizumab PPX Main and No PPX Main study arms contain patients who were randomized. The Concizumab PPX Overall
study arm contains patients from the Concizumab PPX Main study arms and patients from the No PPX Main study arms after they
switched to Concizumab PPX at the start of the extension part of the study (excluding one patient from the No PPX Main study arm
who discontinued before the end of the main part of the study).
Abbreviations: HAwI, hemophilia A with inhibitors; HBwI, hemophilia B with inhibitors; ISS, integrated summary of safety; N, number
of patients in treatment arm; n, number of patients with adverse event; PPX, prophylaxis; SOC, system organ class

Patient
was a 34-year-old male with HBwI and baseline ABR of 45. He was receiving
concizumab at 0.15 mg/kg. The patient had an accident at Week 32 resulting in bone fractures
and required surgery. Concizumab was stopped and the patient developed hemorrhagic shock a
week later after a femoral artery puncture. Concizumab was restarted at Week 40. The patient
developed a medium ADA titer with neutralizing antibodies, and the TFPI level returned to
baseline. The ADA titer further increased to very high levels starting at Week 48. Per the
Applicant, the patient’s “bleeding pattern” did not change. The patient also had worsening liver
function which was a pre-existing condition due to alcoholic fatty liver disease. At Week 72 the
patient fell and developed a muscle and subdural hemorrhage. The patient was hospitalized and
treatment was discontinued due to lack of therapeutic effect due to neutralizing antibody.
(b) (6)

Clinical Reviewer Comment: One SAE occurred during the randomized treatment period, which
was central line removal. This was an elective procedure and not related to concizumab
treatment.
One patient had multiple SAEs related to hemorrhage. While each AE was due to a traumatic
event, the lack of therapeutic effect of concizumab due to neutralizing antibodies likely
contributed. If concizumab is approved, the risk of neutralizing antibodies should be noted in
the product label. See Section 14.4 for further details regarding immunogenicity.
7.6.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to
Treatment Discontinuation, Study 4310
No adverse events (AEs) leading to treatment discontinuation were reported in Study 4310.

7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study 4310
The treatment-emergent adverse events reported in Study 4310 are shown in the table below. In
the main treatment period, the most common TEAEs in the concizumab PPX arm were injection
site hematoma (17.6%), nasopharyngitis (17.6%), headache (11.8%) and prothrombin level
increased (11.8%). The most common TEAEs in the no PPX arm was contusion (22.2%).
The most common TEAEs (>10%) in patients exposed to concizumab in the main and extension
periods of the study included diarrhea (16%), upper respiratory tract infection (16%), arthralgia
(12%), and injection site hematoma (12%).

The most common TEAEs by FMQ (>15%) which occurred in a higher proportion of patients in
the concizumab PPX arm compared to the no PPX arm were nasopharyngitis (29% versus
11.1%), local administration reaction (23.5% versus 0%), and hemorrhage (23.5% versus
22.2%), respectively.
In the main and extension parts, the most common TEAEs (>10%) were nasopharyngitis (32%),
hemorrhage (32%), local administration reaction (24%), bacterial infection (16%), diarrhea
(16%), anemia (12%), viral infection (12%), and arthralgia (12%).

7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study 4310
No clinically relevant differences in clinical laboratory testing results (i.e., serum albumin, serum
creatinine, eGFR, alkaline phosphatase, ALT, AST, and total bilirubin, respectively, were
observed between treatment arms in Study 4310, as shown in the tables below. A higher
proportion of patients reported lymphopenia (<1000 cells/µL) in the concizumab PPX arm (3 of
17 (18% patients)) compared to the No PPX arm (0 of 9 (0%) patients)) in Study 4310, as shown
below. Overall, there were 6 patients with mild (>4000 cells/µL) increase. The mild changes
lymphocyte counts or other minor blood cell count changes during concizumab therapy are of
unclear clinical significance.

7.6.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Risk of Thrombosis
Issue Thrombosis
Three patients experienced non-fatal thrombotic events while receiving concizumab in clinical
studies. The clinical reviewer assessed the safety data surrounding these events.
Background
Concizumab acts by enhancing the initiation phase of coagulation through increased activated
coagulation factor X (Fxa) production, allowing sufficient thrombin generation and thus
formation of clots. As a pro-coagulant, there is a risk of thrombosis with exaggerated
pharmacology. Thrombosis formation was observed in non-clinical studies.
In clinical trials, three patients experienced thrombotic TEAEs while receiving concizumab. The
clinical development program was subsequently put on a full clinical hold due to the potential for
increased risk of thrombosis among patients treated with concizumab. The full clinical hold was
removed after risk mitigation strategies were put in place as described below.
•

Included a new guidance for treatment of breakthrough bleeds, with specific guidance for use
of the lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab
prophylaxis (PPX).

•

Patients must contact the site when they have a suspected bleed.

•

Continuation of previous PPX for up to two weeks after initiation of concizumab dosing was
no longer allowed (Study 4307).

•

A new concizumab dosing regimen including an initial daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg concizumab
(instead of 0.25 mg/kg) was put in place. Criteria for an increase or decrease in the daily
maintenance dose to 0.25 mg/kg or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively (based on concizumab exposure
levels at the Week 4 visit 200 ng/mL-4000 ng/mL) were provided. The loading dose
remained 1.0 mg/kg.

•

Elective major surgery was no longer allowed in clinical studies.

•

Trial stopping rule requiring urgent evaluation by the Novo Nordisk Safety Committee and
consultation with the data monitoring committee (DMC) in case of one (instead of two)
114

[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]

BLA 761315

Concizumab

significant thromboembolic event, disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC), thrombotic
microangiopathy (TMA) or death of study patient which may be related to the study product
put in place.
Assessment
A summary of the three patients with thrombotic events while receiving concizumab is described
in the table below. It is notable that both arterial and venous thrombotic events were reported
21 to 86 days after starting concizumab. In addition, all patients had additional risk factors for
thrombosis including smoking, history of hypertension (HTN) and/or obesity. Two of the three
patients had elevated concizumab levels >4000ng/mL. Concizumab levels for each patient is
described in the figure below. Lastly, all patients had received factor or a bypassing agent at the
time of the thrombotic event. No patient had ADAs at the time of the event.

One additional patient (Patient
) age 42 years male with HAwI was treated with
concizumab PPX at 0.25 mg/kg in the main part of the study prior to the treatment pause. Due to

from the study on study Day 364. On study Day 106 (15 days after discontinuation of
concizumab PPX), the patient had a non-serious event of infection in his left forearm following a
shunt puncture. An ultrasound showed partial shunt stenosis. The patient had started treatment
with eptacog alfa (activated) on study Day 104 (2 days prior to the event of shunt thrombosis)
and initiated treatment with emicizumab (240 mg SC once weekly) on study Day 106 after which
(b) (6)
a complete shunt stenosis was reported. The thrombotic event for patient
is not clearly
related to concizumab therapy due to infection and treatment with additional coagulant factor
and emicizumab.
(b) (6)

After the clinical hold was removed and clinical studies were restarted, there were no thrombotic
events.
Conclusion
Patients exposed to supratherapeutic doses of concizumab are at increased risk of thrombosis
likely due to exaggerated pharmacologic effect. While the sample size is small, this risk appears
to be increased in the setting of additional thrombotic risk factors, receiving concomitant factor

Concizumab

or bypassing agent, and concizumab levels >4000ng/mL. The risk for thrombosis among patients
undergoing concizumab therapy appears to have decreased once the Applicant included PK
monitoring in the clinical development program, providing specific guidance for use of the
lowest labeled dose of factor product or bypassing agent while on concizumab prophylaxis
(PPX), and a new concizumab dosing regimen which targets concizumab levels <4000ng/mL. If
the product were to be approved, thromboembolic events should be included in the Warnings and
Precautions section of the USPI. The new dosing regimen used after the treatment pause should
be included in the USPI along with a statement to use the lowest effective dose of factor or
bypassing agent to decrease the risk of thrombotic events. The frequency of PK monitoring is
still unresolved at the time of this review and will be deferred to the next review cycle.

7.7.2. Pharmacokinetic Fluctuations Leading to
Subtherapeutic Concentrations During
Concizumab Treatment
Issue: PK Fluctuations Leading to Subtherapeutic Concentrations During Concizumab
Treatment
Due to PK fluctuations, there were patients with concizumab levels <200ng/mL. Patients with
concizumab levels <200 ng/mL are at increased risk of bleeding.
Background
Bleeding is inextricably linked with hemophilia and is commonly observed among the subset of
patients with inhibitors. A concern for concizumab is the potential increase for bleeding if
patients do not maintain the recommended concizumab plasma concentration of 200 ng/mL4000 ng/ mL, in particular a level over 200ng/mL (see Section 6.1 for further details). Significant
fluctuations in plasma PK profiles were observed in individual patients. The review team
(clinical pharmacology and clinical) investigated why PK fluctuations occurred and the potential
impact on bleeding.
Assessment
The review team noted that 42 out of 97 patients (43%) had concizumab exposure levels below
200 ng/mL at some point during the maintenance dose setting and up until primary analysis cutoff (PACO). In total, 7 out of 42 patients (16.7%) with subtherapeutic levels of concizumab
(<200ng/mL) had 11 AEs of bleeding in Study 4311. Nine out of 11 bleeding adverse events
appear to be related to injection site reactions and were mild in severity. The remaining 2 events,
occurring simultaneously in 1 patient, were severe and serious events related to muscle
hemorrhage. No other bleeding episodes were reported for this patient. An additional patient
reported in the 120-day safety update had a fatal bleeding event with a subtherapeutic level
(28.1ng/mL) of concizumab. This patient is discussed further in Section 7.7.3.
A total of 16 patients out of the 42 patients with concizumab exposure levels below 200 ng/mL
at some point in time from maintenance dose to the end of data closure tested positive for
binding ADAs. Most occurrences were transient, and titers were very low. Three patients had in
119
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

vitro neutralizing antibodies at 1 or a few visits (Subjects
);
however, binding titers were very low, and no clinical impact of ADAs was observed in these
patients. Based on further analysis (see Section 14.4) immunogenicity does not appear to have
impacted PK.
(b) (6)

The Applicant believes the significant PK variability observed in some patients was due to
TMDD. The review team believes this certainly contributed to PK fluctuations but could not
solely explain the large PK fluctuations. As described in detail in Section 7.2, the most plausible
explanation is the issue of compliance. Study 4159 demonstrated subtherapeutic concizumab
levels within a few days of missed doses. In addition, when concentrations drop to low
subtherapeutic levels due to missed doses, in the absence of a loading dose, it may take several
days of consecutive dosing to gradually return to target concentrations of >200 ng/mL.
Conclusion
While most bleeds that occurred when patients had subtherapeutic concizumab levels were
minor, the potential remains for a moderate or severe or fatal bleeding events as observed in
clinical studies. Outside of a controlled clinical study, risk of poor compliance to concizumab
PPX will be greater and could pose a serious risk for the patient by increasing the potential for
serious and fatal bleeding events. Given that a concizumab concentration above 200ng/mL is key
to the therapeutic effect, the Applicant needs to provide a plan for a missed dose or multiple
missed doses in labeling. This may include a plan for additional concizumab laboratory
monitoring and/or a loading dose. In addition, given patients can become subtherapeutic quickly
with missed doses, the Applicant should include a Medication Guide and address the need for
good patient education and compliance with treatment.

7.7.3. Patient Fatality Due to Bleeding and
Subtherapeutic Concizumab Levels
Issue: Fatal Bleeding Event With Subtherapeutic Levels
A 13-year-old male with HBwI had a fatal intracranial hemorrhage while receiving concizumab
PPX.
Background
Patient
is summarized in Section 7.6.1.2 Deaths, Study 4311 in this review. Briefly, this
patient was a male age 13 years with HBwI who developed a fatal intracranial bleed after 345
days of concizumab treatment. The patient had subtherapeutic concizumab levels at the time of
the fatal event.
(b) (6)

Assessment
The etiology of the fatal intracranial hemorrhage was investigated by the clinical reviewer..
Based on the eDiary, the patient had multiple periods of 4 or more missed doses and missed
doses corresponded to the subtherapeutic concizumab levels at visit 9a and 11a, see Table 30
during treatment.
120
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

The patient did not have ADAs near the time of the fatal event, so it is not likely that this
contributed. During visits in which the patient received concizumab at the study site enhanced
PK monitoring was conducted. The patient was therapeutic at those visits and there was inverse
relationship between concizumab exposure and free TFPI levels over time.
Given the information gathered by the FDA reviewer the most likely explanation for the
subtherapeutic concizumab level at the time of the fatal intracranial hemorrhage was noncompliance, as the patient did have therapeutic levels when administered concizumab on site and
eDiary had documentation of missing doses which resulted in a protocol violation. No other
patients had fatal bleeding events while utilizing concizumab.
Conclusion
In summary, patient
was likely non-compliant with concizumab, and therefore was
subtherapeutic and vulnerable to fatal bleeding. This case highlights the need for strict
compliance, which can be difficult with a daily medication. Please refer to Section 7.7.2
regarding recommendations to improve patient compliance.
(b) (6)

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
The PK of concizumab is not influenced by age (12 to 79 years), race (White 62.9%, Asian
25.0%, Black 6.0%), hemophilia type (A versus B). Concizumab exposures are generally higher
in healthy subjects compared to patients with hemophilia. The apparent clearance and volume of
distribution of concizumab increased with increasing body weight (27 kg to 130.7 kg). See figure
below for concizumab trough plasma concentration as a function of body weight following
administration of concizumab 0.2 mg/kg. Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment, patients with
low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively, resulting in more
similar exposure across patients.
No dedicated studies have been conducted to evaluate the impact of renal or hepatic impairment.
As concizumab is a monoclonal antibody, there is no expectation for concizumab exposures to
be different in patients with renal or hepatic impairment.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
No clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) study was conducted with concizumab. Considering
concizumab is a monoclonal antibody, the likelihood of PK-based drug interaction is low.
In vitro and ex vivo DDI studies were performed with factor products or bypassing agents
including rFVIIa, activated prothrombin complex concentrate (aPCC), rFVIII or rFIX in plasma
122
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

from hemophilia patients who were on prophylactic treatment with concizumab. The studies
show that thrombin peak is increased in an additive and sometimes synergistic manner with
concomitant factor product or bypass agents, indicating pharmacodynamic interaction. While
these factor products or bypassing agents can be used in cases of breakthrough bleeds, the
following dosing strategies should be implemented as was done in the Phase 3 study by the
Applicant:
•

Discontinue treatment with rFVIIa at least 12 hours before starting concizumab

•

Discontinue treatment with aPCC at least 48 hours before starting concizumab

•

Healthcare providers should discuss with patients receiving concizumab and/or their
caregivers the dose and schedule of bypassing agents, if required, while receiving
concizumab prophylaxis
“Treatment with bypassing agents (e.g., rFVIIa or aPCC) can be used for
breakthrough bleeds, and the dose and duration will depend on the location
and severity of the bleed. For mild and moderate bleeds that require additional
treatment with bypassing agents (e.g., rFVIIa or aPCC), the lowest-approved
dose in the approved product labeling is recommended. For aPCC, a
maximum dose of 100 U/kg body weight within 24 hours is recommended. For
severe bleeds, follow the dosing instructions provided in the approved labeling
for the specific product based on clinical judgement.”

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Animal Data
Dedicated juvenile animal studies have not been conducted with concizumab as studies in
neonatal and infant monkeys are technically challenging and are unlikely to identify potential
reactions that were not identified from studies in fully developed (adult) animals. Notable
findings in the hemostatic system and immune-related vascular toxicity were observed in
adolescent (2 to 3 years of age) and sexually mature (3.5 to 6 years of age) cynomolgus
monkeys. The human hemostatic system matures during the early weeks and months of life, and
the concentrations of most hemostatic proteins, both In full-term and preterm infants, are
comparable to adult values by 6 months of age (Albisetti 2003; Chalmers 2004).
Clinical Data
Clinical Study 4311, 42 pediatric patients ages 12 to <18 years old, with HAwI or HBwI, were
exposed to concizumab either in the main or extension treatment periods. Concizumab has been
provided for compassionate use (expanded access) to a total of 14 children and adolescents ages
1 month to <17 years with HBwI up to the cut-off date for the compassionate use report (10
March 2022). Exposure from the compassionate use program has ranged from 1 month to 3
(b) (4)
years.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Animal Data
The following nonclinical information was used in support of the indicated labeling sections and
final labeling discussed in Section 23.
Table 52. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Pregnancy and Lactation
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
Pregnancy
Dedicated reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were not
conducted as concizumab is primarily indicated for use in male patients with
hemophilia and the clinical development program was conducted exclusively in
males.
Hemophilia A is inherited in an X-linked recessive manner and the disease
which occurs predominantly in males (99%) is extremely rare in females.
Concizumab is also being indicated for hemophilia B (Factor IX deficiency or
Christmas disease, caused by mutations in the Factor IX (FIX) gene on the X
chromosome) and hence, is predominantly observed in males (some females
who carry the gene may display mild or, rarely, severe symptoms of bleeding).
8.3 Females and
Histopathological evaluations conducted during repeat-dose toxicology studies
Males of
in adolescent (up to 13 weeks in duration) and sexually mature (up to 52
Reproductive
weeks in duration) male and female cynomolgus monkeys did not elicit any
Potential
notable effects in reproductive organs.
In mice, inactivation of the TFPI-gene has been shown to result in embryonic
lethality (Huang et al. 1997; Ellery et al. 2015) and total TFPI deficiency has
not been described in humans (Wood et al. 2014). As placental transfer of IgG,
particularly during organogenesis, may differ between mice and humans, the
clinical relevance of these findings is unknown (DeSesso et al. 2012).
Recurrent pregnancy loss has been observed in women who exhibited TFPI
deficiency in combination with an Activated Protein C (APC) resistance
phenotype or in women with anti-TFPI antibodies in combination with
antiphospholipid antibodies and suggests that TFPI may be critical to the
maintenance of normal pregnancy in humans (Martinuzzo et al. 2005;
Gardiner et al. 2006).
The potential risk of blocking TFPI with a monoclonal IgG antibody (e.g.,
concizumab) in pregnant women is not clear.

13.1 Carcinogenesis,
Mutagenesis,
Impairment of Fertility

Concizumab is not functional in rodents and standard carcinogenicity studies
have not been conducted. Instead, the Applicant conducted a carcinogenicity
risk assessment of concizumab using a weight-of-evidence (WOE) approach
that considered the mode of action (MOA), a review of relevant literature and
an evaluation of the nonclinical and clinical data. It was concluded that
treatment with concizumab has the potential to enhance the prothrombotic
conditions observed in some cancer patients (as would be expected for any
procoagulant compound). However, the assessment did not identify evidence
that treatment with concizumab could definitively lead to an increased risk of
inducing or enhancing tumor development in humans. The Division determined
that no additional nonclinical studies assessing the carcinogenic potential of
concizumab were warranted for the indications of hemophilia A and hemophilia
B and the CDER Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC)
concurred with the Division’s recommendation.
Genotoxicity studies were not conducted, as concizumab consists of naturally
occurring amino acids (endogenous) and is not anticipated to be genotoxic
and there were no mechanistic data that suggested a mutagenic potential for
concizumab.
Fertility evaluations were included in the 26-week chronic toxicity study
conducted in sexually mature male and female cynomolgus monkeys and
there were no notable effects on the menstrual cycle duration, the length,
width and volume of the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility or sperm
morphology in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at doses up to
9 mg/kg/day (SC).
Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of reproductive toxicology studies submitted to the BLA.
Abbreviations: APC, Activated Protein C; ECAC, Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee; FIX, Factor IX; IgG,
immunoglobulin G; MOA, mode of action; SC, subcutaneous; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor; WOE, weight-of-evidence.

8.5. Key Efficacy Review Issues
8.5.1. Determination If the Pause in Study 4311 Due
to Thrombotic Events Impacted the Benefit
(ABR)
Issue
Determination if the pause in Study 4311 due to thrombotic events impacted the benefit (ABR).
Background
Study 4311 had a concizumab treatment pause for 6 months due to thromboembolic events
reported in 3 patients. After the concizumab treatment pause and subsequent restart of Study
4311, a new concizumab dosing regimen was introduced to concizumab PPX arms. The original
randomization could have been compromised because more than half of the planned sample size
(refer to Table 54) were already enrolled and treated on the original regimen. Four patients
randomized before the study pause in the concizumab PPX arm 2 did not restart on the new
concizumab dosing regimen.

Assessment
As recommended in previous IND meetings, the impact of potential bias introduced by the
treatment pause and the robustness of the primary analysis was investigated by the Applicant
with four sensitivity analyses. The results from all four sensitivity analyses in Table 55 were
confirmed by the FDA statistical reviewer. It was determined that the results of superiority from
the primary analysis are robust.

Conclusion
Study 4311 was paused due to thromboembolic events. However, multiple sensitivity analysis
confirmed the conclusion of superiority of concizumab PPX over on-demand therapy in
reduction in ABR. Despite the study pause, Study 4311 is determined to be an adequate and well
controlled study and provides substantial evidence of effectiveness of concizumab for routine
prophylaxis to prevent or reduce the frequency of bleeding episodes in adult and adolescent
patients with HAwI and HBwI.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
13.1.1. Concizumab Pharmacology
Concizumab binds to an epitope in the K2 domain of tissue factor pathway inhibitor (TFPI) that
overlaps with the interface of Fxa binding. The results of in vitro and in vivo nonclinical
pharmacology studies are consistent with the proposed mechanism of action of TFPI
neutralization, whereby the Fxa produced by the activated coagulation factor VII (FVIIa)/tissue
factor (TF) complex (normally downregulated by TFPI activity) results in increased thrombin
generation leading to increased clot formation and theoretically hemostasis.
The rabbit and cynomolgus monkey were identified as relevant nonclinical species. No other
nonclinical species were identified that expressed Kunitz domain sequences that contained the
amino acid residues in the epitope of concizumab on the human K2 domain. The cynomolgus
monkey and rabbit K2 domains have a 96% and 93% sequence homology to the human K2
domain, respectively.
The binding constants (KD) of concizumab to rabbit, cynomolgus monkey and human TFPI are
0.22nM, 0.06nM, and 0.04nM, respectively (determined by surface plasmon resonance, SPR).
The TFPI association constants (ka) for concizumab were 1.3x106, 4.2x106, and 1.7x106 (M-1×s-1)
and the TFPI disassociation constants (kd) for concizumab were 2.8x106, 2.3x106, and 6.3x106 (s1
) in rabbits, cynomolgus monkeys and humans, respectively. These data suggest that
concizumab binds to TFPI with high affinity and to a comparable extent in humans, cynomolgus
monkeys and rabbits. Functional cross-reactivity was evaluated in a modified prothrombin time
(PT) clot assay and concizumab was found to be functionally active against TFPI in plasmas
derived from rabbits, cynomolgus monkeys, healthy humans, and FVIII-deficient humans (half
maximal effective concentration (EC50) values were 0.49nM, 0.41nM, 0.56nM, and 0.62nM,
respectively).

elicit hemostatic effects in patients with hemophilia A.

The thromboelastography results indicate that concizumab has a
pro-hemostatic effect in (male) human blood (in vitro) and
suggests that concizumab may elicit similar hemostatic effects in
patients with hemophilia A or hemophilia B.
Concizumab elicited a comparable level (versus humans) of
hemostatic activity with regards to reduced clotting times (0.4nM,
EC50) and improved clot development (0.5nM, EC50) in blood
derived from healthy male cynomolgus monkeys that was
treated (ex vivo) with a neutralizing anti-FVIII antibody to mimic
the hemophilia A condition.
The thromboelastography results indicate that concizumab has a
pro-hemostatic effect in (male) cynomolgus monkey blood (in
vitro) and supports the premise that the cynomolgus monkey is a
pharmacologically relevant animal species.

assay buffer and components

Concizumab neutralized TFPI activity (the inhibition of the
catalytic activity of Fxa) in a concentration dependent manner
(EC50, 6nM) in pooled human plasma (10-fold diluted samples).
Note: As dilution linearity was not evaluated, the EC50 could vary
if other dilution factors are employed.
The EC50 values for concizumab inhibition of TFPI activity were
estimated using FVIII-inhibited rabbit plasma (5.8nM) and
monkey plasma (1.6nM) and were comparable to the human
EC50 value. The optimal dilution factors with respect to the best
Fxa response were found to be a 10-fold dilution for rabbit
samples and a 5-fold dilution for monkey samples in human
TFPI depleted plasma. Dilution linearity was not observed with
either rabbit plasma or monkey plasma and as the EC50s varied
widely when applying different dilution factors in these species
similar variability would be expected with human samples.

pharmacokinetic profile indicated that plasma concizumab levels
(40 µg/mL) were relatively stable in rabbits over the 1-hour
observation period and corresponded to the observed
pharmacodynamic activity.

Study No.: PJOH090802
Study Title: Acute Effects of (IV)
Concizumab (NNC 0172-0000-2021)
a humanized anti-TFPI antibody on
Local Clot Formation in a Venous
Stasis Model in Female NZW Rabbits
Following Vessel Wall Injury and
Prevention with a Low Molecular
Weight Heparin (LMWH).
Species: NZW Rabbit (Female)
Dose: 2 mg/kg (IV) Concizumab
Dose: 500 IU/kg (IV) LMWH

Prophylactic treatment with concizumab significantly increased
local clot formation in healthy female NZW rabbits when facial
veins were crushed and then ligated 30 minutes later.
When concizumab dosing was followed by (5 minutes later) the
administration of LMWH (before injury) local clot formation was
prevented.
No changes in coagulation parameters were observed (platelet
counts, WBC counts, PT, and fibrinogen). A minimal decrease in
APTT was observed after concizumab (only) dosing.
These results indicate that LMWH can prevent the procoagulant
effects of concizumab in non-hemophilic rabbits and suggest that
LMWH may be used as a preventive treatment option if
complications caused by exaggerated pharmacology arise.

Control: Saline buffer with isotype
control (IgG) antibody (2 mg/kg)

13.1.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology studies were conducted as part of the pivotal 13-week toxicity study with
an 11-week recovery in cynomolgus monkeys (See Study 209206 for a summarized report of
findings in Section 13.1.2.1). In brief, neurobehavioral observations which included extended
clinical signs (CNS endpoints), respiratory evaluations (visual appraisal of respiratory rate/depth)
and a cytokine release assessment (IL-2, IL-4, IL-5, IL-6, TNF-α, IFN-γ) were all unremarkable.
Cardiovascular assessments (blood pressure, heart rates, electrocardiogram (ECG) intervals, and
the morphology of the ECG waveform) and urinalysis were conducted during the pivotal 13week and 52-week repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys, and no effects of
concizumab were observed up to the highest doses assessed (200 mg/kg/QW, intravenous (IV)
and 50 mg/kg/day, SC).
Safety pharmacology assessments conducted in vitro consisted of human Fc receptor binding,
antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC), complement fixation and binding to
peripheral blood cells and are described in detail below.

Findings
Flow cytometric analysis revealed that concizumab does not
significantly bind to human peripheral blood cell subsets
(granulocytes, monocytes, lymphocytes, thrombocytes, or
erythrocytes) that do not express significant levels of TFPI on
their cell surface.
These findings are consistent with the results that showed that
concizumab selectively binds to the TFPI expressing endothelial
cell line (EAHy926WT) but not to the TFPI negative cell line
(EAHy926AR).

Procoagulant effects were not observed when concizumab was

Due to the nature and design of these rabbit studies, it is not
possible to fully exclude an additive or synergistic effect of rFVIIa
and concizumab and precautions should be taken when patients
receiving concizumab prophylaxis experience break-through
bleeding events and require treatment with rFVIIa (or another
bypassing agent).

Key Study Findings:
•

Administration of rFVIIa (IV) to cynomolgus monkeys at doses ≤1 mg/kg (three times with

concizumab was well tolerated, with no dose-related pathological findings (e.g., no thrombi)

Concizumab and rFVIIa were pharmacologically active in cynomolgus monkeys as indicated
by the observed reduction in plasma fibrinogen, free TFPI, and residual TFPI functionality
and the shortening of prothrombin times (PT), respectively.

increase in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab or rFVIIa alone and the effects of

Parameters

Major Findings
(RBC Parameter): Erythroid parameters (hemoglobin, red blood
cells, packed cell volumes and mean cell volumes) tended to
decline with repeat blood sampling (Day 28 and Day 29) and
mean values on Day 26 were similar to pretreatment. The decline
in RBC parameters was considered driven by the frequent blood
sampling (not test article related).
(WBC Parameters): A similar decline was observed in platelet
counts in all dosing groups (including controls).

Coagulation

Prothrombin Time (PT) and Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time
(APTT): There were no notable changes in PT and APTT in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (only) for
4 weeks at 1 mg/kg/day (SC). A shortening of PT was observed in
both sexes at 2 hours after the third dose of rFVIIa (only) and
rebounded thereafter. Prothrombin Time (independent of dose
level of rFVIIa) was shortened in both sexes in the presence of
concizumab at 2 hours after the first rFVIIa administration and
persisted after the third administration at the HD (1 mg/kg) only.
Prothrombin time reductions at the HD (1 mg/kg, rFVIIa) in the
presence of steady state concizumab were comparable to those
seen with rFVIIa only and normalized on Day 29 (slightly
exceeding baseline values).
Fibrinogen: A reduction in fibrinogen was present in monkeys
administered concizumab for 4 weeks at 1 mg/kg/day (SC).
(Thrombin Antithrombin (TAT) Complex): Thrombin antithrombin
complex is a protein complex of thrombin and antithrombin and is
a marker of net activation of coagulation. Thrombin antithrombin
complex (TAT) concentrations (in general) increased in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (only) for
4 weeks at 1 mg/kg/day (SC). On Day 28, after treatment with
rFVIIa (2-hrs after first and third administrations) in the presence
of concizumab the average TAT concentration was increased
further (dose-dependently) and indicated an additive effect.

decreased in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab
from Day 2 forward (often below the lower limit of quantification),
particularly in males. The residual TFPI functionality was not
altered significantly by rFVIIa treatment.

Parameters

Major Findings
(Protein C and Protein S): Plasma protein C/S concentrations

Clinical chemistry

There were no notable changes in clinical chemistry parameters in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4 weeks at
1 mg/kg/day (SC) followed by 3 doses of rFVIIa at 2-hour intervals
of 0.25, 0.5 or 1 mg/kg (Day 28 Only, IV Bolus).
Low levels of ADAs (close to the assay cut point), were detected
at pre-treatment (#109 and #9) without explanation and on Day 29
(#9). Anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) detected in male #8 on Day 29,
were deemed related to concizumab dosing and the Optical
Density (OD) value in the ADA assay in male #8 exceeded the
assay cut point by 10-fold. No ADAs were detected in the other
cynomolgus monkeys in this study however the negative results
may be due to interference from high concentrations of
concizumab in plasma samples (leading to false negative results).
(Concizumab)

Anti-drug antibody (ADA) analysis

Toxicokinetics

The plasma exposure to concizumab tended to be lower in
females than in males and the median tmax was 24 hours (Day 1)
and ranged from 4 hours to 6 hours on Day 27. Concizumab
exposures were reduced in one male (#8) that developed ADAs.
(rFVIIa)
The mean terminal half-life ranged from 1.4 hours to 1.8 hours
and there was no apparent difference in exposure to rFVIIa
between the sexes. The FVIIa clot activity increased with
ascending dose of rFVIIa (dose-proportional). There was no
apparent difference in the toxicokinetics of 1 mg/kg rFVIIa
administered in the presence or absence of steady state
concizumab.
Gross pathology

Organ weights

Subcutaneous and intravenous injection sites (saphenous vein)
and sampling sites (foreleg and inguinal areas) were found to be
red and corresponded with the microscopic finding of hemorrhage
at these sites. These findings were considered consistent with the
injection procedures and venipuncture.
Organ weight changes in the testis, epididymis, prostate, seminal
vesicles, ovary, and uterus were deemed to reflect the different
stages of sexual maturity in the adolescent cynomolgus monkeys
used on study rather than a direct effect of concizumab.

13.1.1.4. ADME/Pharmacokinetics
Overall, the nonclinical data characterizing the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and
elimination of concizumab supported to use of the selected nonclinical species (New Zealand
White (NZW) rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys), allowed for interspecies exposure comparisons
and provided relevant information to determine clinical dose selection.
Absorption
The pharmacokinetics of concizumab was evaluated in NZW rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys
after single intravenous (IV) and subcutaneous (SC) administration. Repeat-dose
pharmacokinetics and toxicokinetics were assessed in cynomolgus monkeys.

The clearance and volume of distribution of concizumab decreased with increasing (IV) dose in

150
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

notable differences in the single dose pharmacokinetics between the sexes in cynomolgus
monkeys (210156).
Lower doses of concizumab were cleared more rapidly from the circulation in cynomolgus
monkeys, while at higher doses the plasma concentration profile became flat until a
concentration of 10-20μg/mL, at which the elimination rate increased. This pattern is consistent
with target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD) where a compound is cleared from the circulation
when binding to its target (typically a cell-bound target) in this case most likely the TFPI bound
to the surface of endothelial cells. At higher concentrations, the TMDD of concizumab is
saturated, resulting in a slower clearance and a flat plasma concentration profile with a half-life
of 200 hours in the cynomolgus monkey, which is consistent with the pharmacokinetic
characteristics of monoclonal antibodies. At lower concentrations, the target-mediated clearance
of concizumab accelerates and plasma concentrations decrease rapidly.
Exposure increased with ascending dose level in NZW rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys when
concizumab was administered as a single subcutaneous (SC) dose. The tmax increased with
ascending dose from 36 hours (2 mg/kg) to 120 hours (20 mg/kg) in NZW rabbits and supports
the premise of TMDD. Bioavailability was found to be 80% in NZW rabbits and cynomolgus
monkeys.

Repeat-Dose Toxicokinetics
The toxicokinetics of concizumab was assessed as a part of a Dose Range Finding (DRF) study
(SC) and the five repeat-dose toxicity studies (SC/IV) of 4, 13, 26, or 52 weeks in duration
conducted in cynomolgus monkeys at dose levels ranging from 0.5 mg/kg to 200 mg/kg. Daily
administration (SC) of concizumab was employed in repeat-dose toxicology studies in an attempt
to overcome the effects of TMDD (particularly at low doses) and maintain target (TFPI)
saturation.

Distribution
No dedicated distribution studies were conducted with concizumab. The distribution of
concizumab was evaluated by immunohistochemistry in rabbits (Hansen et al. 2014), tissue
cross-reactivity studies (human and monkey), and immunohistochemistry in cynomolgus
monkeys. In general, concizumab was found to be present in the blood and associated with the
vascular endothelium, with only minor differences between humans and cynomolgus monkeys.
Pilot Tissue Cross-Reactivity (TCR) Study of Concizumab with Limited Normal Human
and Cynomolgus Monkey Tissues (Study 209125, Non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

The concizumab staining pattern was (in general) similar between the human and
cynomolgus monkey tissues examined and corresponded to known sites of TFPI tissue
expression.

•

The noted differences observed between humans and cynomolgus monkeys with regards to
concizumab tissue staining patterns (e.g., cynomolgus monkey islet cells and acinar
epithelium in the pancreas) may represent individual variations and/or differences in age,
disease status, artifacts of the process of dying, or true species differences. As these sites in
the pancreas are not known to express TFPI the observed staining in these tissue elements
might represent either previously unrecognized sites of TFPI expression or true tissue crossreactivity.

Reviewer Comments: Tissue cross-reactivity of concizumab was similar between tissue
cryosections derived from healthy humans and cynomolgus monkeys.

152
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Study Features and Methods
Methods

Details
Concizumab was used to stain cryosections of limited
normal human and cynomolgus monkey tissues
(3 donors per tissue) at concentrations of 1 μg/mL or
10 μg/mL using an indirect precomplexing immuneperoxidase procedure. Positive controls consisted of
cryosections of HEK cells expressing human TFPI and
HEK cells expressing cynomolgus monkey TFPI.
Negative controls consisted of cryosections of HEK
cells expressing human interferon alpha and HEK cells
expressing cynomolgus monkey interferon alpha. The
specific reactivity of concizumab with the positive
controls and the lack of specific reactivity with the
negative controls, as well as the lack of reactivity of the
control IgG with the positive and negative controls,
indicated that the assay was sensitive and specific.

Results

(Human)
Human tissues staining by concizumab was present in
the endothelium of small to medium sized vessels in
cerebellum, esophagus, small intestine, heart, kidney,
liver, lung, lymph node, pancreas, spleen, and thymus.
In mononuclear cells in the esophagus, small intestine,
kidney, liver, lung, lymph node, spleen, and thymus. In
spindle cells in the cerebellum, esophagus, small
intestine, heart, kidney, liver, lung, lymph node,
pancreas, spleen, and thymus. In the extracellular
proteinaceous material in cerebellum, esophagus, small
intestine, heart, kidney, liver, lung, lymph node,
pancreas, spleen, and thymus.
(Cynomolgus Monkey)
In cynomolgus monkeys, concizumab tissue staining of
the same cell types was present in the corresponding
tissues with the addition of the cerebrum (endothelium,
spindle cells, and extracellular proteinaceous material)
and pancreas (mononuclear cells). Staining of islet cells
and acinar epithelium in the pancreas and mesothelium
in the spleen was observed only in the cynomolgus
monkey tissue panel (not in the human tissue panel).

Table 69. Study Information, Study 209280, GLP
Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
Yes
Objective

The objective of this study was to evaluate the potential
tissue cross reactivity (TCR) of concizumab with
cryosections of normal healthy human and cynomolgus
monkey tissues.

Methods

Concizumab was used to stain cryosections of normal
human and cynomolgus monkey tissues (3 donors per
tissue) at concentrations of 1 μg/mL or 10 μg/mL using
an indirect precomplexing immune-peroxidase
procedure. Positive controls consisted of cryosections
of HEK cells expressing human TFPI and HEK cells
expressing cynomolgus monkey TFPI. The negative
control consisted of cryosections of non-transfected
HEK cells. The specific reactivity of concizumab with
the positive controls and the lack of specific reactivity
with the negative control, as well as the lack of reactivity
of a control IgG with the positive and negative controls,
indicated that the assay was sensitive and specific.

Results

(Human)
Human tissues staining by concizumab was present in
the endothelium of small to medium sized vessels, in
hematopoietic precursor cells and mononuclear cells, in
spindle cells, in extracellular proteinaceous material,
and in placental decidual cells.
(Cynomolgus Monkey)
In cynomolgus monkeys, concizumab tissue staining of
the same cell types was present with the exception of
placental decidual cells. Staining of testicular
spermatogenic cells, placental Hofbauer cells (placental
villous macrophages) and trophoblastic epithelium was
observed only in the cynomolgus monkey tissue panel
(not observed in the human tissue panel).

Concizumab Biodistribution and Immunohistochemistry (IHC) for Endogenous TFPI in
the Cynomolgus Monkey Choroid Plexus (Study KAHL130402, non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

The intensity of IHC staining for concizumab increased (in a dose-dependent manner) in the
choroid plexus of cynomolgus monkeys (more intense staining at higher doses of
concizumab)

•

Immunohistochemical reactivity for concizumab was predominantly observed in the lumen
of blood vessels (suggesting its presence in the blood plasma) and the endothelium

•

Immunohistochemical reactivity for concizumab in a sample of inflamed choroid plexus
tissue derived from the 26-week cynomolgus monkey study displayed a punctate staining
pattern that likely represented granular tissue deposits of concizumab

•

Immunohistochemical reactivity for endogenous TFPI was predominantly associated with the
endothelium and the intensity of the endothelial TFPI staining was stronger (versus controls)
in tissues derived from concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys

•

A minimal amount of TFPI staining was observed in the lumen of blood vessels derived from
concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys only (not present in controls)

Reviewer Comments: Concizumab was predominantly distributed to the plasma and endothelium
in tissues derived from the 26-week cynomolgus monkey study. Staining of TFPI was enhanced in
tissues derived from concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys and suggests feedback from the
coagulation system to increase TFPI levels in response to the loss in TFPI activity in normal
cynomolgus monkeys.

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Metabolism
Concizumab is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) consisting of naturally occurring (endogenous)
amino acids. Hence the metabolism of concizumab is expected to involve the proteolytic
digestion of the antibody into peptides and its constituent amino acids. Prototypical
biotransformation studies are therefore not considered relevant for concizumab.
Excretion
No dedicated excretion studies were conducted as concizumab is expected to be proteolytically
broken down into individual amino acids that will either be repurposed or excreted in vivo.
Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
The potential for pharmacokinetic drug-drug interactions between concizumab and rFVIIa was
investigated in a 28 Day (PK/PD) Drug-Drug Interaction (Concizumab and rFVIIa) Toxicity
Study in Cynomolgus Monkeys (Study 215431). There were no notable differences in rFVIIa
exposures (Day 28) in the presence or absence of steady state concizumab and the
pharmacokinetics of concizumab was not assessed after the administration of rFVIIa (Day 28).
This study was thoroughly reviewed under the PD drug interactions section above (Study 215431
Section 13.1.1.3).

No
Objective
The objective of this study was to examine preliminary
toxicity, safety pharmacology, toxicokinetics,
immunogenicity and bioavailability of concizumab
following three repeat subcutaneous (SC) dose
administrations to cynomolgus monkeys over a 4-week
period. The toxicokinetic profile of concizumab was also
assessed over a 6-week period following a single
subcutaneous or intravenous (IV) administration to male
cynomolgus monkeys.
Methods
Assessment of toxicity was based on mortality, clinical
observations, body weight, food consumption
(qualitative assessment), blood pressure,
electrocardiogram (ECG), and clinical and anatomical
pathology investigations. Blood samples, for
toxicokinetic evaluation, antibody analyses,
immunophenotyping, coagulation markers and cytokine
analysis were also acquired.

Monkey (Study 210130, GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

Concizumab was pharmacologically active in cynomolgus monkeys as indicated by the
observed reduction in circulating levels of fibrinogen (consistent with the recruitment of
fibrinogen to sites of thrombogenesis) and increases in D-dimers and TAT (indicative of
hemostatic activation), and are consistent with the exaggerated pro-coagulative
(antihemorrhagic) effects of concizumab.

•

The pro-coagulative effects of concizumab were not associated with any notable changes in
PT, APTT, platelets parameters or thrombi formation in normo-coagulative cynomolgus

Major Findings

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Parameters
Major Findings
Body weights and food consumption There were no notable changes in body weights or qualitative
appraisals (visual inspections) of food consumption in cynomolgus
monkeys administered concizumab for 4 weeks at ≤ 9 mg/kg/day
(SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Hematology
(WBC Parameters): White blood cell counts (↑82%) and neutrophil
counts (↑91%) were statistically increased in females (only)
administered concizumab at 9 mg/kg/day (SC) on Day 29. This
effect was not observed at the lower dose (SC) or at the IV dose
(30 mg/kg/week). There were no notable changes in platelet
parameters in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for
4 weeks at ≤ 9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Coagulation
Prothrombin Time (PT) and Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time
(APTT): There were no notable changes in PT and APTT in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4 weeks at ≤
9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Fibrinogen, D-dimers, and Thrombin/Anti-Thrombin (TAT): A
reduction in circulating levels of fibrinogen (consistent with the
recruitment of fibrinogen to thrombus sites) and increases in Ddimers and TAT (indicative of hemostatic activation) were
observed in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4
weeks at ≤ 9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) and are
consistent with the exaggerated pro-coagulative (antihemorrhagic)
pharmacological effects of concizumab. These findings were not
associated with notable changes in platelet parameters or thrombi
formation in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4
weeks at ≤ 9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Clinical chemistry
There were no notable changes in clinical chemistry parameters in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4 weeks at ≤
9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Toxicokinetics
The plasma exposure to concizumab tended to be lower in
females relative to males with both routes of administration (not
statistically significant). Accumulation after once-daily
subcutaneous dosing (3 mg/kg/day and 9 mg/kg/day) was
extensive (up to 275-fold) and after once-weekly intravenous
dosing (30 mg/kg) plasma accumulation was 2-fold to 3-fold. The
plasma terminal half-life (t1/2) after intravenous (IV) administration
ranged from 122 hrs to 340 hrs (5 days to 14 days), barring one
male (#11) in which a t1/2 of 24 hrs was observed. This male was
excluded from the TK analysis due to the notable reduction in
exposure on Day 22 and the minimal exposure noted on Day 29.
These changes were likely driven by the formation of neutralizing
anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) to concizumab (no ADA analysis was
conducted) and correlated with a loss of pharmacodynamic activity
in this individual.
Gross pathology
There were no notable changes in tissues derived from
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for 4 weeks at ≤
9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV).
Organ weights
(Spleen): Spleen weight increased significantly (↑59%, body
weight relative) in females administered 9 mg/kg/day (SC) in the
absence of microscopic changes. This finding was not observed in
males (SC) or in either sex administered 30 mg/kg/week (IV) and
its clinical relevance is unclear.

13 Week Administration (SC/IV) Toxicity and Safety Pharmacology Study in Cynomolgus
Monkeys Followed by a 11 Week Recovery Period (Study 209206, GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

There was one unscheduled mortality (Day 35) of a MD male (#12) that was deemed related
to a gastrointestinal tract lesion that correlated with loose feces and debility (weakness).
While it’s likely that these findings were unrelated to concizumab dosing it should be noted
that this individual presented with a single thrombus in the liver which was considered driven
by dehydration but is potentially related to concizumab exposure in view of its
pharmacology.

•

Concizumab was pharmacologically active in cynomolgus monkeys as indicated by the
observed decreases in fibrinogen and increases in Thrombin Antithrombin Complex (TAT)
and D-dimers (biomarkers of increased coagulation). Activated partial thromboplastin time
(APTT) was prolonged (increased clotting time) early on (Day 16) in males only (in the
absence of a decline in platelets) and may be indicative of homeostatic regulation of the
coagulation system in normo-coagulative cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab.

•

Safety pharmacology endpoints (neurobehavioral, respiratory rate, blood pressure and
electrocardiography), immunophenotyping data and a cytokine assessment were all
unremarkable in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at ≤ 50 mg/kg/day (SC) or
200 mg/kg/week (IV) for 13-weeks. Safety pharmacology endpoints were not assessed postrecovery and there was no indication of delayed toxicity in immunophenotyping or cytokine
values.

•

Antibodies to concizumab (ADAs) were detected in 90% of control cynomolgus monkeys;
however, measurable levels of drug exposure were not observed, and the Applicant did not
provide an explanation for the presence of ADAs in the control group. The Applicant’s antidrug antibody (ADA) assessment was likely insufficient to assess the extent of ADA
production in the presence of high plasma concentrations of concizumab (due to assay
interference). When ADAs were detected, their presence correlated with a decline in
pharmacodynamic activity (e.g., fibrinogen, TAT, and D-dimers) and a reduction in systemic
exposure, indicating that the ADAs produced in cynomolgus monkeys (particularly at the
LD) were inhibitory in nature.

•

Adverse changes in the vasculature (e.g., heart and lung thrombosis and pulmonary vascular
intimal proliferation, chronic inflammation, hemorrhage, and blood vessel
162

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

necrosis/mineralization) were observed in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab
(50 mg/kg/day, SC and/or 200 mg/kg/week, IV) and thrombosis persisted in the heart and
lung following the 11-week recovery period. Thrombosis was likely the result of exaggerated
pharmacology and was considered adverse in cynomolgus monkeys in a normo-coagulative
state as their presence impacted local blood circulation in the individuals affected and failed
to normalize post recovery.
•

Chronic inflammation and subendothelial intimal proliferation in the lung were likely driven
by the observed immunogenic response (deposition of immune complexes, secondary to
development of ADAs) to concizumab (a humanized mAb) in cynomolgus monkeys and
resolved during the 11-week recovery period.

•

Inflammatory cell foci/perivascular cuffing were present in the subcutaneous injection site in
all groups (including controls). However, the overall incidence and severity tended to
increase in HD males (SC) and in females at all doses (≥ 1 mg/kg/day, SC). These findings
were characterized by the presence of perivascular inflammatory cells (predominantly
mononuclear) forming a sheath (cuff) around blood vessels in the subcutis and were trending
towards resolution (minimal severity) in both sexes (MD/HD) following the 11-week
recovery period.

•

Cellulitis/fibrosis was noted predominantly in the subcutaneous injection site of females
(dose independent) at all doses (≥ 1 mg/kg/day) and was characterized by mixed
inflammatory cell infiltration into the subcutis, with varying levels of fibroblastic
proliferation and collagen deposition. The extent of these findings was comparable to
controls in males (SC) and this condition had completely resolved by the end of the 11-week
recovery period.

Reviewer Comments: The reviewer agreed with the Applicant proposed NOAEL (1 mg/kg/day
[LD], SC) based in the adverse changes in the vasculature (e.g., thrombosis) observed at the
MD/HD that persisted at the HD following a 11-week recovery period in cynomolgus monkeys
administered (SC) concizumab at 1, 10 and 50 mg/kg/day for 13-weeks. A NOAEL for
intravenous (IV) dosing was not established as thrombosis-related histopathological findings
were observed at the 200 mg/kg/week (IV) dose. The NOAEL (1 mg/kg/day, SC, 5923 µg.hr/mL
AUC0-24, Day 91) represents a 264x exposure margin to the anticipated clinical dose (0.2 mg/kg,
22.4 µg.hr/mL AUC0-24).

Unremarkable

Hematology

There were no notable changes in RBC, WBC or platelet

There were no definitive changes observed during assessments of
white blood cell (WBC) sub-sets (performed by flow cytometry) in

(Prothrombin Time, PT): There were no clear changes in PT

[≤ 50 mg/kg/day (SC) or 200 mg/kg/week (IV)] for 13 weeks and
there was no evidence of a delayed onset of toxicity.
(Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time, APTT): Activated partial
thromboplastin times were prolonged in males at 24 hrs after the
administration (Day 16) of concizumab on Day 15 (↑28%, ↑44%
and ↑43% at the LD, MD, and HD, respectively (SC) and ↑68% at
the 200 mg/kg/week dose, IV). These changes occurred in the
absence of changes in platelet parameters and there were no
significant changes in APTT during the remainder of the
administration period in males (Days 30, 58, 92 and 93). This
effect was not observed in females at any point during the study
and there was no evidence of a delayed onset of toxicity in either
sex. Activated partial thromboplastin time prolongation (increased
clotting time) is counterintuitive to the anticipated effects of an
antihemorrhagic (that seeks to decrease the time needed to
induce clotting) and may be indicative of homeostatic regulation of
the coagulation system in cynomolgus monkeys in a normocoagulative state.

independent of dose (SC and IV) level (intermittently significant)
from Day 16 forward in females (≤ ↓31%) and from Day 30 forward

Major Findings

(D-dimer Biomarker): D-dimer is a fibrin degradation product (a
small protein fragment present in the blood after a blood clot is
degraded by fibrinolysis) and it is so named because it contains
two D fragments of the fibrin protein joined by a cross-link, hence
forming a protein dimer. D-dimers are not normally present in
blood plasma and tend to manifest when the coagulation system
has been activated. The concentrations of D-dimer increased (in
general, dose-dependently) and were significantly higher on Day
16 (up to ↑5-fold, females), Day 30 (up to ↑5-fold, males), Day 58
(up to ↑6-fold, females), and Day 92 (up to ↑7-fold and ↑5-fold, in
males and females, respectively). Elevations in D-dimer levels
tended to persist through the end of recovery (both sexes) at
doses ≥ 10 mg/kg (SC and IV) indicating that the 11-week
recovery period was insufficient to restore normal coagulation in
cynomolgus monkeys previously administered concizumab at
these levels.
(Thrombin Antithrombin (TAT) Complex): Thrombin antithrombin
complex is a protein complex of thrombin and antithrombin and is
a marker of net activation of coagulation. Thrombin antithrombin
complex (TAT) concentrations increased with dose in both sexes
on Day 16 and were found to be statistically significant in males (≤
↑2-fold, SC). Consistent with the observed increases in D-dimer
levels, elevations in TAT tended to persist through the end of
recovery (both sexes) at doses ≥ 10 mg/kg (SC and IV) indicating
that the 11-week recovery period was insufficient to restore normal
coagulation in cynomolgus monkeys previous administered
concizumab at these levels.

Clinical chemistry

There were no clear changes in clinical chemistry parameters in
whole blood derived from cynomolgus monkeys dosed with
concizumab [≤ 50 mg/kg/day (SC) or 200 mg/kg/week (IV)] for 13
weeks and there was no evidence of a delayed onset of toxicity.

Cytokine assessment

There were no definitive changes in cytokine (IL-2, IL-4, IL-5, IL-6,
TNF-alpha and IFN-gamma) levels during assessments (beadbased flow cytometry) of serum derived from cynomolgus
monkeys dosed with concizumab [≤ 50 mg/kg/day (SC) or
200 mg/kg/week (IV)] for 13 weeks and there was no evidence of
a delayed onset of toxicity.

Anti-drug antibody (ADA) analysis

Antibodies to concizumab (ADAs) were detected in 9 of the 10
cynomolgus monkeys in the control group; however, measurable
levels of drug exposure were not observed (based on toxicokinetic
assessment). The Applicant did not provide an explanation for the
antibody results in the control group.
At the end of the main study period, ADAs were detected in 2 of 9
cynomolgus monkeys in the low dose group (1 mg/kg/day, SC)
and at the end of the recovery period ADAs were detected in the
low and intermediate dose groups (1 and 10 mg/kg/day; 4/4 and
1/3 cynomolgus monkeys, respectively). The study report stated
that the ADA assay could tolerate up to 100 µg/mL concizumab in

Toxicokinetics

The plasma terminal half-life (t1/2) for concizumab was, in general,
short at the low dose (70 hrs to 80 hrs) and was extended at
doses ≥ 10 mg/kg (400 hrs to 500 hrs). Accumulation in systemic
exposure to concizumab after (SC) dosing was extensive (up to
1000-fold) and after once weekly (IV) dosing at 200 mg/kg, plasma
accumulation was 2-fold to 4-fold.
Cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab once daily (SC)
or once weekly (IV) for 3 months were systemically exposed
throughout the administration period, with the exception of two LD
individuals that displayed no exposure after nearly two months of
dosing (Day 58). Cynomolgus monkeys dosed at ≥ 10 mg/kg/day
(SC), or 200 mg/kg/week (IV) were likely exposed throughout the
11-week recovery period

Unremarkable

Histopathology

(Heart): Histopathological evaluations revealed thrombosis in the
heart (a mature thrombus was present in the right ventricle of 1
HD female (#38) administered (SC) concizumab at 50 mg/kg/day.

was present in 1 HD (SC) female (#40) following the 11-week
recovery period suggesting non-reversibility and/or delay onset.

Adequate battery: yes

and medium sized blood vessels) where the vessels exhibited
vascular intimal proliferation in 1 HD female (#39) administered
(SC) concizumab at 50 mg/kg/day. This female also presented
with chronic inflammation of the lung (left posterior lobe) that was
characterized as a focal area of lung with fibroblastic proliferation
and hemorrhage, and blood vessels with necrotic/mineralized
walls. Vascular intimal proliferation in the lung manifested at the
200 mg/kg/week (IV) dose in the posterior lung lobe in 1 male
(#19) and 1 female (#42) and was characterized by multi-layering
and infolding of the vascular endothelium, and endothelial cells
with plump nuclei and increased levels of cytoplasm. A thrombus
(medium sized artery) was present in the female (#42, IV) and
there were no notable findings in the lungs at doses ≤
10 mg/kg/day (SC).
An early fibrin thrombus was present in a large artery of the
posterior lung lobe in 1 HD (SC) female (#40) following the 11week recovery period (Note: this female also presented with a
mature thrombus in the left ventricle of the heart, post recovery).

26 Week Toxicity Study by the Subcutaneous and Intravenous Routes of Administration in
Cynomolgus Monkeys With a 13 Week Recovery Period (Study 211087, GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

A NOAEL was not identified in the 26-week repeat-dose toxicity study conducted in
cynomolgus monkeys due to the presence of thrombi and/or vascular changes at all doses.
The material assessed in the 26-week study contained a disproportionate increase in the level
of high molecular weight proteins (HMWPs) that consisted predominantly of concizumab
multimers. In contrast, the material used in the 4-, 13-, and 52-week toxicity studies was
comparable to the clinical material and contained significantly lower amounts of HMWPs.
Evaluations were conducted to assess if the change in impurity profile could account for the
increased toxicity observed in the 26-week cynomolgus monkey study. There were no
significant differences noted between the monomeric and the multimeric forms of
concizumab across multiple in vitro assays (See Section 13.1.2.5.3 Mechanistic Analyses).
The contribution of the multimeric forms of concizumab to the observed pathology/toxicity
in the 26-week cynomolgus monkey study was considered driven by increased
immunogenicity and the subsequent immune complex (IC) formation/deposition.

•

There was one unscheduled mortality at the 9 mg/kg/day (SC) dose (HD) that was triggered
by the onset of a severe adverse reaction (Day 121) to concizumab (0.5 hours post-dose) in a
single female (#34) where thrombosis and subsequent massive coagulative lung necrosis
precipitated the moribund condition.
168

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

•

Concizumab was pharmacologically active in cynomolgus monkeys as indicated by the
observed decreases in TFPI activity and fibrinogen and the increases in D-dimer and TAT
(biomarkers of increased coagulation). Prothrombin time and activated partial thromboplastin
time were prolonged (in the absence of a significant decline in platelets) and may be
indicative of homeostatic regulation of coagulation in normo-coagulative monkeys
administered an antihemorrhagic.

•

The Applicant’s anti-drug antibody (ADA) assessment in cynomolgus monkeys was likely
insufficient to assess the extent of ADA production in the presence of high plasma
concentrations of concizumab (due to assay interference). In some cases, the development of
ADAs correlated with a reduction in systemic exposure and suggests that the ADAs
produced in cynomolgus monkeys were likely inhibitory in nature.

•

Adverse changes in the vasculature of the brain and/or choroid plexus (e.g., hypertrophy,
hyperplasia, inflammation, edema and/or lymphoid foci) and the lungs (e.g., subendothelial
intimal thickening and/or endothelial cell hypertrophy) were observed in cynomolgus
monkeys administered concizumab at ≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) for 26
weeks. These vascular changes are consistent with an immunogenic response (deposition of
immune complexes, secondary to development of ADAs) to a foreign protein and the
administration of an antihemorrhagic (pro-coagulant) to cynomolgus monkeys in a normocoagulative state and tended to resolve during recovery.

•

Thrombosis manifested in the lungs, liver, epididymis, and thymus of cynomolgus monkeys
administered concizumab at ≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) for 26 weeks.
Thrombi were present in the lungs, heart, and IV injection site at the end of recovery and
indicate that a duration of 13 weeks was insufficient to reverse the presence of drug-induced
thrombi in normo-coagulative monkeys previously administered concizumab at
≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV). Thrombosis was likely the result of exaggerated
pharmacology and was considered adverse at all doses in cynomolgus monkeys.

•

Perivascular cellular infiltrates (consisting predominantly of plasma cells and lymphocytes,
e.g., neutrophils, eosinophils, and giant cells) and endothelial hypertrophy in the local blood
vessels were associated with the subcutaneous injection site and were trending towards
resolution post-recovery (13 weeks) in cynomolgus monkeys previously administered
concizumab at ≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) for 26 weeks.

Reviewer Comments: The reviewer agreed with the Applicant that a NOAEL could not be
established for this study based in the adverse changes in the vasculature and thrombosis
observed in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at ≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) and
30 mg/kg/week (IV) for 26-weeks. The lowest observed adverse effect level (LOAEL) for
subcutaneous (SC) dosing was 3 mg/kg/day (21350 µg.hr/mL, AUC0-24, sex-averaged, Week 26)
and represents a 953x exposure margin to the anticipated clinical dose (0.2 mg/kg/day,
22.4 µg.hr/mL AUC0-24).

Table 82. Observations and Results, Study 211087, GLP
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
There was one unscheduled death (female #34) at the
9 mg/kg/day (SC) dose (HD) where this individual was terminated
on Day 121 due to the onset of a severe adverse reaction to
concizumab. Clinical signs in this HD female following the
administration of concizumab (Day 121) included: severe subdued
behavior, paleness (gums and face), shallow breathing, slow
movement, and a collapsed state (unconsciousness). Due to the
moribund condition, this female was terminated at 0.5 hrs postdose on Day 121. There were no clinical signs recorded in this
female (#34) before Day 121.
Hematological values (versus predose) in this female showed a
marked reduction in hemoglobin concentrations, erythrocyte
counts and hematocrit that corresponded to a notable increase in
reticulocyte counts and elevations in total leukocyte, and
neutrophil counts. Coagulation parameter values were comparable
to predose and no dose controls. The Applicant indicated that (at
the time of termination) this female had presented with a heavy
menstrual blood flow that was observed for 9 days and may have
contributed to the observed anemia.
Clinical chemistry values (versus predose) in this HD female
showed increases in creatine phosphokinase activity, creatinine
and phosphate concentrations and reductions in sodium, chloride,
total protein, albumin, and globulin concentrations.

Parameters

Major Findings
Pale discoloration of the internal organs was observed at necropsy
and a recently formed blood clot was present in the vagina that
correlated clinically and histologically with menstruation. Two firm,
mottled masses were present in the caudal lung lobes and upon
histopathological evaluation, thrombosis and subsequent massive
coagulative necrosis of the lung parenchyma were determined to
be the cause of the moribund condition. In addition, subendothelial
intimal thickening manifested in a number of blood vessels, and a
subset of affected blood vessels showed an accumulation of
foamy macrophages within the thickened intimal stroma.
Endothelial hypertrophy and hyperplasia were also observed in
blood vessels in the lungs and mononuclear cell infiltration
(minimal) was present in the choroid plexus.

Clinical signs

Barring the adverse clinical signs observed in the HD female (#37)
that was terminated early (Day 121; Week 18), there were no
notable clinical signs associated with the administration of
concizumab at ≤ 9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) to
cynomolgus monkeys for 26-weeks.

Body weights and food consumption There were no notable differences in the observed trends in body
weight changes or the qualitative measurement of food
consumption in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (≤
9 mg/kg/day, SC or 30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26-weeks.
Ophthalmoscopy

Unremarkable

Hematology

Barring the hematological changes observed in the HD female
(#37) that was terminated early (Day 121; Week 18), there were
no notable changes in hematological parameters (including
platelet parameters) in cynomolgus monkeys administered
concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day, SC or 30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26weeks or following the 13-week recovery period

Coagulation

(Prothrombin Time, PT) Prothrombin time prolongation
(significant) was observed in intravenously dosed males only
(30 mg/kg/week, IV) from Week 6 forward and tended to normalize
during the recovery period. Prothrombin time prolongation
(increased clotting time) is counterintuitive to the anticipated
effects of an antihemorrhagic (that seeks to decrease the time
needed to induce clotting) and may be indicative of homeostatic
regulation of the coagulation system in cynomolgus monkeys in a
normo-coagulative state.
(Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time, APTT) Activated partial
thromboplastin time prolongation (significant intermittently)
manifested during Week 6 in intravenously dosed males
(30 mg/kg/week, IV) and in LD (3 mg/kg/day, SC) females during
Week 13 and LD/HD (≤ 9 mg/kg/day, SC) females during Week
26. These findings tended to normalize during the recovery period.
Activated partial thromboplastin time prolongation (increased
clotting time) is also counterintuitive to the anticipated effects of an
antihemorrhagic (that seeks to decrease the time needed to
induce clotting) and may be indicative of homeostatic regulation of
the coagulation system in cynomolgus monkeys in a normocoagulative state.

Concizumab

Parameters

Major Findings
(Fibrinogen - Prothrombin Time-Derived): Fibrinogen
concentrations tended to decline independent of dose level
(intermittently significant) from Week 6 forward in cynomolgus
monkeys administered concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day, SC or
30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26-weeks and is consistent with the
recruitment of fibrinogen to sites of clot formation. Fibrinogen
concentrations were comparable to pre-dose controls in recovery
females but tended to remain lower (not significant) in males
following the 13-week recovery period.
(Fibrinogen - Clauss Assay): The Clauss fibrinogen assay is a
quantitative, clot-based, functional assay that measures the ability
of fibrinogen to form fibrin clot after being exposed to a high
concentration of purified thrombin. Fibrinogen concentrations
tended to decline (intermittently significant) from Week 6 forward
in males administered concizumab (30 mg/kg/week, IV). Similar
findings were observed during Week 13 in LD males (3 mg/kg/day,
SC) and to a lesser extent in HD males (9 mg/kg/day, SC).
Numerical decreases were noted intermittently in females (not
significant) and these changes tended to normalize in both sexes
during recovery.
(D-dimer Biomarker): D-dimer is a fibrin degradation product (a
small protein fragment present in the blood after a blood clot is
degraded by fibrinolysis) that is so named because it contains two
D fragments of the fibrin protein joined by a cross-link, hence
forming a protein dimer. D-dimers are not normally present in
blood plasma and tend to manifest when the coagulation system
has been activated. The concentrations of D-dimer increased from
Week 6 forward in cynomolgus monkeys administered
concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day, SC or 30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26weeks and is consistent with the clot promoting effects of
concizumab. This effect was persistent at the 9 mg/kg/day (SC)
dose and was tending towards normalization at the 3 mg/kg/day
(SC) dose and the 30 mg/kg/week (IV) dose.
(Thrombin Antithrombin (TAT) Complex): Thrombin antithrombin
complex (TAT) is a protein complex of thrombin and antithrombin
and is a marker of net activation of coagulation. Thrombin
antithrombin complex (TAT) concentrations tended to increase
(intermittently) with dose during the administration period but
showed a high degree of intra- and inter-group variability
throughout the administration period and following recovery in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day,
SC or 30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26-weeks.

Clinical chemistry

There were no notable changes in clinical chemistry parameters in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day,
SC or 30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26-weeks or following the 13-week
recovery period.

(predose) from cynomolgus monkeys. A high degree of variability
was observed in the chromogenic activity assay that attempted to
quantitate the residual TFPI functionality/activity in the plasma of
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab. Assay values

Anti-drug antibody (ADA) analysis

Two males and two females administered 3 mg/kg/day (SC), 1
male (9 mg/kg/day, SC) and 1 male and 1 female dosed at
30 mg/kg/week (IV) developed anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) to
concizumab.

Toxicokinetics

Exposure to concizumab was reduced in two females (#30,
3 mg/kg/day, SC and #36, 30 mg/kg/week, IV) that developed
ADAs. In the subcutaneously dose female (#30), exposure was
reduced from Week 5 forward and in the female (#36)
administered 30 mg/kg/week (IV) the exposure was reduced from
Week 13 forward indicating that the ADAs produced in these
individuals were likely neutralizing.
In general, lower exposures were observed in females relative to
males after subcutaneous administration. Accumulation ratios
(Racobs) ranged from: 60 to 195 at 3 mg/kg/day (SC), 29 to 115 at
9 mg/kg/day (SC) and 1.5 to 5.8 at 30 mg/kg/week (IV).

Urinalysis

Unremarkable

Gross pathology

Irregular red foci were noted in the lungs of 1 male (#17) that was
administered concizumab at 30 mg/kg/week (IV) and correlated
with the presence of thrombi observed microscopically. There
were no other notable findings observed grossly in cynomolgus
monkeys administered concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day, SC or
30 mg/kg/week, IV) for 26-weeks or following the 13-week
recovery period.

this finding to the administration of concizumab is unclear.
