BLA 761315

Concizumab

52-Week Toxicity Study by the Subcutaneous Route of Administration in Cynomolgus
Monkeys with No Assessment of Recovery (Study 213542, GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

Concizumab was pharmacologically active in cynomolgus monkeys as indicated by the
observed decreases in free TFPI, fibrinogen and platelets, and the increase in D-dimer
(a biomarker of increased coagulation). Prothrombin time and activated partial
thromboplastin time were prolonged (increased clotting time) and may be indicative of
homeostatic regulation of the coagulation system in normo-coagulative cynomolgus monkeys
administered concizumab.

•

The Applicant’s anti-drug antibody (ADA) assessment in cynomolgus monkeys was likely
insufficient to assess the extent of ADA production in the presence of high plasma
concentrations of concizumab (due to assay interference). The development of ADAs
correlated with a decline in pharmacodynamic activity (e.g., Protein C and platelets), an
increase in chromogenic (TFPI) activity and a reduction in systemic exposure, indicating that
the ADAs produced in cynomolgus monkeys were inhibitory in nature.

•

Adverse changes in the vasculature (e.g., thrombosis, infarction/necrosis and subendothelial
intimal thickening) of multiple organ systems were observed in cynomolgus monkeys
administered concizumab (≥1 mg/kg/day, MD) for 13, 26 and 52 Weeks, and the reversibility
of these conditions was not assessed. These findings are consistent with the administration of
an antihemorrhagic (pro-coagulant) to cynomolgus monkeys in a normo-coagulative state.

•

Thrombosis manifested in the lungs and liver at the HD (9 mg/kg/day) after 13, 26 and 52
weeks, and at the MD (1 mg/kg/day) after 52 weeks. Thrombosis was also present in the
heart and axillary lymph nodes at the HD after 13 weeks, in the spleen at the HD after 26 and
52 weeks, in the epididymis and heart at the HD and in the mesenteric lymph nodes at the
MD after 52 weeks. Thrombosis at the MD/HD were likely the result of exaggerated
pharmacology and were considered adverse in cynomolgus monkeys in a normo-coagulative
state as their presence impacted local blood circulation in the individuals affected.

•

Subendothelial intimal thickening of the blood vessels (small to medium-sized arteries and/or
veins) in the lungs was observed at the HD after 13, 26 and 52 weeks and at the MD after 52
weeks. This condition was also present in the liver at the HD after 26 weeks and was initially
reported to be present in the large intestine (colon) after 13 weeks in the interim study report
but, was ultimately excluded from the final study report. The subendothelial intimal
thickening was likely driven by the observed immunogenic response (deposition of immune
complexes, secondary to development of ADAs) to concizumab (a humanized mAb) in
cynomolgus monkeys.

•

In the central nervous system (CNS), minimal to marked inflammation (lymphoplasmacytic
infiltration, with or without associated eosinophils and macrophages) of the choroid plexus
(brain) was observed at the HD after 13 weeks and at all dose levels (≥0.5 mg/kg/day) after
26 and 52 weeks. and was likely related to the deposition of immune complexes (ADAs) in
cynomolgus monkeys.

•

Lymphoplasmacytic infiltrations were also present at the subcutaneous injection site at the
HD after 13 weeks and at all dose levels (≥0.5 mg/kg/day) after 26 and 52 weeks, and were
consistent with the findings observed in the CNS.

Reviewer Comments: The reviewer agreed with the Applicant proposed NOAEL (0.5 mg/kg/day,
LD) based in the adverse changes in the vasculature (e.g., thrombosis, infarction, subendothelial
intimal thickening) and the increased severity in CNS inflammation observed at the MD/HD in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab at 0.5, 1, or 9 mg/kg/day for 52 weeks. The
reversibility of the findings in cynomolgus monkeys was not assessed and the NOAEL
(0.5 mg/kg/day, 1660 µg.hr/mL AUC0-24, Week 52) represents a 74x exposure margin to the
anticipated clinical dose (0.20 mg/kg, 22.4 µg.hr/mL AUC0-24).

Parameters
Electrocardiograms (ECG)

Major Findings
Electrocardiograms (ECG) were recorded in non-fasted,
cynomolgus monkeys (unrestrained except for during the dosing
procedure) via a jacketed external telemetry system (lead II ECG
waveform morphology) once during pretreatment and at Week 12,
25 and 51 for 20 hrs to 24 hrs (post-dose). Measurement of RR
(heart rate), PR, QRS and QT interval values were performed. A
distribution-based rate-corrected QT (QTca) was calculated (Note:
due to missing baseline data at the HD (9 mg/kg/day), a speciesspecific QT correction was applied instead of an individual animal
correction; however, due to a lack of dose-relationship in the HR
and QT interval the study cardiologist considered it unlikely that
the QTca was affected).
Significant changes in ECG parameters (Week 12, Week 25, and
Week 51) were noted in males and/or females intermittently (at all
doses) and in view of statistically significant changes present in
some of the baseline data and the lack of a clear dose
relationship, the study cardiologist considered these findings to be
biologically irrelevant.

Hematology

(Platelet Parameters): A significant decline in platelet counts was
observed in males at all doses, with the effect at the HD (↓44% to
↓86%) manifesting by Week 6 and continuing until the end of
dosing. The decline in platelets in LD/MD males (↓67% to ↓79%)
started during Week 34 and continued to the end of dosing. A
similar effect was observed in HD females where platelet counts
declined (↓58% to ↓62%) from Week 44 forward. A significant
effect on platelet counts was not observed in MD females,
however platelet counts declined significantly in LD females (↓67%
to ↓73%). The decline in circulating platelet counts is consistent
with the recruitment of platelets to thrombus sites.

Concizumab

Coagulation

(Prothrombin Time - PT): Significant prothrombin time
prolongation (up to ↑17%) manifested during Week 6 at the HD in
both sexes and persisted thru Week 26. This effect tended to
wane by Week 30 in both sexes and PT values in HD males were
comparable to controls by Week 48. Prothrombin time
prolongation (increased clotting time) is counterintuitive to the
anticipated effects of an antihemorrhagic (that seeks to decrease
the time needed to induce clotting) and may be indicative of
homeostatic regulation of the coagulation system in cynomolgus
monkeys in a normo-coagulative state.
(Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time - APTT): Significant
activated partial thromboplastin time prolongation (up to ↑16%)
manifested during Week 9 and persisted thru Week 48 in HD
males. This effect was present to a lesser extent in HD females
during Week 17 (up to ↑7%). Activated partial thromboplastin time
prolongation (increased clotting time) is also counterintuitive to the
anticipated effects of an antihemorrhagic (that seeks to decrease
the time needed to induce clotting) and may be indicative of
homeostatic regulation of the coagulation system in cynomolgus
monkeys in a normo-coagulative state.
(Fibrinogen - Prothrombin Time-Derived): Fibrinogen
concentrations tended to decline independent of dose level
(intermittently significant) from Week 6 forward in males (↓41% to
↓102%) and females (↓60% to ↓109%) and is consistent with the
recruitment of fibrinogen to thrombus sites.
(D-dimer Biomarker): The concentrations of D-dimer increased
(dose-dependently) and were notably higher at Week 13, 26, 39
and 52 in MD/HD monkeys. These changes tended to be
statistically significant at the HD after Week 13 (excluding females
at Week 52) and at the MD during Weeks 26 and/or 39. The
increase in D-dimer is consistent with the clot promoting effects of
concizumab.
(Protein C (Autoprothrombin IIA) Biomarker): Protein C (a natural
anticoagulant) is a vitamin K-dependent glycoprotein structurally
similar to other vitamin K-dependent proteins affecting blood
clotting (i.e., prothrombin, Factor VII, Factor IX and Factor X). The
Protein C system provides important control of blood coagulation
by regulating the activities of factor VIIIa (FVIIIa) and factor Va
(FVa), cofactors in the activation of factor X and prothrombin,
respectively. Protein C levels in the plasma tended to be
numerically lower (up to ↓14%) in concizumab-dosed groups and
reached statistical significance on a few occasions prior to Week
52. During the last week of dosing (Week 52), plasma
concentrations of Protein C increased significantly (↑15%) in MD
females (consistent with the lack of an effect on platelet counts)
and suggests a decline in pharmacodynamics activity.
(Protein S (PROS) Biomarker): Protein S (a natural anticoagulant)
is a vitamin K-dependent plasma glycoprotein synthesized in the
liver. Protein S functions as a cofactor to Protein C in the
inactivation of Factors Va and VIIIa. Protein S concentrations
increased significantly in HD males (↑33%) and decreased
significantly in LD males and in MD/HD females (↓14% to ↓16%)
during Week 6. These results did not correspond sufficiently with

(Antithrombin Biomarker): Antithrombin is the primary inhibitor of
thrombin, Factor Xa and Factor IXa. Antithrombin levels in the
plasma tended to decline numerically (up to ↓27%) in concizumabdosed cynomolgus monkeys (intermittently reached statistical

(Thrombin Generation Test - TGT): Plasma samples for TGT were
analyzed using a non-validated methodology and the results were
stated to be reported separately by the Applicant.

Clinical chemistry

There were no notable changes in clinical chemistry parameters in
cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab (≤ 9 mg/kg/day)
for 52-weeks.

Free TFPI biomarker

Blood samples for the free TFPI were collected on Weeks -2, -1,
Residual TFPI functionality (activity) Day 1, Week 6, 13, 26, 39 and 52. A dose-dependent reduction in
the level of free TFPI (not bound to concizumab) following the
administration of concizumab was observed in cynomolgus
monkeys. The levels of free TFPI were below the lower limit of
detection (12.6 ng/mL) for the majority of MD/HD cynomolgus
monkeys from Week 6 forward. Free TFPI levels tended to be
higher in males. These findings are consistent with the proposed
mechanism of action, whereby concizumab binds specifically to
the Kunitz-domain-2 of the TFPI and prevents TFPI from binding
to and blocking the active site of the coagulation factor Xa (FXa).
Blood samples for the chromogenic plasma TFPI activity were
collected on Weeks -2, -1, Day 1, Week 6, 9, 13, 17, 21, 26, 30,
34, 39, 44 and 48 from cynomolgus monkeys. A high degree of
variability was observed in the chromogenic activity assay (U/mL)
that attempted to quantitate the residual TFPI functionality/activity
in the plasma of cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab.
Assay values tended to be lower in concizumab-dosed groups
(when adjusted for individual baseline levels) and suggests that
the plasma TFPI activity may have been reduced by concizumab.
The development of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) tended to
correspond to an increase in chromogenic (TFPI) activity and a
decline in systemic exposure indicating that the ADAs produced in
cynomolgus monkeys were likely inhibitory in nature.
Total TFPI (free TFPI and TFPI bound to concizumab) was
analyzed using a non-validated methodology and the results were
stated to be reported separately by the Applicant.

Concizumab

Parameters
Anti-drug antibody (ADA) analysis

Major Findings
Anti-drug antibodies were detectable in LD monkeys at Week 6
(67%) and the number of ADA-positive monkeys increased by
Week 13 (89%) and remained positive thru Week 26. Cynomolgus
monkeys (LD) continuing after the 26-week interim sacrifice (N=9)
were all ADA positive between Weeks 34 and Week 52, excluding
a LD male (#2007) that was negative during Weeks 44 and 52. At
the MD, ADAs were detectable at Week 6 (17%) and the number
of ADA-positive monkeys increased by Week 13 (28%) and
remained positive thru Week 26. Two of the cynomolgus monkeys
(MD) continuing after the 26-week interim sacrifice (20%) were
ADA positive thru Week 52. Anti-drug antibodies (ADAs) were not
detected in HD monkeys and suggests that higher concentrations
of concizumab in the plasma may interfere with the ADA assay.
It’s likely that the actual number of ADA positive monkeys at the
MD was higher and that ADAs were present at the HD but not
detectable due to deficiencies in ADA detection assay
methodology.

Toxicokinetic

The toxicokinetic evaluation indicated that systemic exposure to
concizumab was affected by the presence of anti-drug antibodies
(ADAs) at the LD/MD.
After the first administration, tmax (median) was reached at 6 hrs or
24 hrs (post dose). After repeat-dose administration (Weeks 13,
26 and 52), tmax (median) ranged from 0 hrs to 24 hrs.
Concizumab exposures (Cmax and AUC0-24hrs) tended to be lower in
females and increased in a greater than dose-proportional
manner, particularly at the LD/MD (non-linear pharmacokinetics).
Concizumab accumulation was observed at all doses but was
particularly notable at the LD/MD where individual accumulation
values (RacObs) of up to 639 (MD Male - #3006) were observed.

Gross pathology

(Thrombi): Thrombus (dark focus on the left caudal lobe of the
lung) was observed grossly in 1 HD female (#4502) at Week 13.
No gross pathological findings were noted in cynomolgus
monkeys after 26 weeks of dosing (≤ 9 mg/kg/day) with
concizumab. Thrombus (mass) was observed grossly in the heart
of 1 HD female (#4513) and in the lung of 1 MD female (#3505) at
Week 52. The formation of thrombi is consistent with the procoagulative effects of concizumab in normal healthy cynomolgus
monkeys.

Organ weights

Unremarkable.

13.1.2.2. Genotoxicity Studies
Concizumab is a monoclonal antibody (mAb) consisting of naturally occurring amino acids, and
is not expected to be genotoxic or to interact directly with DNA or other chromosomal material.
There were no mechanistic data submitted which suggested that concizumab had mutagenic or
proliferative potential, and as a result, genotoxicity studies were not performed with concizumab.

BLA 761315

Concizumab

13.1.2.3. Carcinogenicity Studies
As concizumab is not functionally active in rodents, standard regulatory carcinogenicity studies
in rats and/or mice would not be expected to provide a valuable assessment of carcinogenicity.
Therefore, the Applicant conducted a carcinogenicity assessment of concizumab using a weight
of evidence approach (Study 321420) that considered the mode of action, a review of the
relevant literature (that included an evaluation of >670 publications) and an evaluation of the
nonclinical and clinical data, consistent with the guidance provided in ICH S6 (R1). A summary
of the Applicant’s assessment is provided below.
•

Tumor growth and metastasis is a complex and multi-factorial process and the TF/Factor VII
(FVIIa) signaling pathway is known to contribute to this process. However, physiological
levels of Tissue Factor Pathway Inhibitor (TFPI) do not appear to influence these reactions.
Instead, at physiological levels, TFPI (and by extension concizumab, a TFPI antagonist)
influence signaling via TF/FVIIa/FXa and this signaling pathway has not been associated
with tumor development.

•

The C-terminal part of TFPI has been suggested to play a role in reducing tumor cell
proliferation, angiogenesis, and metastasis. These processes are not expected to be affected
by the specific binding of concizumab to the K2 domain of TFPI.

•

The inhibition of TFPI by concizumab leads to increased thrombin generation downstream in
the coagulation process. Thrombin has been reported to mediate signaling reactions
implicated in cancer, and these reactions may proceed if/when hemostasis is restored in
hemophilia. Therefore, concizumab (and, in general, any procoagulant agent) has the
potential to enhance the effects of thrombin, including the prothrombotic state, observed in
many types of cancer.

•

Tissue Factor Pathway Inhibitor (TFPI) is present in some cancer cell lines at varying
expression levels, and increased expression of TFPI would be expected to dampen the
procoagulant effects of TF. Therefore, it is conceivable that concizumab (as well as any other
procoagulant agent) could enhance the hypercoagulable state often observed with cancer.
Treatment of hemophilia patients with concizumab therefore has the potential to increase the
risk of thromboembolic events in subjects with undiagnosed malignancies.

•

In individuals with partial TFPI deficiency (e.g., due to specific TFPI polymorphisms, factor
V (FV) deficiency or intake of pharmaceutical estrogen products, i.e., oral contraceptives),
the effect of reduced TFPI on general cancer risk in humans is considered predominantly
neutral.

•

Concizumab is a first-in-class mAb directed against TFPI. The available literature reports
from three other TFPI antibodies in clinical development did not reveal any information
related to human cancer risk.

•

Concizumab has been evaluated in nonclinical repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus
monkeys with daily subcutaneous administration for up to 52 weeks. In these studies, no
signs of proliferation, neovascularization, immunosuppression, or organ weight changes were
observed.
183

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

While the reviewer agreed with the Applicant that there were no signs of neovascularization or
immunosuppression in cynomolgus monkeys administered concizumab for up to 52 weeks,
vascular intimal proliferation/hypertrophy/hyperplasia in the lungs, vascular hypertrophy and/or
hyperplasia in the brain (choroid plexus) and/or the presence of a granuloma in the caudate
nucleus of the brain were observed in cynomolgus monkeys dosed with concizumab for
≥13 weeks. While the Applicant suggests that the proliferative changes in the vasculature are
likely the consequence of an immunogenic response to (and not a pharmacological effect of)
concizumab, the reviewer contends that these changes are relevant to the overall carcinogenicity
risk assessment of concizumab.
•

Concizumab is currently being assessed in two clinical Phase 3 studies. The clinical data
from the completed Phase 1B study and the two Phase 2 studies as well as serious adverse
events reported from the two ongoing Phase 3 studies, have not identified any safety
concerns in relation to cancer.

The Applicant considers the carcinogenic potential of concizumab to be adequately evaluated by
this weight of evidence evaluation. It is concluded that treatment with concizumab potentially
may enhance the prothrombotic conditions observed in some cancer patients, as would be
expected for any other procoagulant agent. However, the assessment has not identified evidence
that treatment with concizumab may lead to an increased risk of inducing or enhancing tumor
development in humans.
•

The Division determined that no additional nonclinical studies assessing the carcinogenic
potential of concizumab were warranted for the indications of hemophilia A and
hemophilia B, and the CDER Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC)
concurred with the Division’s recommendation.
13.1.2.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity

No reproductive and developmental toxicity studies were conducted with concizumab as the
intended population is male hemophilia patients, and the clinical development program was
conducted exclusively in males. Hemophilia A is inherited in an X-linked recessive manner and
the disease which occurs predominantly in males (99%) is extremely rare in females.
Concizumab is also being indicated for Hemophilia B (Factor IX deficiency or Christmas
disease, caused by mutations in the coagulation factor IX (FIX) gene on the X chromosome) and
hence, is predominantly observed in males (some females who carry the gene may display mild
or, rarely, severe symptoms of bleeding).
13.1.2.4.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
Consistent with the guidance provided in ICH S6 (R1), specialized evaluations of fertility
parameters were included in the 26-week chronic toxicity study conducted in sexually mature
cynomolgus monkeys (Study 211087 in Section 13.1.2.1).
There were no notable effects on the menstrual cycle duration, the length, width and volume of
the testis, total sperm count, sperm motility or sperm morphology in cynomolgus monkeys
administered concizumab at ≤9 mg/kg/day (SC) or 30 mg/kg/week (IV) for 26 weeks.
184
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Histopathological evaluations of the reproductive organs conducted during the repeat-dose
toxicity studies (≤52 weeks, GLP) in juvenile and sexually mature male and female cynomolgus
monkeys did not indicate any adverse effects on the reproductive organs.
13.1.2.4.2. Embryo Fetal Development
In mice, inactivation of the TFPI-gene has been shown to result in embryonic lethality (Huang et
al. 1997; Ellery et al. 2015) and total TFPI deficiency has not been described in humans (Wood
et al. 2014).The Applicant suggests that, as placental transfer of IgG is limited during
organogenesis in humans, these findings may not be relevant when administering a monoclonal
IgG antibody (i.e., concizumab) to humans (DeSesso et al. 2012).
It should be noted that an association of recurrent pregnancy loss was observed in women who
exhibited TFPI deficiency in combination with an Activated Protein C (APC) resistance
phenotype, and in women with anti-TFPI antibodies in combination with antiphospholipid
antibodies, suggesting that TFPI may be critical to the maintenance of normal pregnancy in
humans (Martinuzzo et al. 2005; Gardiner et al. 2006).
The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that the implications of blocking TFPI
functionality with a monoclonal antibody in pregnant women are not clear.
13.1.2.4.3. Pre- and Postnatal Development
No pre- and postnatal development studies have been conducted with concizumab as the
hemophilia patient population is almost exclusively male.
13.1.2.4.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
Dedicated juvenile animal studies were not conducted with concizumab, consistent with the
guidance provided in ICH M3 (R2) 2.
The Applicant contends that the hemostatic system was the only target organ identified during
toxicity assessments. However, the reviewer does not agree with this statement based on the
extensive immune-related and vascular toxicity observed in cynomolgus monkeys. The human
hemostatic system matures during the early weeks and months of life, and the concentrations of
most hemostatic proteins, both in full-term and preterm infants, are comparable to adult values
by 6 months of age (Albisetti 2003; Chalmers 2004).
Consequently, the Applicant argues that the toxicity of concizumab has been adequately
characterized through the general toxicology program in juvenile (2 to 3 years of age) and
sexually mature (3.5 to 6 years of age) cynomolgus monkeys.
Studies in neonatal and infant monkeys are technically challenging and are unlikely to identify
potential reactions that were not identified from studies in fully developed (adult) animals.

13.1.2.5.3. Mechanistic Analyses
Separation of Concizumab High Molecular Weight Proteins (HMWPs) by Size Exclusion
Chromatography (Study 215027, non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

Concizumab (batch ATfK505001) was separated into monomer, dimer, trimer and multimer
fractions to allow for detailed biological characterization of concizumab derived HMWP.

Reviewer Comments: The material assessed in the 26-week cynomolgus monkey toxicity study
contained increased levels of high molecular weight proteins (HMWPs) that consisted
predominantly of concizumab multimers. In contrast, the material used in the 4-, 13- and 52week toxicity studies was comparable to the clinical material and contained significantly lower
amounts of HMWPs. This study is part of the evaluations that were conducted to assess whether
the change in impurity profile could account for the increased toxicity observed in the 26-week
cynomolgus monkey study.

Table 88. Study Information, Study 215027, Non-GLP
Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
No
Objective
The preclinical batch of concizumab (ATfK505001)
contained 12.8% high molecular weight protein (HMWP)
comprising a heterogeneous mixture of species with
molecular weights ranging from 300 kDa (corresponding
to a dimer) to at least 1 MDa. In order to enable a more
detailed biological characterization of concizumab
HMWPs, sub-populations of HMWPs were isolated with
more narrow size distributions.
Methods
Concizumab (Batch ATfK505001) was subjected to size
exclusion chromatography (SEC) and separated into
monomer, dimer, trimer, and higher order multimer
fractions. The size distribution of the individual fractions
was assessed by size exclusion high performance liquid
chromatography multi-angle light scattering (SE-HPLCMALS) analysis. Dimer, trimer and multimer species
were found to be relatively stable, i.e., only minor
dissociation into monomer was observed upon handling
and storage.
Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of the toxicology Study (215027) submitted to the BLA.

Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practices; HMWP, high molecular weight protein; SEC, size exclusion chromatography; SEHPLC-MALS, size exclusion high performance liquid chromatography multi-angle light scattering

Separation Analysis of Anti-Concizumab Antibodies by Size Exclusion Chromatography
and Immune Complex (IC) Enzyme-linked Immunoassay (ELISA) in Cynomolgus Monkey

Key Study Findings
•

Immune complexes (ADA/concizumab) of different sizes developed over time (compared to

monkeys. In the control group, no immune complex formation was observed over time.

There was a trend toward more immune complexes of a high molecular weight in samples
derived from cynomolgus monkeys in the 26-week toxicity study (versus the 52-week study)
that employed concizumab with an increased level of high molecular weight proteins
(HMWPs).

•

In the 52-week toxicity study there was a trend towards larger immune complexes at the LD
(compared to controls, MD, and HD).

Reviewer Comments: The assay for qualitative detection of ADA/concizumab ICs was considered
acceptable and batches of concizumab enriched in multimers tended to form ICs more efficiently
in cynomolgus monkeys.

Immunohistochemistry Study to Investigate Biodistribution of Concizumab-Related
Immune Complexes in Tissues Derived from Cynomolgus Monkeys Administered
Concizumab for 26 Weeks (Study 214391, non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

Granular deposits containing concizumab, monkey IgG, IgM, and/or complement component
C3 (C3) were observed in a subset of the affected choroid plexus blood vessels or associated
macrophages, respectively, in 2/4 (50%) and 3/4 (75%) concizumab-dosed cynomolgus

concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys with lung vascular/perivascular alterations.

the lung and choroid plexus of cynomolgus monkeys is consistent with a role of immune
complexes in the pathogenesis of these lesions. As only a subset of the affected vessels contained
granular deposits, it cannot be ruled out that other factors may have contributed to the histologic
alterations in the choroid plexus/lung.

Study Features and Methods
Results

Details
(Choroid Plexus)
Granular deposits were observed (IHC) in intima/media
of some affected blood vessels and/or intramural and/or
perivascular macrophage infiltrates, respectively, in 2/4
and 3/4 dosed cynomolgus monkeys with endothelial
cell hypertrophy and/or hyperplasia or vasculitis in
choroid plexus. The granular deposits were either
associated with the surface of the macrophages or had
been phagocytosed by the macrophages. Granular
deposits were not observed in controls or in two
concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys without
vascular alterations in choroid plexus.

observed in or near the affected portions of the
intima/media (typical sites for immune complex
deposition), whereas granular deposits were not
observed in unaffected blood vessels. Probable
granular deposit localization at vascular branch points
was identified in a LD (3 mg/kg/day) male (#8) and were
characteristic of immune complex deposition.
The vascular wall granular deposits were composed of
co-localized concizumab (human IgG), monkey IgG
and/or IgM (likely ADA) but did not contain C3. In
choroid plexus macrophages, the granular deposits
contained human IgG and IgM and/or C3. It’s unclear
whether the differences in granular deposit composition
between the vascular wall and the macrophages
reflected differential processing or a differential uptake
by macrophages.
(Lung)
Granular deposits containing concizumab, monkey IgG,
IgM, and/or C3 were observed in the intima/media of a
subset of affected blood vessels in 3/5 concizumabdosed cynomolgus monkeys with vascular/perivascular
inflammation or other alterations in lung blood vessels
but were not observed in controls without vascular
and/or perivascular inflammation.
The granular deposits were observed in intima/internal
elastic lamina and/or at vascular branch points of
relatively early lesions in lung in a LD male (#6) and in a
30 mg/kg IV dosed male (#17). Concizumab-, monkey
IgG-, and IgM-containing granular deposits were also
observed in at least one thrombus in the 30 mg/kg IV
dosed male (#17). In a 30 mg/kg (IV) dosed male (#18),
the granular deposits associated with the intimal
thickening were composed of IgM and C3, however the
presence of concizumab was not detected.

Analysis of Complement Activation and Circulating Immune Complexes in Samples
Derived from the 52-Week Cynomolgus Monkey Toxicity Study (Study 214471, non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

Circulating immune complexes (CIC) were present in all concizumab-dosed cynomolgus
monkeys at Week 26 (prior to dose administration) and were significantly elevated at the
HD. Following the administration of concizumab (Week 26), significant increases in CIC
levels were observed at 4 hours (post-dose) in all concizumab-dosed cynomolgus monkeys.

•

Classical complement pathway activity (50% Hemolytic Complement equivalent, CH50) was
not notably affected in blood sample derived from cynomolgus monkeys administered
concizumab for 26-/52-weeks (≤9 mg/kg/day).

•

The results of the complement fragment C3a assay were inconclusive at Week 26, and there
were no notable effects observed at Week 52.

Reviewer Comments: The assays for CIC, CH50 and C3a were considered acceptable and
indicated that concizumab elicited the formation of CICs but did not increase complement
activation in cynomolgus monkeys.
Table 91. Study Information, Study 214471, Non-GLP
Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
No
Objective
The purpose of the study was to investigate
complement activation in samples from the 52-week
cynomolgus monkey toxicity Study (213542) using three
different assays.
Methods
Samples were obtained during Week 26 and Week 52
when steady state plasma concentrations of
concizumab had been established. Circulating immune
complexes (CIC) were assessed using an enzyme
immunoassay (EIA) that measures circulating immune
complexes containing C3 fragments. Classical
complement pathway activity (50% Hemolytic
Complement equivalent (CH50)) was assessed using
an EIA that measures the total classical complement
pathway activity. Complement fragment C3a was
assessed in an EIA where C3a binds to microwell plates
coated with a monoclonal antibody against C3a.
Analyses were performed on samples collected at predose, 4-, 8- and 24-hours after dosing (SC).

Table 93. Study Information, Study 214400, Non-GLP
Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
No
Objective

The purpose of the IHC phase of the study was to
provide information regarding concizumab
biodistribution and the formation/deposition of immune
complexes (ICs) containing concizumab, monkey IgG,
IgM, and/or complement component C3. This report
presents the IHC findings in cynomolgus monkeys from
Study 213542 and contains the data from selected
cynomolgus monkeys dosed for 13-, 26-, and 52-weeks.

Methods

Hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) slides were prepared and
evaluated for histopathological alterations. Based on the
H&E evaluation, the lung, liver, and brain-choroid
plexus with vascular/inflammatory changes
(subendothelial intimal thickening/plasmacytic/histiocytic
infiltration) were selected for IHC and were stained for
the presence of human IgG (indicative of concizumab),
monkey immunoglobulins (IgG and IgM), and/or
complement component C3. Granular deposits of
human IgG, monkey IgG and IgM, and/or C3 were
considered evidence of immune complex deposition.

Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab
Results

(13-Week Interim Sacrifice)
Granular deposits containing concizumab, IgM, and/or
C3 were observed in a subset of affected blood vessels
but not in any unaffected blood vessels in the lungs of
1/3 (33%) of the dosed cynomolgus monkeys with lung
vascular and/or perivascular alterations. All three
(100%) cynomolgus monkeys had resolving or chronic
lesions (days to weeks old) characterized by intimal
thickening while a single HD female (4502) had active
(days old) lung lesions characterized by coagulative
necrosis, thrombosis, and neutrophilic/mononuclear cell
infiltration. In this female, granular deposits were
observed in the intima/media of the vascular wall and/or
at branch points of a subset of actively affected blood
vessels with ongoing inflammation (consistent with
immune complex-mediated vascular alterations). In this
same female, granular deposits were also observed in
neutrophils, monocytes/macrophages, and/or alveolar
macrophages associated with the vascular lesions and
likely represent phagocytosis and/or processing of
immune complexes.
Granular deposits composed of concizumab, IgG, and
C3 were also observed in the choroid plexus stroma in
1/2 (50%) of the concizumab-dosed cynomolgus
monkeys that presented with inflammatory cell
infiltration in the choroid plexus. No granular deposits
were observed in the 13-week interim controls.
(26-Week Interim Sacrifice)
Granular deposits were observed in the choroid plexus
of 2/4 (50%) of the cynomolgus monkeys that presented
with inflammatory alterations in the choroid plexus. The
alterations consisted of inflammatory cell infiltration
(including plasma cells) in 1 MD male (3002) where the
granular deposits were associated with the intima/media
of blood vessels. In a HD female (4506) lymphocytic
and/or plasmacytic infiltration and granular deposits
were located in the choroid plexus stroma.
Various stages of vascular/perivascular alterations
including thrombosis (occasionally with partial
resolution) and pleural inflammation were observed in
the lungs and/or liver in a single HD male (4006). No
clear granular deposits were observed in this male;
however, macrophage phagocytosis of concizumab,
monkey IgG, IgM, and C3-coated erythrocytes and
platelets were present in the lungs. This male also
presented with increases in intraluminal staining of
concizumab, monkey IgG, IgM, and C3 in resolving
and/or recanalizing thrombi in the liver and lungs. These
observations may indicate macrophage processing of
immune complexes in the lung and possible local
intravascular apposition and/or formation of immune
complexes in liver and lung. No granular deposits were
observed in the 26-week interim controls.

In Vitro Functional Characterization of Multimer and Monomer Forms of Concizumab
(Study CCOC130901, non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

A minimal (albeit significant) increase in the half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50)
was observed for the multimeric form of concizumab in a FXa generation assay (in the
presence or absence of 25μM phospholipids) and suggests that the TFPI neutralizing capacity
of the high molecular weight protein (HMWP) form of concizumab may be reduced relative
to the monomeric form.

•

In contrast, when FXa generation was assessed using endothelial cells that expressed surface
associated TF and TFPI, a lower half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50)(nonsignificant) was achieved with the multimeric form of concizumab relative to the monomeric
form.

•

In a thrombin generation assay (employing platelet-poor plasma with added phospholipids)
significantly higher procoagulant activity (thrombin peak height) was achieved with the
multimeric form of concizumab (P1) relative to the monomeric form (P4) and this effect was
enhanced (dose dependently) as the level of TF was reduced. Thrombin peak heights were

•

The procoagulant effects in whole blood were comparable between the multimeric form and

Table 95. Study Information, Study 215023, Non-GLP
Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
No
Objective
The report summarizes the results for the dose
formulation analysis from the 26 week (211087) and the
52 week (213542) toxicity studies conducted in
cynomolgus monkeys. The concizumab employed in
these two studies differed in the content of high
molecular weight protein (HMWP, i.e., increased dimers
and polymers) and the concizumab batch for Study
211087 (26-Weeks) was deliberately enriched with
HMWP polymers whereas the batch for Study 213542
(52-weeks) was of the same quality as the clinical study
material.
Methods
The percentages of monomers, dimers, and polymers in
the concizumab formulations used in the two toxicity
studies were measured after storage of concizumab at
or below -70ºC for the length of the study. A second
aliquot (213542) of all concentrations of the dose
formulation samples were stored at 5ºC for seven days
following sampling before being frozen and stored at or
below -70ºC until analysis. Size exclusion liquid
chromatography (SE-HPLC) was employed to
determine the content of monomers and HMWPs in the
monoclonal antibody samples.
Source: Reviewer constructed summary table of the toxicology Study (215023) submitted to the BLA
Abbreviations: GLP, good laboratory practices; HMWP, high molecular weight protein; SE-HPLC, size exclusion liquid
chromatography

Assessment of Binding and Internalization of Different Batches of Concizumab by
Endothelial Cells (Study CNGU131001, Non-GLP)
Key Study Findings
•

The binding of concizumab and concizumab multimer batches to endothelial cells was TFPIspecific and concentration-dependent, and the specific binding affinity was found to be
comparable (0.3 μg/mL) between the two batches.

•

Concizumab and concizumab multimer batches competed for each other’s binding to TFPI
equally, supporting further that the binding affinities were comparable between these
batches.

•

A comparable degree (minimal) of cellular internalization was observed between the batches
of concizumab (monomeric and multimeric) over time and was found to be specific and
saturable.

•

The amount of concizumab internalized (specific) by endothelial cells was reduced
significantly relative to the amount of cell surface binding (specific) with both the
monomeric batch (15-fold lower) and the multimeric batch (20-fold lower) of concizumab
and suggests that the cellular target (TFPI) is either internalized minimally/slowly or recycled
rapidly to the cell surface.

internalization by endothelial cells (in vitro), and suggests that aggregates of concizumab are

concizumab).

Study Features and Methods
Details
GLP compliance
No
Objective
The aim of this study was to evaluate and compare the
cellular binding and internalization of two different
batches of concizumab containing varying levels of high
molecular weight proteins (HMWPs) using vascular
endothelial cells.
Methods
One batch of concizumab contained a low level of

contained a high level of HMWP (93.6%) which was the
isolated multimer fraction purified from the batch used in
the 26-Week cynomolgus monkey toxicity Study

designed to assess the direct cellular binding and
internalization by an endothelial cell line (EA.hy926) and
primary human umbilical vein endothelial cells
(HUVEC). The specificity of the binding was determined
by an excess addition of unlabeled concizumab.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
All nonclinical studies submitted to the BLA were also submitted and reviewed under the IND.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
rFVIIa and activated prothrombin complex concentrates (aPCC) are used to treat bleeds in
hemophilia patients with inhibitors and may be used for treatment of breakthrough bleeds in
patients on concizumab prophylaxis. Likewise, FVIII and FIX are used to treat bleeds in patients
with hemophilia A or B without inhibitors. To evaluate the potential for pharmacodynamic
interaction between hemostatic agents and concizumab, the following in vitro studies were
conducted.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

14.1.1. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab
and rFVIIa, Study 319076
The effects of concizumab and rFVIIa were quantified in a thrombin generation assay with HA
plasma pool and plasma from patients on concizumab prophylaxis in Studies 4310 and 4255.
•

In the HA plasma pool (Figure 18, left), the effects of concizumab and rFVIIa were mainly
additive. A statistically significant synergistic effect (p<0.05) was observed for 25nM or
75nM rFVIIa and 1500 ng/mL concizumab. The additive effects accounted for around 85%
of the total observed effect for 1500 ng/mL concizumab combined with either 25nM (85.5%)
or 75nM (84.7%) rFVIIa (mean values for n = 3).

•

rFVIIa increased thrombin peak in plasma from HA patients with inhibitors (Study 4310) or
without inhibitors (Study 4255) on concizumab prophylaxis (Figure 18, right). The effects of
concizumab and rFVIIa were mainly additive accounting for 60±38% (25nM rFVIIa,
Study 4255, mean and standard deviation (SD) of data from 28 subjects), 67 ± 8% (75nM
rFVIIa, Study 4255, mean and SD of data from 24 subjects) and 78 ± 14% (75nM rFVIIa,
Study 4310, mean and SD of data from 15 subjects) of the total observed effects.

Overall, a significant synergistic effect between rFVIIa and concizumab was observed in plasma
accounting for up to 40% of the total observed effect.

14.1.2. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab
and aPCC, Study 319076
Drug interactions between concizumab and aPCC was evaluated in thrombin generation assay.
aPCC enhanced the thrombin peak both in the absence and presence of concizumab in a HA
plasma pool (Figure 19, left) and in plasma from HA patients with inhibitors (Study 4310) and
without inhibitors (Study 4255) on concizumab prophylaxis (Figure 19, right).
•

The effect of concizumab and aPCC were mainly additive accounting for between 83%
(150 ng/mL concizumab combined with 1 U/mL aPCC) and 67% (1500 ng/mL concizumab
combined with 0.5 U/mL aPCC) of the total observed effects in the plasma pool.

•

In the patient samples, the additive effects of concizumab and 1 U/mL aPCC corresponded to
80 ± 22% of the total observed effects (Study 4310, mean and SD of data from 15 subjects).
No significant synergistic effect was observed in Study 4255.

Overall, the data demonstrated that aPCC enhanced the thrombin generation in plasma
containing concizumab and that the effect of concizumab and aPCC were mainly additive with a
significant synergistic effect of up to 33% of the total effect.

14.1.4. Drug-Drug Interaction Between Concizumab
and rFIX, Study 321538
rFIX was added to a HB plasma pool alone or together with concizumab. rFIX enhanced the
thrombin peak in HB plasma both in the absence and presence of concizumab (Figure 21). Drug
interaction was observed for 1 IU/mL rFIX combined with concizumab at 1500 ng/mL,
4500 ng/mL and 15000 ng/mL and for 0.5 IU/mL rFIX combined with 100nM concizumab. The
additive effects of concizumab and 1 IU/mL rFIX corresponded to between 111% (1500 ng/mL
concizumab) and 113% (15000 ng/mL concizumab) of the total observed effects, and the
additive effects of 0.5 IU/mL rFIX and 15000 ng/mL concizumab corresponded to 112% of the
total observed effects.
Overall, the data suggest an additive effect when combining rFIX with concizumab.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Single Ascending Dose in Healthy and
Hemophilia Subjects, Study 3813
Study Design
This is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single-dose, dose-escalation study
investigating safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of concizumab administered
intravenously and subcutaneously to healthy male subjects and patients with hemophilia (HA or
HB). A total of 52 subjects (28 healthy subjects and 24 hemophilia subjects) were randomized
and completed the study. The subjects were randomized in a 3:1 manner to receive concizumab
or placebo. The studied doses are listed below:
•

Intravenous administration

– Healthy subjects (12): 0.5, 5, 50, 250 μg/kg
– Hemophilia patients (9 HA, 3 HB): 250, 1000, 3000, 9000 mg/kg
– Placebo (4 healthy subjects and 4 hemophilia patients)
•

Subcutaneous administration

– Healthy subjects (9): 50, 250, 1000 mg/kg
– Hemophilia patients (6 HA): 1000, 3000 mg/kg
– Placebo (3 healthy subjects and 2 hemophilia patients)
204
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Serial PK and PD samples were collected at pre-dose, 0, 5, 15 and 30 minutes, 1, 4, 8, 12, 24, 36,
and 48 hours post-dosing, at each study visit till the end of study (i.e., up to 43 days after
dosing). Validated ELISA and chromogenic assay were used for the PK and PD assessment,
respectively.
Results
Concizumab concentration-time profiles following IV and SC administration are presented in
Figure 22 with PK parameters summarized in Table 97 and Table 98.
•

Across dose levels, concizumab exposure (maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) and area
under the concentration-time curve) increased with increasing dose in a greater than dose
proportional manner following IV and SC administration. For example, there was a 23-fold
decrease in mean clearance with a 12-fold increase in concizumab doses (i.e., 250 to 3000
μg/kg). The non-linear PK was considered to be mainly due to target-mediated drug
disposition, which is a common characteristic of monoclonal antibodies.

•

PK profiles were similar between healthy subjects and hemophilia patients and between HA
and HB patients.

•

The median tmax across dose cohorts ranged from 0.5 to 2.9 days following SC
administration.

•

Following a single dose IV injection of 0.25 mg/kg, the half-life was about 26 to 31 hours.

Mean plasma profiles for free tissue factor pathway inhibitor (free TFPI, the PD marker)
concentrations following IV and SC administration of concizumab to healthy subjects and
patients with hemophilia are presented in Figure 23 and Figure 24.
•

The free TFPI levels decreased with increasing concizumab doses following both IV and SC
administration in both healthy subjects and hemophilia patients. Duration of free TFPI
suppression increased with increasing concizumab dose. Following a single IV
administration, the free TFPI level was completely suppressed for approximately 12 hours
and a week for 0.25 mg/kg and 3 mg/kg, respectively.

Concizumab was well-tolerated and there were no deaths or other serious adverse events
observed in this study.

Reviewer’s Comments:
•

The non-linear elimination of concizumab seems to be due to the target-mediated drug
disposition (TMDD) following binding of concizumab to endothelial cell-anchored TFPI
(the target) with subsequent endocytosis and degradation (target-mediated clearance). In
addition to the drug-target complex elimination pathway, concizumab is also eliminated
in a linear fashion, as other monoclonal antibodies, mainly by catabolism with a half-life
of approximately 3 weeks.

•

TFPI is present on endothelial cells (~92%), platelets (~1%) and in plasma (~7%) (Broze
and Girard 2012). Concizumab can readily access the TFPI on the surface of endothelial
cells (mTFPI) and the free soluble TFPI circulating in the plasma (sTFPI). While both
sTFPI and the TFPI in circulating platelets can migrate to the sites of vessel injury and
inhibit coagulation, sTFPI is used as an exploratory pharmacodynamics (PD) marker for
assessing hemophilia treatment due to its ease of sampling.

•

Nonlinear PK of concizumab was observed after IV and SC dosing in healthy volunteers
and patients, reflecting dose-dependent, target-mediated clearance at low doses. The
209

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

exact clearance mechanisms at each phase of elimination are not clear. The current
hypothesis from the Applicant is that when drug concentrations are high enough to
saturate targets, the rate of drug elimination is governed by linear nonspecific routes,
together with a fixed rate of target mediated elimination. When drug concentrations go
lower, the targets are not all saturated and both nonspecific and target-mediated
elimination routes are important, resulting in nonlinear PK. When drug concentrations
are even lower such that targets are not saturated, the target mediated elimination
becomes the main route of elimination and PK is linear. Evaluation of the concentrationtime profile provides an estimate of drug concentrations associated with each of these
phases. A change in the slope of the concentration-time curve during the elimination
phase, from shallow (target saturated; linear nonspecific routes govern elimination) to
steep (targets not all saturated; combination of linear and nonlinear routes govern
elimination), and then again from steep to shallow (targets not saturated; linear routes
govern elimination) will be apparent for profiles that have high enough concentrations to
fully saturate the target receptor and PK sampling sufficient to capture the phase when
concentrations fall well below the threshold.

14.2.2. Single Dose Study in Healthy Japanese
Subjects, Study 3981
Study Design
This was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, single-dose study to assess the PK of
SC administered concizumab in healthy male Japanese subjects. A total of 8 subjects received a
single SC dose of concizumab (250 μg/kg or 1000 μg/kg) or placebo. In each dose cohort,
3 subjects received active treatment and 1 subject received placebo. Blood sampling was done
prior to dosing and up to 33 days post-dosing for assessment of PK, PD, and laboratory safety
parameters.
Study Results
Mean concizumab concentration-time profiles are presented in Figure 25. Derived PK endpoints
are presented in Table 99.

14.2.4. Multiple Dose Study in Hemophilia Patients,
Study 4159
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple-dose, dose-escalation study in
18 patients with hemophilia A without inhibitors. Three dose levels of concizumab (0.25, 0.5,
0.8 mg/kg) were administered SC every fourth days for 12 times over a 42-day period. However,
to rapidly reach steady state at the targeted plasma concentration, the first two doses were given
on two consecutive days. All study products were administered to the study subjects at the clinic
under the surveillance of medically trained study site staff. At each dose level, eight patients
were randomized in a 3: 1 ratio to receive concizumab or placebo treatment. Blood samples for
PK and PD assessment were collected pre-dose at each day of dose administration, 4 hours after
administration on Days 18 and 42, and 48 hours after the last dose on Day 42.
Study Results
•

Non-linear PK of concizumab was observed in HA patients. Relative to the 0.25 mg/kg
group, geometric mean AUCτ (planned Day 38-42) values were 3-fold higher in the
0.50 mg/kg group and nearly 11-fold higher in the 0.80 mg/kg group (Figure 30 and Table
100).

•

There was a high between-subject variability following high doses of concizumab.

•

A clear PD response to concizumab dosing was observed as the concentration of free TFPI in
plasma was inversely correlated with that of concizumab. Mean trough levels of free plasma
TFPI was reduced by ~27%, 55% and 75% from baseline for the 0.25, 0.5 and 0.80 mg/kg
groups, respectively (Figure 31).

•

No ADAs (binding or neutralizing) were detected during this study.

Reviewer’s Comments: The study aimed to include five concizumab dose levels, but was stopped
after three due to a safety committee decision not to dose-escalate to the fourth concizumab dose
level of 1.1 mg/kg, i.e., this study was finalized following the completion of the 0.80 mg/kg group.
Blinded preliminary safety and PK/PD data from the 0.80 mg/kg group was reviewed by the
safety committee. Marked changes in coagulation parameters were observed, including a
decrease from baseline in fibrinogen, and a pronounced increase in D-dimer and F1 + 2 outside
of normal range. In addition, a substantial between-subject variation in pro-coagulant response
to concizumab was observed. Based on this, the safety committee decided not to proceed to the
1.1 mg/kg concizumab dose level.
•

In this study, concizumab was given at doses of 0.25 mg/kg, 0.5 mg/kg and 0.8 mg/kg on
Day 1, Day 2 and every 3 days afterward. As shown in Figure 30, slope of the PK curve
during the elimination phase was steeper for the 0.8 and 0.5 mg/kg doses than that for the
0.25 mg/kg dose. It is consistent with the observation that non-linear PK due to TMDD
plays a dominant role in the target concentration range, leading to a much faster drug
clearance at these concentrations. In addition, higher between-subject and within-subject
variability were observed at the higher doses of concizumab (Table 101). Given a
significant concentration-dependent clearance due to TMDD, once-daily dosing may
minimize within-patient PK variability by minimizing the peak-to-trough differences.

14.2.5. Efficacy and Safety Study in Adult and
Adolescent Patients With Hemophilia, Study
4311, After Restart
Study Design
This was a randomized, open-label study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of daily concizumab
prophylaxis (PPX) administered SC in patients with HA or HB with inhibitors. Initially, patients
218
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

were randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) or no PPX (arm 1) or assigned to non-randomized
PPX arms (arms 3 or 4) based on their treatment regimen before entering the study. After the
restart of the study, patients continued in the treatment arm which they were initially randomized
or assigned to. New eligible patients were randomized to concizumab PPX (arm 2) or no PPX
(arm 1) or assigned to arm 4. The study consisted of a 3-week screening period, a main part (2432 week treatment period), an extension part (128-136 week treatment period), and a 7-week
follow-up period (Figure 33).
Upon restart of the study, the dosing regimen was a loading dose of 1.0 mg/kg concizumab SC
on the first day of treatment, followed by an initial daily dose of 0.20 mg/kg concizumab. Within
the initial 5 to 8 week maintenance dose setting period on 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, the dose was
either increased to 0.25 mg/kg if the concizumab exposure was below 200 ng/mL, decreased to
0.15 mg/kg if the exposure was above 4000 ng/mL or maintained at 0.20 mg/kg if exposure was
between 200-4000 ng/mL. The dose setting was based on the concizumab exposure level after 4
weeks of concizumab exposure at visit 4a. The investigational drug product consisted of the drug
(b) (4)
constituent (concizumab 40 mg/mL and 100 mg/mL), and a device constituent (a
peninjector).
For patients on concizumab PPX (patients in arms 2, 3 and 4), two 24-hour PK/PD-sessions were
conducted at visits 2a and 9a. The concizumab dose was administered in the abdomen and blood
samples were drawn pre-dosing, 3, 6, 9 and 24 hours after dosing. In addition to the two PK/PD
sessions, pre-dose trough concizumab plasma concentration (Ctrough) was measured throughout
the concizumab treatment period.

Source: Study NN7415-4311 Clinical Study Report, Figure 9-1 (p.42)
Abbreviations: OnD, on demand; PPX, prophylaxis; V, Visit

Study Results
•

The concizumab plasma concentrations measured at Week 4 were used to determine the
maintenance dose for each patient following an initial dose of 0.20 mg/kg. One (1) patient
(1.0%) had a concizumab plasma concentration >4000 ng/mL prior to maintenance dose
setting and was decreased to a maintenance dose of 0.15 mg/kg. Twenty-four (24) patients
(24.7%) had concizumab plasma concentrations <200 ng/mL prior to maintenance dose

Concizumab

setting and were increased to a maintenance dose of 0.25 mg/kg. Seventy-two patients

14.3.2. Bioanalytical Method for PD Assessment
Validated PD assays were used to measure the levels of free TFPI (not bound to concizumab),
and thrombin generation in plasma.
Free TFPI
Free TFPI in plasma, i.e., TFPI not bound to concizumab, was quantified using a commercially
available ELISA kit (Asserachrom® Total TFPI, Diagnostica Stago, Asnières-sur-Seine, France).
The assay uses 2 monoclonal anti-TFPI antibodies where the catching antibody competes with
concizumab resulting in specific detection of TFPI not bound to concizumab. Validation
parameters for free TFPI are presented below in Table 107.
Table 107. Validation Parameters for Free TFPI
(b) (4)

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Source: Summary of biopharmaceutical studies and associated analytical methods Table 1-5
Abbreviations: LLOQ, lower limit of quantification; RT, room temperature; TFPI, tissue factor pathway inhibitor

Thrombin Generation
Thrombin generation is a process that determines the extent of the thrombin burst which is
crucial for the formation of a stable fibrin clot. The TG assay (TGA) was an exploratory assay
for a global measurement of the potential of platelet-poor plasma to support hemostasis. The
assay is validated as an exploratory biomarker with regards to concizumab interference and
precision.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
Development of anti-concizumab antibodies was investigated in all clinical studies (Phase 1
single dose studies 3813 and 3981, Phase 1 multiple dose studies 3986 and 4159, Phase 2
multiple dose studies 4310 and 4255, and Phase 3 multiple dose Study 4311). ADA samples
were collected with time-matched PK samples. The sampling intervals in each of the clinical
studies are shown in Table 109.

No anti-concizumab antibodies (ADAs were detected in Phase 1 studies. Of the 185 patients
treated with concizumab in the multiple-dose studies throughout the clinical development
program (Studies 4159, 4310, 4255 and 4311 up until PACO), 25.4% (47 patients) tested
positive for binding anti-concizumab antibodies at one or more visits after first concizumab dose.
Twelve patients (6.5%) tested positive for anti-concizumab antibodies with in vitro neutralizing
effects (Table 110).

Of the 47 patients testing positive for ADAs, binding anti-concizumab antibody titers were
generally low. Apart from 1 patient with medium level titers and 1 patient with high titers, for
96% of ADA-positive patients, titers ranged from 100 to 6400 (equals 1-64 without the MRD
factored).
•

Medium-level ADA titers: One ADA-positive patient (1/47; 2%) had a maximum ADA titer
at 25600. This patient had anti-concizumab antibody titers declined to 12800 after 24 weeks,
where they stayed constant until the data cut-off for this summary.

•

High-level ADA titers: One patient (subject ID
) first tested positive for ADAs
(titer 1600) about 12 weeks, peaked at 12800, and returned to titer 1600 at the Week 32. The
patient discontinued concizumab treatment in Week 33 due to surgery after an accident
leading to bone fracture in the hand. Following the surgery, the patient experienced
hemorrhagic shock after arterial puncture during blood sampling. The patient restarted
concizumab treatment at week 40. ADA titers had increased to medium levels (51200) at
Week 40 to high levels (204800) at Week 56 and 64, likely caused by a combination of a
boosted immune response after the traumatic events experienced by the patient and reinitiation of concizumab treatment.
(b) (6)

It should be noted ADA and neutralizing antibody assays were not considered fully validated by
OBP reviewers. See Complete Response (CR) letter for comments related to immunogenicity

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Summary of Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
The population PK (PPK) model was developed based on all Phase 1 and 2 studies, and the
Phase 3 study 4311 in patients with hemophilia A and B with inhibitors. The proposed
concizumab regimen (applied in Phase 3) includes a loading dose of 1 mg/kg once on Day 1, and
from Day 2 until individual maintenance dose setting, once daily dosing of 0.20 mg/kg. Four
weeks after initiation of treatment, concizumab plasma concentration is measured and based on
this exposure level, the maintenance dose is set:
•

<200 ng/mL: 0.25 mg/kg once daily

•

200 to 4000 ng/mL: 0.20 mg/kg once daily

•

>4000 ng/mL: 0.15 mg/kg once daily

The PPK analysis was conducted using 4145 evaluable concizumab concentrations in a total of
232 patients. The concizumab dose level ranges included from 0.0005 to 9 mg/kg administered
by IV and 0.05 to 3 mg/kg SC injection. The summary of studies used for PPK analysis are listed
in Table 111. Summary statistics of the continuous and categorical covariates that were evaluated
in the population PK analysis are shown in Table 112.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

The PK of concizumab was best characterized by a 2-compartment model with combined linear
clearance and target mediated drug disposition (TMDD), together with a transit compartment for
delayed absorption. Of note, the TMDD was estimated using a quasi-steady-state approximation,
which assumes an instant equilibrium between concizumab and TFPI. The final concizumab PK
model parameter estimates, and the corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CIs) are presented
in Table 113. Due to sparse PK profiles in the Phase 3, data from previous Phase 1 & 2 was used
for estimation of PK parameters, then PK parameters were fixed. The only parameter - the total
capacity TMDD parameter Rtot and covariate effects were then re-estimated using data from
Phase 3 Study 4311.
Table 113. Parameter Estimates of the Final Population PK Model

Source: Population PK Study Report Modeling Report – Phase 3a, Table 10-5.

covariate on V; BWRTOT, body weight covariate on RTOT; C4311RTOT, Trial 4311 covariate on RTOT; CI, confidence interval; CL,
clearance; CV, coefficient of variation; KA1, absorption rate; KCOM, elimination rate for complex at endothelium; KM, concentration
for 50% saturation of TMDD; KTR, transit rate constant; NA, not applicable; PK, pharmacokinetics; Q, inter compartmental
clearance; RSE, relative standard errors; RTOT, total capacity of TMDD; SD, standard deviation; TMDD, target mediated drug
disposition; V2, volume of distribution

The goodness-of-fit plots for the final concizumab PK model stratified by patient population are
presented in the Figure 43. The prediction-corrected visual predictive check (pcVPC) stratified
by study (Figure 44) illustrated the prediction percentiles and corresponding 95% CI of simulated
concentrations overlaid on the observed concizumab concentrations and the corresponding 5th
and 95th percentiles.

Simulation for Maintenance-Dose Setting
The proposed concizumab regimen (applied in Phase 3) includes a loading dose of 1 mg/kg once
on Day 1, and from Day 2 until individual maintenance dose setting, once daily dosing of
0.20 mg/kg. Four weeks after initiation of treatment, concizumab plasma concentration is
measured and based on this exposure level, the maintenance dose is set:
•

<200 ng/mL: 0.25 mg/kg once daily

•

200 to 4000 ng/mL: 0.20 mg/kg once daily

•

>4000 ng/mL: 0.15 mg/kg once daily

Simulations support that maintenance dose setting reduced the total, population-level variability
in concizumab exposure (Figure 48). Specifically, there was a marginal increase in exposure at
the low end and a decrease in exposure at the high end (Table 114).

Reviewer’s Comments: The Applicant’s PPK model appears adequate to describe the
concizumab serum concentration-time profiles in the observed range following the
administration of concizumab in patients with hemophilia A and B with inhibitors from Phase 3
Study 4311. The shrinkage value was 9.05% for IIV on Rtot, indicating there was no obvious bias
in the parameter estimates. Therefore, the PK model is acceptable for simulating post hoc
exposure metrics, e.g., Cavg and Cavg, trial of concizumab for exposure-response (E-R) analyses for
efficacy and safety measurements.
Covariate analysis identified two statistically significant covariates: body weight (27-130.7 kg),
and Study 4311 on Rtot. Allometric scaling was also applied to CL and volume terms (Vc, Vp)
using fixed exponents (0.75 and 1.00 respectively) to account for PK variability related to body
weight in the PPK model. In addition, the other covariates such as age (12 to 79 years), race
249
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

(White (62.9%), Asian (25%), Black or African American (6%), American Indian or Alaska
Native (1.3%), other (1.3%)), Ethnicity (Hispanic or Latino (6%), not Hispanic or Latino (91%))
were not identified as significant covariate for the concizumab exposure.
The significant impact of body weight on concizumab PK supported the body-weight-based
dosing regimen:
•

The concizumab exposure increases with increasing body weight.

•

Using a flat dose (e.g., 15 mg) will result high exposure for subjects with low body weight
and low exposure for subjects with high body weight.

However, the mean concizumab exposure still evidently increases with increasing body weight
(with a ~5 fold exposure increase for body weight increasing from 40 kg to 100 kg) despite the
per-kilogram dosing. This is likely caused by the nonlinear PK due to TMDD. If necessary, a
body weight banded dosing approach (either with flat or per kilogram dosing) could be
considered to further reduce exposure variability. Nonetheless, after 4 weeks of treatment,
patients with low or high exposure should adjust dose to 0.25 or 0.15 mg/kg, respectively,
resulting in more similar exposure across patients.
In addition, PPK simulations further supported maintenance dose setting with an increase in
exposure at the low end and a decrease in exposure at the high end, and almost all patients were
above the 200 ng/mL concentration level: concentrations at the plateau of effect predicted by
exposure-response (E-R) for annualized bleeding rate.
Additional simulation with steady-state exposure estimates for adolescents (12 to 17 years)
enrolled in Study 4311 were overlapping with exposure estimates in adults (>18 years) when
treated with 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, which indicated no significant effect of age (adolescents
(b) (4)
[12 to 17 years] versus adults [≥18 years]).

High inter- and intra-subject variability in plasma concizumab concentrations following high
doses of concizumab was observed in Phase 3 Study 4311. The high inter-subject variability and
wide therapeutic window (200 to 4000 ng/mL) in maintenance setting can have important
implications for therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM). Generally, TDM is generally used for
narrow therapeutic index drug. In cases where there is high inter-subject variability, it may be
difficult to establish a one-size-fits-all dosing regimen for concizumab. The wide therapeutic
window can also affect therapeutic drug monitoring, as it may be more challenging to determine
the optimal dosage when there is dose delay or dose interruption. The high inter-subject
variability and wide therapeutic window may underscore the importance of individualized
dosing and the use of therapeutic drug monitoring to optimize treatment outcomes. However,
given the large therapeutic window here, the TDM might be ok to reduce potential outliers, and
for patients with low or high exposure, the TDM can help to adjust individual dose according to
the maintenance dose setting, resulting in more similar exposure across patients.

250
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
[To complete this review, detailed instructions are available in the Integrated Review Template How-to Guide.]
Reference ID: 5162849

BLA 761315

Concizumab

Although Current PPK model had included nonlinear elimination of concizumab via target
mediated drug disposition (TMDD), we noticed several limitations in their PPK model:
•

The Current PPK model had some limitations on handling the variabilities: current
model tends to put almost all the variability into the residual variabilities with high
additive error on log data (SD) = 0.6. This can be problematic because it can make it
difficult to determine the true sources of variability. Additionally, this can limit the ability
of the model to make accurate predictions.

•

Additionally, current PPK model fixed the most model structure parameters values for
TMDD and PK: only Rtot with inter-subject variance is allowed to vary, which can limit
the flexibility of the model when analyzing complex PK data with large inter- and intrasubject variability that require more flexibility in the model.

Additional simulation with steady-state exposure estimates for adolescents (12 to 17 years)
enrolled in Study 4311 were overlapping with exposure estimates in adults (>18 years) when
treated with 0.20 mg/kg concizumab, which indicated no significant effect of age (adolescents
(b) (4)
[12 to 17 years] versus adults [≥18 years]).

In summary, PPK modeling and simulation results support no dose adjustment for proposed dose
regimen.

14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
The Applicant’s exposure-response (E-R) for efficacy analysis of concizumab was conducted
with Phase 3 data. The total number of subjects and samples in each exposure-response model
was:
•

Free TFPI: 110 subjects with 941 time-matched free TFPI and concizumab plasma
concentrations (327 of these samples were below the lower limit of quantification (BLOQ))

•

Thrombin peak: 108 subjects with 1048 time-matched thrombin peak and concizumab
concentrations

•

Bleeding rate model: 117 subjects with 440 bleeding episodes (including data from ondemand treatment

The demographics for the populations used for the three exposure-response models are presented
in Table 115.

14.5.2.1. Exposure-Response Analysis of Bleeding Episodes
(Repeated Time-to-Event Model)
An RTTE analysis using a hazard function was performed to analyze the occurrence of treated
bleeds.
The occurrence of bleeding events in hemophilia patients with inhibitors treated on-demand was
described by an exponential Gompertz distribution function. The effect of concizumab exposure
on the hazard function was evaluated by inclusion of an inhibitory sigmoidal Emax model,
parameterized with a constant baseline hazard (h0), maximum reduction in instantaneous
bleeding rate from baseline (Emax), plasma concentration to achieve 50% of maximum response
(EC50), and Hill coefficient (n).

Exposure-response analysis of bleeding episodes show that the concizumab dosing regimen
maintains plasma concentrations at the plateau of effect (Figure 51). The maximum drug effect
(Emax) was predicted to be a 93% reduction in annualized bleeding rate (ABR) (Table 116). The
exposure-response analysis of bleeding episodes provided an adequate description of the
occurrence of the first bleeding episode over time for subjects on concizumab and on-demand as
evident from Figure 52.

VPC in Figure 57). As concizumab concentrations increased, the proportion of free TFPI
samples below lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) increased; however, the model could
adequately capture the proportion of samples below LLOQ (Figure 58). Diagnostic plots (Figure
56) support that the model adequately captured observed data.

14.5.2.3. Exposure-Response for Thrombin Peak Model
A sigmoidal Emax model was used to describe the relationship between concizumab
concentration (C) and thrombin peak. The model was parameterized with a baseline (E0), and a
sigmoidal Emax model parameterized with maximum thrombin peak response (Emax), plasma
concentration to achieve 50% of maximum response (EC50), hill coefficient (n) and a hemophilia
B with inhibitors factor on E0 according to:
Equation 3. Sigmoidal Emax Model for Thrombin Peak

The exposure metric for the Thrombin peak exposure-response models was the observed
concentrations with time-matched biomarker concentrations. All parameter estimates were
estimated with acceptable precision, and all BSV parameter estimates had low shrinkage (Table
118). One significant covariate was identified during the SCM procedure (HBwI was a
significant covariate on baseline thrombin peak).
Exposure-response analysis of thrombin peak shows that the concizumab dosing regimen
maintains plasma concentrations at the plateau of effect (Figure 59).

observed thrombin peak appeared lower than predicted by the model (Figure 59). The model
diagnostic plots (Figure 60) support that the model adequately fit the observed data. However,
the observed data were well captured by the model when accounting for variability between
subjects (see Figure 61).

and thrombin peak appears acceptable with an adequate fit of the data as evident. The exposureresponse analyses showed that the concizumab dosing regimen maintains plasma concentrations
at the plateau of effect, in terms of reducing bleeding rate and free TFPI, and increasing peak
thrombin in the thrombin generation assay.
Overall, exposure-response analyses support the proposed dosing regimen of concizumab.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Descriptive Results for Bleeding Episodes
Related Endpoints
ABRs for the following bleeding episodes related endpoints were also presented as descriptive
statistics for each arm in Table 120:
•

Treated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes (the primary efficacy endpoint)

•

Treated spontaneous bleeding episodes (pre-specified supportive secondary efficacy endpoint
not in statistical testing hierarchy)

•

Treated spontaneous and traumatic joint bleeding episodes (pre-specified supportive
secondary efficacy endpoint not in statistical testing hierarchy)

•

Treated spontaneous and traumatic target joint bleeding episodes (pre-specified supportive
secondary efficacy endpoint not in statistical testing hierarchy)

•

All treated and untreated spontaneous and traumatic bleeding episodes (not a pre-specified
efficacy endpoint)

Note that concizumab PPX arm 1 corresponds to the patients randomized to no PPX (arm 1) that
started concizumab PPX before the primary analysis cut-off but after having completed the ondemand treatment in the main part.
